### Question
What is StatefulWidget in Flutter?

### Answer
**StatefulWidget** in Flutter is a widget that can **change over time** (i.e., it has mutable state). Unlike `StatelessWidget`, which is immutable and only builds once, a `StatefulWidget` can rebuild its UI when its internal state changes.

### Key Concepts

- A `StatefulWidget` itself is **immutable** (like all widgets in Flutter).
- The **mutable state** is stored in a separate `State` object.
- When the state changes (via `setState()`), Flutter rebuilds the widget.

### Basic Structure

Every `StatefulWidget` consists of **two classes**:

1. The `StatefulWidget` class (configuration)
2. The `State` class (holds mutable data and UI)

### Example: Counter App

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: CounterPage());
  }
}

class CounterPage extends StatefulWidget {
  const CounterPage({super.key});

  @override
  State<CounterPage> createState() => _CounterPageState();
}

class _CounterPageState extends State<CounterPage> {
  int _counter = 0; // Mutable state

  void _incrementCounter() {
    setState(() {
      _counter++; // Triggers UI rebuild
    });
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('StatefulWidget Example')),
      body: Center(
        child: Text(
          'Counter: $_counter',
          style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 24),
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: _incrementCounter,
        child: const Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Important Lifecycle Methods

| Method                    | When it's called                          | Common Use Case                     |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------|
| `initState()`            | Once when the widget is created           | Initialize data, start listeners    |
| `didChangeDependencies()`| After `initState()` and when dependencies change | Access `InheritedWidget` data     |
| `build()`                | Every time `setState()` is called         | Build the UI                        |
| `setState()`             | Manually called to update state           | Notify Flutter of state change      |
| `dispose()`              | When the widget is removed                | Clean up controllers, listeners     |

### When to Use `StatefulWidget`

Use it when:

- The UI needs to update based on user interaction
- Data changes over time (e.g., form inputs, animations, timers)
- You need to maintain local state

> **Note**: In modern Flutter, many developers

### Question
What is the difference between StatelessWidget and StatefulWidget?

### Answer
**StatelessWidget vs StatefulWidget** is one of the most fundamental concepts in Flutter.

### 1. Core Difference

| Feature                    | **StatelessWidget**                          | **StatefulWidget**                              |
|---------------------------|----------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| **Mutability**            | Immutable                                    | Mutable                                         |
| **Can change after build**| No                                           | Yes                                             |
| **Has internal state**    | No                                           | Yes (`State` object)                            |
| **Rebuilds when**         | Parent rebuilds                              | `setState()` is called or parent rebuilds       |
| **Performance**           | Slightly better (simpler)                    | Slightly heavier (manages state)                |
| **Use case**              | Static UI                                    | Dynamic / Interactive UI                        |

### 2. StatelessWidget

Used when the widget **does not need to change** after it's built.

```dart
class Greeting extends StatelessWidget {
  final String name;

  const Greeting({super.key, required this.name});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Text('Hello, $name!');
  }
}
```

### 3. StatefulWidget

Used when the widget needs to **change over time** (user interaction, data updates, animations, etc.).

It consists of two classes:
- `StatefulWidget` (immutable configuration)
- `State` (mutable state)

```dart
class Counter extends StatefulWidget {
  const Counter({super.key});

  @override
  State<Counter> createState() => _CounterState();
}

class _CounterState extends State<Counter> {
  int _count = 0;

  void _increment() {
    setState(() {
      _count++;
    });
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Column(
      mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
      children: [
        Text('Count: $_count', style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 24)),
        ElevatedButton(
          onPressed: _increment,
          child: const Text('Increment'),
        ),
      ],
    );
  }
}
```

### 4. Key Rules of Thumb

- **Start with `StatelessWidget`** — convert to `StatefulWidget` only when you need `setState()`.
- If a widget’s appearance depends only on its **constructor parameters**, use `StatelessWidget`.
- If it needs to manage **internal changing data**, use `StatefulWidget`.
- Prefer lifting state up or using state management solutions (`Provider`, `Riverpod`, `Bloc`, etc.) instead of having too many `StatefulWidget`s.

### 5. Quick Comparison Example

```dart
// Stateless - static
const Text('Flutter is awesome');

// Stateful - changes
class LikeButton extends StatefulWidget { ... }
```

**Summary**:  
`StatelessWidget` = **static** UI  
`StatefulWidget` =

### Question
How do I use setState in Flutter?

### Answer
**`setState`** is the most basic way to update the UI in Flutter when using a `StatefulWidget`. It tells Flutter to rebuild the widget with the new state.

### How `setState` Works

- It must be called inside the `State` class of a `StatefulWidget`.
- You pass a function to `setState()` where you update your state variables.
- After the function runs, Flutter rebuilds the widget.

### Basic Example (Counter)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: CounterPage());
  }
}

class CounterPage extends StatefulWidget {
  const CounterPage({super.key});

  @override
  State<CounterPage> createState() => _CounterPageState();
}

class _CounterPageState extends State<CounterPage> {
  int _count = 0;

  void _increment() {
    setState(() {
      _count++;           // Update state here
    });
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('setState Example')),
      body: Center(
        child: Text(
          'Count: $_count',
          style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 32),
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: _increment,
        child: const Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Rules & Best Practices

1. **Only put state updates inside `setState`**:
   ```dart
   setState(() {
     _count++;
     _isLoading = false;
     // Any other state changes
   });
   ```

2. **Avoid heavy work inside `setState`** — keep it lightweight:
   ```dart
   // Bad
   setState(() {
     final result = heavyComputation(); // This blocks the rebuild
   });

   // Good
   final result = heavyComputation();
   setState(() {
     _data = result;
   });
   ```

3. **Don’t call `setState` after the widget is disposed** (can cause errors):
   ```dart
   if (!mounted) return;
   setState(() {});
   ```

4. **Use `setState` only for simple/local state**. For complex apps, consider:
   - `Provider` / `Riverpod`
   - `Bloc` / `Cubit`
   - `ValueNotifier` + `ValueListenableBuilder`

### Common Pattern: Multiple State Updates

```dart
void _updateUser(String name, int age) {
  setState(() {
    _name = name;
    _age = age;
    _isUpdated = true;
  });
}
```

### Question
What is BuildContext in Flutter?

### Answer
**BuildContext** is a handle to the location of a **Widget** in the widget tree. It represents where a widget is mounted and gives you access to ancestor widgets (especially `InheritedWidget`s like `Theme`, `MediaQuery`, `Navigator`, etc.).

### Core Concept

Every widget in Flutter has access to a `BuildContext`, which is passed as a parameter to the `build()` method:

```dart
class MyWidget extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    // 'context' refers to the location of MyWidget in the tree
    return Text('Hello');
  }
}
```

The context is **above** the widget in the tree — meaning you can use it to find ancestors, but not the widget itself.

### Common Use Cases

**1. Accessing Inherited Widgets (Theme, MediaQuery, etc.)**

```dart
@override
Widget build(BuildContext context) {
  final theme = Theme.of(context);
  final mediaQuery = MediaQuery.of(context);
  final textScaler = MediaQuery.textScalerOf(context);

  return Text(
    'Hello',
    style: theme.textTheme.headlineMedium,
  );
}
```

**2. Navigation**

```dart
void goToNextScreen(BuildContext context) {
  Navigator.of(context).push(
    MaterialPageRoute(builder: (_) => NextScreen()),
  );
}
```

**3. Showing SnackBars / Dialogs**

```dart
ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
  SnackBar(content: Text('Action completed')),
);
```

### Important Gotchas

**1. Context Goes Stale in `initState` / `async` gaps**

You cannot use `context` in `initState()` directly (before the widget is inserted). Use `didChangeDependencies()` or `addPostFrameCallback`:

```dart
@override
void initState() {
  super.initState();
  WidgetsBinding.instance.addPostFrameCallback((_) {
    // Safe to use context here
    Navigator.of(context).push(...);
  });
}
```

**2. Using context after `async` operations**

If you `await` something, the widget might be disposed. Always check `mounted`:

```dart
Future<void> fetchData() async {
  await someAsyncOperation();
  
  if (!mounted) return; // Important!
  
  Navigator.of(context).pop();
}
```

### Key Takeaway

`BuildContext` is not just a parameter — it's your gateway to the widget tree. Most of Flutter's powerful features (`Theme`, `MediaQuery`, `Navigator`, `Provider`, etc.) work through the context.

### Question
What is the widget lifecycle in Flutter?

### Answer
**Flutter Widget Lifecycle**

Flutter widgets are **immutable**. The lifecycle you're referring to is primarily the **`StatefulWidget`** lifecycle (via its `State` object). `StatelessWidget` has a very simple lifecycle.

### 1. StatelessWidget Lifecycle

Very minimal:

- `build()` is called when the widget is inserted into the tree.
- If the parent rebuilds and passes new data, a **new** `StatelessWidget` instance is created (old one is discarded).

### 2. StatefulWidget Lifecycle (Most Important)

Here's the full lifecycle order:

| Phase                        | Method                        | Called When                                      | Can Call `setState()`? | Frequency      |
|-----------------------------|-------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------|------------------------|----------------|
| Creation                    | `createState()`               | Widget is inserted into the tree                 | No                     | Once           |
| Initialization              | `initState()`                 | State object is created                          | No                     | Once           |
| Dependency Resolution       | `didChangeDependencies()`     | After `initState()` + when `InheritedWidget` changes | Yes                    | Multiple       |
| Building                    | `build()`                     | Widget needs to render                           | Yes                    | Many times     |
| Update                      | `didUpdateWidget()`           | Widget is rebuilt with new configuration         | Yes                    | Multiple       |
| Deactivation                | `deactivate()`                | Widget is removed from tree (temporarily)        | No                     | Multiple       |
| Disposal                    | `dispose()`                   | Widget is permanently removed                    | No                     | Once           |

### Lifecycle Diagram (Code Example)

```dart
class MyWidget extends StatefulWidget {
  final String title;

  const MyWidget({super.key, required this.title});

  @override
  State<MyWidget> createState() => _MyWidgetState();
}

class _MyWidgetState extends State<MyWidget> {
  int _counter = 0;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    print('1. initState() - Widget created');
    // Good for: initial setup, controllers, listeners
  }

  @override
  void didChangeDependencies() {
    super.didChangeDependencies();
    print('2. didChangeDependencies() - Dependencies changed');
  }

  @override
  void didUpdateWidget(MyWidget oldWidget) {
    super.didUpdateWidget(oldWidget);
    print('3. didUpdateWidget() - Widget config changed');
    if (widget.title != oldWidget.title) {
      // React to prop changes
    }
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    print('4. build() - UI rendered');
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text(widget.title)),
      body: Center(
        child: Text('Counter: $_counter'),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () => set

### Question
How do I create a custom widget in Flutter?

### Answer
To create a **custom widget** in Flutter, you typically extend either `StatelessWidget` or `StatefulWidget`. Custom widgets are built by composing existing Flutter widgets.

### 1. StatelessWidget (Recommended for most cases)

Use this when the widget doesn’t need to manage internal state.

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class CustomButton extends StatelessWidget {
  final String label;
  final VoidCallback onPressed;
  final Color color;

  const CustomButton({
    super.key,
    required this.label,
    required this.onPressed,
    this.color = Colors.blue,
  });

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return ElevatedButton(
      style: ElevatedButton.styleFrom(
        backgroundColor: color,
        padding: const EdgeInsets.symmetric(horizontal: 24, vertical: 12),
        shape: RoundedRectangleBorder(
          borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(8),
        ),
      ),
      onPressed: onPressed,
      child: Text(
        label,
        style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 16, color: Colors.white),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

**Usage:**

```dart
CustomButton(
  label: "Submit",
  onPressed: () => print("Button pressed"),
  color: Colors.green,
)
```

### 2. StatefulWidget

Use this when the widget needs to manage its own state.

```dart
class CounterWidget extends StatefulWidget {
  final int initialCount;

  const CounterWidget({super.key, this.initialCount = 0});

  @override
  State<CounterWidget> createState() => _CounterWidgetState();
}

class _CounterWidgetState extends State<CounterWidget> {
  late int _count;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _count = widget.initialCount;
  }

  void _increment() {
    setState(() {
      _count++;
    });
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Column(
      mainAxisSize: MainAxisSize.min,
      children: [
        Text(
          'Count: $_count',
          style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 24),
        ),
        const SizedBox(height: 8),
        ElevatedButton(
          onPressed: _increment,
          child: const Text('Increment'),
        ),
      ],
    );
  }
}
```

**Usage:**

```dart
const CounterWidget(initialCount: 5)
```

### Best Practices

- Always add a `const` constructor when possible.
- Use `super.key`.
- Name your widgets in **PascalCase** (e.g., `ProfileCard`).
- Extract repeated UI into custom widgets for better readability and reusability.
- Prefer **composition** over deep inheritance.

### Bonus: Using `StatelessWidget` with `HookWidget` (if using `flutter_hooks`)

```dart
class CustomTextField extends HookWidget {


### Question
What is InheritedWidget in Flutter?

### Answer
**InheritedWidget** is a special type of widget in Flutter designed to efficiently propagate data down the widget tree. It allows descendants to access shared data without manually passing it through constructors at every level.

### Why Use InheritedWidget?

- Avoid "prop drilling" (passing data through many widgets).
- Provide data (like themes, user info, app state) to any widget in the subtree.
- Only rebuild widgets that actually depend on the data (efficient).

Many core Flutter APIs are built on `InheritedWidget`:
- `Theme`
- `MediaQuery`
- `DefaultTextStyle`
- `Navigator`

### How It Works

`InheritedWidget` uses Flutter’s `BuildContext` to establish a dependency. When a descendant calls:

```dart
context.dependOnInheritedWidgetOfExactType<MyInheritedWidget>()
```

it registers itself as a dependent. If the `InheritedWidget` later changes and `updateShouldNotify` returns `true`, only the dependent widgets are rebuilt.

### Basic Example

Here's a complete, working example of an `InheritedWidget` that shares a counter value:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: CounterPage(),
    );
  }
}

// 1. The InheritedWidget
class CounterInherited extends InheritedWidget {
  final int counter;
  final VoidCallback increment;

  const CounterInherited({
    super.key,
    required this.counter,
    required this.increment,
    required super.child,
  });

  static CounterInherited of(BuildContext context) {
    final result = context.dependOnInheritedWidgetOfExactType<CounterInherited>();
    assert(result != null, 'No CounterInherited found in context');
    return result!;
  }

  @override
  bool updateShouldNotify(CounterInherited oldWidget) {
    return oldWidget.counter != counter;
  }
}

// 2. Widget that holds the state
class CounterPage extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<CounterPage> createState() => _CounterPageState();
}

class _CounterPageState extends State<CounterPage> {
  int _counter = 0;

  void _increment() {
    setState(() => _counter++);
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return CounterInherited(
      counter: _counter,
      increment: _increment,
      child: const CounterView(),
    );
  }
}

// 3. Descendant widgets can access the data
class CounterView extends StatelessWidget {
  const CounterView({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final inherited = CounterInherited.of(context);

    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('InheritedWidget Demo')),
      body: Center(
        child: Text(
          'Counter:

### Question
What is the difference between Container and SizedBox?

### Answer
The main difference between `Container` and `SizedBox` in Flutter is their **purpose and complexity**.

### Quick Comparison

| Feature                    | `SizedBox`                          | `Container`                              | Winner                  |
|---------------------------|-------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|-------------------------|
| **Primary purpose**       | Constraining size                   | Layout + decoration + styling            | -                       |
| **Performance**           | Lightweight                         | Heavier                                  | `SizedBox`              |
| **Width/Height**          | Yes                                 | Yes                                      | `SizedBox` (simpler)    |
| **Padding / Margin**      | No                                  | Yes                                      | `Container`             |
| **Decoration** (color, border, shadow) | No                            | Yes                                      | `Container`             |
| **Alignment**             | No                                  | Yes                                      | `Container`             |
| **Transform / Clip**      | No                                  | Yes                                      | `Container`             |

### Detailed Explanation

### 1. `SizedBox`
A simple widget that **only** imposes size constraints. It’s very efficient because it directly uses a `RenderConstrainedBox`.

**Best used for:**
- Setting explicit width/height
- Creating spacing (empty space)

```dart
// Creating space
const SizedBox(height: 20);

// Giving a widget a fixed size
SizedBox(
  width: 100,
  height: 50,
  child: ElevatedButton(
    onPressed: () {},
    child: const Text('Click'),
  ),
)
```

### 2. `Container`
A much more powerful widget that can do **many things** (padding, decoration, alignment, etc.). Because of this, it has more overhead.

**Best used when** you need styling or layout features beyond just size.

```dart
Container(
  width: 100,
  height: 50,
  padding: const EdgeInsets.all(8),
  decoration: BoxDecoration(
    color: Colors.blue,
    borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(8),
  ),
  child: const Text('Hello'),
)
```

### Key Behavioral Differences

- **Performance**: Use `SizedBox` when you only need to set `width` or `height`. `Container` is overkill in that case.
- **Constraints**: `SizedBox` is more strict about the size. `Container` can behave differently depending on whether it has a child or not.
- **Empty Space**: An empty `SizedBox(width: 100, height: 100)` is more idiomatic than an empty `Container`.

### Recommendation

| Use Case                              | Recommended Widget |
|---------------------------------------|--------------------|
| Just need width/height                | `SizedBox`         |
| Need background, border, padding      | `Container`        |
| Creating spacing between widgets      | `SizedBox`         |
| Need both size + decoration           | `Container`        |

**Rule of

### Question
What is the difference between Row and Column in Flutter?

### Answer
**Row** and **Column** are both layout widgets in Flutter that arrange their children linearly. The main difference is the **direction** in which they lay out their children.

### Key Differences

| Feature                  | `Row`                              | `Column`                            |
|--------------------------|------------------------------------|-------------------------------------|
| **Direction**            | Horizontal (left to right)         | Vertical (top to bottom)            |
| **Main Axis**            | Horizontal                         | Vertical                            |
| **Cross Axis**           | Vertical                           | Horizontal                          |
| **Default `mainAxisSize`** | `MainAxisSize.max`                | `MainAxisSize.max`                  |
| **Use Case**             | Placing widgets side by side       | Placing widgets one below another   |

Both widgets are built on top of `Flex`, so they share the same alignment and sizing properties (`mainAxisAlignment`, `crossAxisAlignment`, `mainAxisSize`, etc.).

### Code Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class RowVsColumnExample extends StatelessWidget {
  const RowVsColumnExample({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Row vs Column')),
      body: Padding(
        padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16.0),
        child: Column(
          children: [
            // ========== ROW ==========
            const Text('Row (Horizontal)', style: TextStyle(fontSize: 18, fontWeight: FontWeight.bold)),
            const SizedBox(height: 8),
            Row(
              mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.spaceEvenly,
              children: [
                Container(width: 60, height: 60, color: Colors.blue),
                Container(width: 60, height: 60, color: Colors.green),
                Container(width: 60, height: 60, color: Colors.orange),
              ],
            ),

            const SizedBox(height: 40),

            // ========== COLUMN ==========
            const Text('Column (Vertical)', style: TextStyle(fontSize: 18, fontWeight: FontWeight.bold)),
            const SizedBox(height: 8),
            Column(
              mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
              children: [
                Container(width: 60, height: 60, color: Colors.red),
                const SizedBox(height: 8),
                Container(width: 60, height: 60, color: Colors.purple),
                const SizedBox(height: 8),
                Container(width: 60, height: 60, color: Colors.teal),
              ],
            ),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Gotchas & Best Practices

- **Overflow issues**: Use `Expanded` or `Flexible` when children might exceed available space.
- **Alignment**:
  - `mainAxisAlignment` controls spacing along the direction of the widget.
  -

### Question
What is the difference between Expanded and Flexible in Flutter?

### Answer
**Expanded** and **Flexible** are both widgets used inside `Row`, `Column`, or `Flex` to control how their children occupy available space. The main difference lies in their **default behavior** regarding how much space they force their child to take.

### Core Difference

| Widget     | Default `fit`     | Behavior                                                                 | Use Case |
|------------|-------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------|
| **Flexible** | `FlexFit.loose`   | Child can be **smaller** than the available space                        | When you want optional flexibility |
| **Expanded** | `FlexFit.tight`   | Child is **forced** to fill all available space                          | When you want the widget to take remaining space |

- `Expanded` is actually just a **convenience widget** for `Flexible` with `fit: FlexFit.tight`.
- `Flexible` gives you more control because you can choose between `loose` and `tight`.

### Visual Comparison

```dart
Row(
  children: [
    Container(width: 50, color: Colors.red),
    
    // Flexible (loose by default)
    Flexible(
      child: Container(height: 50, color: Colors.blue),
    ),
    
    Container(width: 50, color: Colors.green),
  ],
)
```

**Result**: The blue container will only take the space its child needs (it won't stretch to fill).

```dart
Row(
  children: [
    Container(width: 50, color: Colors.red),
    
    // Expanded (always tight)
    Expanded(
      child: Container(height: 50, color: Colors.blue),
    ),
    
    Container(width: 50, color: Colors.green),
  ],
)
```

**Result**: The blue container will stretch to fill all remaining space.

### Code Example with Both

```dart
Column(
  children: [
    // Takes only needed space (can shrink)
    Flexible(
      flex: 2,
      fit: FlexFit.loose, // optional, since it's default
      child: Container(color: Colors.orange, height: 60),
    ),
    
    // Takes all remaining space
    Expanded(
      flex: 3,
      child: Container(color: Colors.purple),
    ),
    
    // Fixed height
    Container(height: 40, color: Colors.teal),
  ],
)
```

### Quick Summary

| Scenario                              | Use          |
|---------------------------------------|--------------|
| Widget should **fill** remaining space | `Expanded`   |
| Widget **can** take remaining space but doesn't have to | `Flexible` |
| Need to control `fit` behavior        | `Flexible`   |
| Most common case (fill space)         | `Expanded`   |

**Rule of thumb**: Use `Expanded` in 90% of cases. Use `Flexible` when you explicitly don't want the widget to stretch.

### Question
How do I use Stack widget in Flutter?

### Answer
**Stack** is a layout widget in Flutter that allows you to **overlap** widgets on top of each other. Children are painted in order (first child is at the bottom).

### Basic Syntax

```dart
Stack(
  alignment: Alignment.center, // default alignment for non-positioned children
  children: [
    // Bottom layer
    Container(color: Colors.blue, width: 200, height: 200),
    
    // Top layer
    Container(color: Colors.red, width: 100, height: 100),
  ],
)
```

### Complete Working Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Stack Example')),
        body: Center(
          child: Stack(
            alignment: Alignment.center,
            children: [
              // Background
              Container(
                width: 300,
                height: 300,
                color: Colors.blue.shade100,
              ),
              
              // Middle layer
              Container(
                width: 200,
                height: 200,
                color: Colors.blue.shade300,
              ),
              
              // Top layer with Positioned
              Positioned(
                top: 20,
                right: 20,
                child: Container(
                  width: 80,
                  height: 80,
                  color: Colors.red,
                  child: const Center(
                    child: Text('Top', style: TextStyle(color: Colors.white)),
                  ),
                ),
              ),
            ],
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Using `Positioned` (Most Common Pattern)

`Positioned` gives you precise control over child placement:

```dart
Stack(
  children: [
    Image.network('https://picsum.photos/300/300'),
    
    Positioned(
      top: 10,
      left: 10,
      child: Container(
        padding: const EdgeInsets.all(8),
        color: Colors.black54,
        child: const Text('Overlay Text', style: TextStyle(color: Colors.white)),
      ),
    ),
    
    // Badge in top-right corner
    const Positioned(
      top: 10,
      right: 10,
      child: CircleAvatar(
        radius: 16,
        backgroundColor: Colors.red,
        child: Text('3'),
      ),
    ),
  ],
)
```

### Key Properties

| Property         | Description                                      | Default          |
|------------------|--------------------------------------------------|------------------|
| `alignment`      | Aligns non-Positioned children                   | `Alignment.topLeft` |
| `clipBehavior`   | Whether to clip children that overflow           | `Clip.none`      |
| `fit`            | How non

### Question
How do I use ListView in Flutter?

### Answer
**ListView** is Flutter's most commonly used scrollable widget for displaying lists of items.

### 1. Basic ListView (Static Children)

Use this when you have a small, fixed number of items:

```dart
ListView(
  padding: const EdgeInsets.all(8),
  children: const [
    ListTile(
      leading: Icon(Icons.map),
      title: Text('Map'),
    ),
    ListTile(
      leading: Icon(Icons.photo),
      title: Text('Album'),
    ),
    ListTile(
      leading: Icon(Icons.phone),
      title: Text('Phone'),
    ),
  ],
)
```

### 2. ListView.builder (Recommended for Most Cases)

Use this for dynamic or large lists. It only builds items that are visible (much more efficient).

```dart
ListView.builder(
  itemCount: items.length,
  itemBuilder: (context, index) {
    return ListTile(
      title: Text(items[index]),
      onTap: () {
        // Handle tap
      },
    );
  },
)
```

### 3. ListView.separated (With Dividers)

Adds separators between items automatically:

```dart
ListView.separated(
  itemCount: items.length,
  separatorBuilder: (context, index) => const Divider(),
  itemBuilder: (context, index) {
    return ListTile(title: Text(items[index]));
  },
)
```

### 4. Horizontal ListView

```dart
SizedBox(
  height: 120,
  child: ListView.builder(
    scrollDirection: Axis.horizontal,
    itemCount: items.length,
    itemBuilder: (context, index) {
      return Container(
        width: 100,
        margin: const EdgeInsets.all(8),
        color: Colors.blue,
        child: Center(child: Text(items[index])),
      );
    },
  ),
)
```

### Important Properties

| Property       | Description                              | Common Use                     |
|----------------|------------------------------------------|--------------------------------|
| `shrinkWrap`   | Wraps the list to its children size      | Use inside `Column`/`Row`      |
| `physics`      | Controls scroll behavior                 | `NeverScrollableScrollPhysics()` |
| `padding`      | Space around the list                    | `EdgeInsets.all(16)`           |
| `primary`      | Whether this is the primary scroll view  | Usually `true`                 |

### Best Practices

1. **Always prefer `ListView.builder`** over `ListView` with many children.
2. When using `ListView` inside a `Column`, use `shrinkWrap: true` + `physics: NeverScrollableScrollPhysics()`.
3. For very long lists with complex items, consider `ListView.custom` with a `SliverChildBuilderDelegate`.

```dart
class MyListScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  final List<String> items = List.generate(100, (i

### Question
How do I use GridView in Flutter?

### Answer
**GridView** in Flutter displays widgets in a 2D scrollable grid. The two most commonly used constructors are:

- `GridView.count` — Simple, fixed number of columns
- `GridView.builder` — Better performance with lazy loading (recommended for large lists)

### 1. Basic Example with `GridView.count`

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('GridView.count')),
        body: GridView.count(
          crossAxisCount: 2,           // Number of columns
          crossAxisSpacing: 10,
          mainAxisSpacing: 10,
          padding: const EdgeInsets.all(10),
          childAspectRatio: 1.0,       // Width / Height ratio
          children: List.generate(20, (index) {
            return Card(
              color: Colors.primaries[index % Colors.primaries.length],
              child: Center(
                child: Text(
                  'Item $index',
                  style: const TextStyle(color: Colors.white, fontSize: 18),
                ),
              ),
            );
          }),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Recommended: `GridView.builder` (Lazy Loading)

Use this when you have many items or the list is dynamic.

```dart
GridView.builder(
  padding: const EdgeInsets.all(10),
  gridDelegate: const SliverGridDelegateWithFixedCrossAxisCount(
    crossAxisCount: 3,
    crossAxisSpacing: 8,
    mainAxisSpacing: 8,
    childAspectRatio: 0.8,
  ),
  itemCount: 100,
  itemBuilder: (context, index) {
    return Container(
      decoration: BoxDecoration(
        color: Colors.blue.shade100,
        borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(12),
      ),
      child: Center(child: Text('Item $index')),
    );
  },
)
```

### Common Grid Delegates

| Delegate | Use Case | Example |
|---------|---------|--------|
| `SliverGridDelegateWithFixedCrossAxisCount` | Fixed number of columns | Most common |
| `SliverGridDelegateWithMaxCrossAxisExtent` | Fixed maximum width per item | Responsive grids |

### Example with `maxCrossAxisExtent`

```dart
GridView.builder(
  gridDelegate: const SliverGridDelegateWithMaxCrossAxisExtent(
    maxCrossAxisExtent: 150, // Each item max 150px wide
    crossAxisSpacing: 8,
    mainAxisSpacing: 8,
  ),
  itemBuilder: (context, index) => ...,
)
```

- Use `

### Question
What is MediaQuery in Flutter?

### Answer
**MediaQuery** is an `InheritedWidget` in Flutter that provides information about the device's screen and media environment (size, orientation, pixel density, safe areas, keyboard insets, etc.). It allows you to build responsive UIs that adapt to different devices and conditions.

### How to Access MediaQuery

You access it using `MediaQuery.of(context)`, which returns a `MediaQueryData` object.

```dart
final mediaQuery = MediaQuery.of(context);

// Common properties
double screenWidth = mediaQuery.size.width;
double screenHeight = mediaQuery.size.height;
Orientation orientation = mediaQuery.orientation;
double devicePixelRatio = mediaQuery.devicePixelRatio;
double textScaleFactor = mediaQuery.textScaleFactor;
EdgeInsets padding = mediaQuery.padding;           // Status bar, notch, etc.
EdgeInsets viewInsets = mediaQuery.viewInsets;     // Keyboard, etc.
```

### Practical Examples

#### 1. Basic Responsive Layout

```dart
class ResponsiveWidget extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final mediaQuery = MediaQuery.of(context);
    final screenWidth = mediaQuery.size.width;
    final isTablet = screenWidth > 600;

    return Scaffold(
      body: Center(
        child: Text(
          isTablet ? 'Tablet View' : 'Mobile View',
          style: TextStyle(fontSize: isTablet ? 32 : 24),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

#### 2. Handling Safe Area + Keyboard (Common Pattern)

```dart
class MyScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final mediaQuery = MediaQuery.of(context);
    final bottomPadding = mediaQuery.viewInsets.bottom; // Keyboard height

    return Scaffold(
      body: Padding(
        padding: EdgeInsets.only(bottom: bottomPadding),
        child: Column(
          children: [
            // Your content
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

#### 3. Using `MediaQuery` with `LayoutBuilder` (Recommended Approach)

`MediaQuery` causes the widget to rebuild whenever the media changes. For better performance with sizing, prefer `LayoutBuilder` when you only need constraints:

```dart
LayoutBuilder(
  builder: (context, constraints) {
    // constraints.maxWidth is usually preferred over MediaQuery
    if (constraints.maxWidth > 600) {
      return TabletLayout();
    } else {
      return MobileLayout();
    }
  },
)
```

### When to Use MediaQuery

| Use Case                        | Recommended Approach      | Reason |
|--------------------------------|---------------------------|--------|
| Screen width/height             | `MediaQuery.of(context).size` | Direct access |
| Orientation changes             | `MediaQuery.of(context).orientation` | Simple |
| Safe area / notch / status bar  | `MediaQuery.of(context).padding` | Handles device insets |
| Keyboard visibility             | `MediaQuery

### Question
How do I use GestureDetector in Flutter?

### Answer
**GestureDetector** is a Flutter widget that detects gestures (tap, double-tap, long-press, drag, scale, etc.) on its child widget.

### Basic Usage

Wrap any widget with `GestureDetector` and provide callback functions:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class GestureExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const GestureExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<GestureExample> createState() => _GestureExampleState();
}

class _GestureExampleState extends State<GestureExample> {
  Color _color = Colors.blue;
  String _message = 'Tap the box';

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('GestureDetector Example')),
      body: Center(
        child: GestureDetector(
          onTap: () {
            setState(() {
              _color = Colors.green;
              _message = 'Tapped!';
            });
          },
          onDoubleTap: () {
            setState(() {
              _color = Colors.orange;
              _message = 'Double Tapped!';
            });
          },
          onLongPress: () {
            setState(() {
              _color = Colors.red;
              _message = 'Long Pressed!';
            });
          },
          child: Container(
            width: 200,
            height: 200,
            color: _color,
            alignment: Alignment.center,
            child: Text(
              _message,
              style: const TextStyle(color: Colors.white, fontSize: 18),
            ),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Gesture Callbacks

| Callback            | Trigger                     | Use Case                     |
|---------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------------|
| `onTap`             | Single tap                  | Buttons, selection           |
| `onDoubleTap`       | Double tap                  | Zoom, edit mode              |
| `onLongPress`       | Long press                  | Context menu, drag start     |
| `onPanUpdate`       | Dragging                    | Custom dragging              |
| `onScaleStart/Update/End` | Pinch/zoom            | Image zoom, scaling          |
| `onTapDown` / `onTapUp` | Tap start/end            | More precise tap handling    |

### Drag Example (Pan)

```dart
GestureDetector(
  onPanUpdate: (details) {
    // Move widget based on drag
    print('Dragging: ${details.delta}');
  },
  onPanEnd: (details) {
    print('Drag ended with velocity: ${details.velocity}');
  },
  child: Container(
    width: 100,
    height: 100,
    color: Colors.purple,
  ),
)
```

### Best Practices

- Use `GestureDetector` when you need **custom** gesture behavior.
- For standard buttons, prefer `InkWell` (has ripple effect) or `ElevatedButton`.
- You

### Question
What is the difference between GestureDetector and InkWell?

### Answer
`GestureDetector` and `InkWell` are both used to detect taps, but they serve different purposes.

## Key Differences

| Feature | `GestureDetector` | `InkWell` |
|---------|-------------------|-----------|
| **Visual feedback** | None by default | Material ripple effect |
| **Gesture support** | Broad (drag, scale, pan, etc.) | Limited (tap, double-tap, long-press) |
| **Material requirement** | No | Yes (needs `Material` ancestor for ripple) |
| **Use case** | Custom gestures, non-Material widgets | Material Design buttons/lists |

## Code Examples

**InkWell** (with ripple effect):
```dart
Material(
  color: Colors.grey[200],
  child: InkWell(
    onTap: () => print('Tapped'),
    splashColor: Colors.blue.withOpacity(0.3),
    borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(8),
    child: Padding(
      padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16),
      child: Text('Tap me'),
    ),
  ),
)
```

**GestureDetector** (no visual feedback, more gestures):
```dart
GestureDetector(
  onTap: () => print('Tapped'),
  onDoubleTap: () => print('Double tapped'),
  onLongPress: () => print('Long pressed'),
  onPanUpdate: (details) => print('Dragging: ${details.delta}'),
  child: Container(
    padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16),
    color: Colors.grey[200],
    child: Text('Tap me'),
  ),
)
```

## When to Use Which

- **Use `InkWell`** when you want Material Design ripple feedback on taps (lists, cards, buttons).
- **Use `GestureDetector`** when you need complex gestures (swipes, scales) or don't want/need the ripple effect.

**Note:** You can combine them—wrap an `InkWell` in a `GestureDetector` if you need both ripple and advanced gesture handling.

### Question
How do I create a bottom navigation bar in Flutter?

### Answer
Here's how to create a **Bottom Navigation Bar** in Flutter using the standard `BottomNavigationBar` widget.

### Basic Example with Page Switching

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(
      debugShowCheckedModeBanner: false,
      home: BottomNavExample(),
    );
  }
}

class BottomNavExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const BottomNavExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<BottomNavExample> createState() => _BottomNavExampleState();
}

class _BottomNavExampleState extends State<BottomNavExample> {
  int _selectedIndex = 0;

  // Pages to display
  static const List<Widget> _pages = <Widget>[
    Center(child: Text('Home Page', style: TextStyle(fontSize: 24))),
    Center(child: Text('Search Page', style: TextStyle(fontSize: 24))),
    Center(child: Text('Profile Page', style: TextStyle(fontSize: 24))),
  ];

  void _onItemTapped(int index) {
    setState(() {
      _selectedIndex = index;
    });
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Bottom Navigation Bar')),
      body: _pages[_selectedIndex], // Switch content here
      bottomNavigationBar: BottomNavigationBar(
        items: const <BottomNavigationBarItem>[
          BottomNavigationBarItem(
            icon: Icon(Icons.home),
            label: 'Home',
          ),
          BottomNavigationBarItem(
            icon: Icon(Icons.search),
            label: 'Search',
          ),
          BottomNavigationBarItem(
            icon: Icon(Icons.person),
            label: 'Profile',
          ),
        ],
        currentIndex: _selectedIndex,
        selectedItemColor: Colors.blue,
        onTap: _onItemTapped,
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Properties Explained

| Property            | Description                                      | Common Values                  |
|---------------------|--------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------|
| `items`             | List of navigation items                         | `BottomNavigationBarItem`      |
| `currentIndex`      | Currently selected index                         | `0`, `1`, `2`...               |
| `onTap`             | Callback when an item is tapped                  | Function that updates state    |
| `type`              | Layout behavior                                  | `fixed` (default) or `shifting`|
| `selectedItemColor` | Color of selected item                           | Any `Color`                    |
| `backgroundColor`   | Background color of the bar                      | Any `Color`                    |

### Recommended: Use `IndexedStack` (Better Performance)

Instead of rebuilding the entire body,

### Question
How do I create an AppBar in Flutter?

### Answer
**Creating an AppBar in Flutter** is straightforward using the `Scaffold` widget.

### Basic Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(
          title: const Text('My App'),
        ),
        body: const Center(
          child: Text('Hello, Flutter!'),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common AppBar Features

Here's a more complete example with common customizations:

```dart
Scaffold(
  appBar: AppBar(
    title: const Text('Flutter App'),
    backgroundColor: Colors.blue,
    foregroundColor: Colors.white, // Text & icon color
    elevation: 4,
    centerTitle: true,
    leading: IconButton(
      icon: const Icon(Icons.menu),
      onPressed: () {
        // Open drawer or custom action
      },
    ),
    actions: [
      IconButton(
        icon: const Icon(Icons.search),
        onPressed: () {
          // Search action
        },
      ),
      IconButton(
        icon: const Icon(Icons.more_vert),
        onPressed: () {
          // More options
        },
      ),
    ],
  ),
  body: const Center(child: Text('Content here')),
)
```

### Key Properties

| Property       | Description                              | Example                     |
|----------------|------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| `title`        | Main title widget                        | `Text('Home')`              |
| `leading`      | Widget on the left (usually icon)        | `IconButton`                |
| `actions`      | Widgets on the right                     | List of `IconButton`s       |
| `backgroundColor` | Background color                      | `Colors.blue`               |
| `elevation`    | Shadow depth                             | `4.0`                       |
| `centerTitle`  | Center the title                         | `true`                      |
| `automaticallyImplyLeading` | Auto show back button         | `false`                     |

### Tip: Custom Height

If you need a taller AppBar:

```dart
appBar: PreferredSize(
  preferredSize: const Size.fromHeight(80),
  child: AppBar(
    title: const Text('Custom Height'),
  ),
),
```

### Question
How do I use Scaffold in Flutter?

### Answer
**Scaffold** is the most commonly used layout widget in Flutter. It provides a structure for implementing the basic Material Design visual layout, including an `AppBar`, `body`, `FloatingActionButton`, `Drawer`, `BottomNavigationBar`, etc.

### Basic Usage

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Scaffold Example')),
        body: const Center(
          child: Text('Hello, Scaffold!'),
        ),
        floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
          onPressed: () {},
          child: const Icon(Icons.add),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Properties of Scaffold

| Property                  | Type                        | Description                              |
|--------------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| `appBar`                 | `PreferredSizeWidget?`      | Top app bar                              |
| `body`                   | `Widget?`                   | Main content area                        |
| `floatingActionButton`   | `Widget?`                   | Floating action button                   |
| `drawer`                 | `Widget?`                   | Left side drawer                         |
| `endDrawer`              | `Widget?`                   | Right side drawer                        |
| `bottomNavigationBar`    | `Widget?`                   | Bottom navigation bar                    |
| `backgroundColor`        | `Color?`                    | Background color of the scaffold         |
| `resizeToAvoidBottomInset` | `bool?`                   | Whether to resize when keyboard appears  |

### Complete Example with Multiple Features

```dart

  const HomePage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(
        title: const Text('My App'),
        actions: [
          IconButton(
            icon: const Icon(Icons.search),
            onPressed: () {},
          ),
        ],
      ),
      body: ListView(
        children: const [
          ListTile(title: Text('Item 1')),
          ListTile(title: Text('Item 2')),
        ],
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton.extended(
        onPressed: () {
          ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
            const SnackBar(content: Text('FAB clicked!')),
          );
        },
        icon: const Icon(Icons.add),
        label: const Text('Add'),
      ),
      drawer: const Drawer(
        child: Center(child: Text('Drawer Content')),
      ),
      bottomNavigationBar: BottomNavigationBar(
        items: const [
          BottomNavigationBarItem(icon: Icon(Icons.home), label: 'Home'),
          BottomNavigationBarItem(icon: Icon(Icons.person),

### Question
How do I use TabBar in Flutter?

### Answer
**TabBar in Flutter** is used together with `TabBarView` to create tabbed navigation. The recommended and simplest approach is to use `DefaultTabController`.

### Basic Example (Recommended)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      debugShowCheckedModeBanner: false,
      home: DefaultTabController(
        length: 3,
        child: Scaffold(
          appBar: AppBar(
            title: const Text('TabBar Demo'),
            bottom: const TabBar(
              tabs: [
                Tab(icon: Icon(Icons.home), text: "Home"),
                Tab(icon: Icon(Icons.business), text: "Business"),
                Tab(icon: Icon(Icons.school), text: "School"),
              ],
            ),
          ),
          body: const TabBarView(
            children: [
              Center(child: Text("Home Page")),
              Center(child: Text("Business Page")),
              Center(child: Text("School Page")),
            ],
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Using Explicit TabController (More Control)

Use this when you need to programmatically control tabs or listen to changes:

```dart
class TabExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const TabExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<TabExample> createState() => _TabExampleState();
}

class _TabExampleState extends State<TabExample>
    with SingleTickerProviderStateMixin {
  late TabController _tabController;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _tabController = TabController(length: 3, vsync: this);
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _tabController.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(
        title: const Text('TabBar with Controller'),
        bottom: TabBar(
          controller: _tabController,
          tabs: const [
            Tab(text: "Tab 1"),
            Tab(text: "Tab 2"),
            Tab(text: "Tab 3"),
          ],
        ),
      ),
      body: TabBarView(
        controller: _tabController,
        children: const [
          Center(child: Text("Content 1")),
          Center(child: Text("Content 2")),
          Center(child: Text("Content 3")),
        ],
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Customizations

```dart
TabBar(
  controller: _tabController,
  isScrollable: true,                    // Allows horizontal scrolling
  labelColor: Colors.white,
  unselectedLabelColor: Colors.white70,
  indicatorColor: Colors.amber,
  indicatorWeight: 4,
  indicatorSize: TabBarIndicator

### Question
How do I use Drawer in Flutter?

### Answer
**Using `Drawer` in Flutter**

The `Drawer` widget creates a side panel (usually from the left) that slides in. It is most commonly used inside a `Scaffold`.

### Basic Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: const HomePage(),
    );
  }
}

class HomePage extends StatelessWidget {
  const HomePage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Drawer Example')),
      drawer: const AppDrawer(),
      body: const Center(child: Text('Main Content')),
    );
  }
}

class AppDrawer extends StatelessWidget {
  const AppDrawer({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Drawer(
      child: ListView(
        padding: EdgeInsets.zero,
        children: [
          const UserAccountsDrawerHeader(
            accountName: Text('John Doe'),
            accountEmail: Text('john@example.com'),
            currentAccountPicture: CircleAvatar(
              backgroundColor: Colors.white,
              child: Text('JD'),
            ),
          ),
          ListTile(
            leading: const Icon(Icons.home),
            title: const Text('Home'),
            onTap: () {
              Navigator.pop(context); // Close drawer
              // Navigate or do something
            },
          ),
          ListTile(
            leading: const Icon(Icons.person),
            title: const Text('Profile'),
            onTap: () {
              Navigator.pop(context);
              // Navigator.push(...);
            },
          ),
          ListTile(
            leading: const Icon(Icons.settings),
            title: const Text('Settings'),
            onTap: () {
              Navigator.pop(context);
            },
          ),
          const Divider(),
          ListTile(
            leading: const Icon(Icons.logout),
            title: const Text('Logout'),
            onTap: () {
              Navigator.pop(context);
            },
          ),
        ],
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points

- `drawer` goes directly on `Scaffold`.
- Use `ListView` inside the `Drawer` so it scrolls if needed.
- `DrawerHeader` or `UserAccountsDrawerHeader` is commonly placed at the top.
- Always call `Navigator.pop(context)` when a `ListTile` is tapped to close the drawer.
- For a drawer on the **right** side, use `endDrawer`.

### Programmatically Opening the Drawer

```dart
IconButton(
  icon: const Icon(Icons.menu),
  onPressed: () => Scaffold.of(context).openDrawer(),
)
```

Or using a `GlobalKey`:

```dart
final GlobalKey<ScaffoldState> _scaffoldKey = GlobalKey<ScaffoldState>();

### Question
How do I use Dialog in Flutter?

### Answer
**Using Dialog in Flutter**

Flutter provides the `showDialog` function to display dialogs. The most common approach is using `AlertDialog`, but you can also create custom dialogs.

### 1. Basic AlertDialog (Most Common)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Dialog Example')),
        body: Center(
          child: ElevatedButton(
            onPressed: () => _showDialog(context),
            child: const Text('Show Dialog'),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }

  void _showDialog(BuildContext context) {
    showDialog(
      context: context,
      builder: (BuildContext context) {
        return AlertDialog(
          title: const Text('Confirm Action'),
          content: const Text('Are you sure you want to proceed?'),
          actions: [
            TextButton(
              onPressed: () => Navigator.pop(context),
              child: const Text('Cancel'),
            ),
            TextButton(
              onPressed: () {
                Navigator.pop(context);
                // Perform action here
              },
              child: const Text('OK'),
            ),
          ],
        );
      },
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Custom Dialog

Use the `Dialog` widget for full customization:

```dart
showDialog(
  context: context,
  builder: (context) => Dialog(
    shape: RoundedRectangleBorder(borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(20)),
    child: Padding(
      padding: const EdgeInsets.all(20),
      child: Column(
        mainAxisSize: MainAxisSize.min,
        children: [
          const Icon(Icons.check_circle, color: Colors.green, size: 60),
          const SizedBox(height: 16),
          const Text('Success!', style: TextStyle(fontSize: 22, fontWeight: FontWeight.bold)),
          const SizedBox(height: 8),
          const Text('Your action was completed successfully.'),
          const SizedBox(height: 20),
          ElevatedButton(
            onPressed: () => Navigator.pop(context),
            child: const Text('Done'),
          ),
        ],
      ),
    ),
  ),
);
```

### 3. Other Dialog Types

| Type              | Use Case                     | Widget          |
|-------------------|------------------------------|-----------------|
| Alert             | Confirmation / warnings      | `AlertDialog`   |
| Simple            | Single choice selection      | `SimpleDialog`  |
| Custom            | Complex UI                   | `Dialog`        |
| Fullscreen        | Large content                | `showGeneralDialog` |

### 4. Useful Parameters in `showDialog`

```dart
showDialog(
  context: context,
  barrierDismissible: false,          

### Question
How do I use SnackBar in Flutter?

### Answer
**SnackBar in Flutter** is a temporary message that appears at the bottom of the screen. Since Flutter 2.0, the recommended way to show a `SnackBar` is using `ScaffoldMessenger`.

### Basic Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('SnackBar Example')),
        body: const SnackBarDemo(),
      ),
    );
  }
}

class SnackBarDemo extends StatelessWidget {
  const SnackBarDemo({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Center(
      child: ElevatedButton(
        onPressed: () {
          // Show SnackBar using ScaffoldMessenger
          ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
            const SnackBar(
              content: Text('This is a SnackBar'),
            ),
          );
        },
        child: const Text('Show SnackBar'),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### SnackBar with Action

```dart
ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
  SnackBar(
    content: const Text('Item deleted'),
    action: SnackBarAction(
      label: 'Undo',
      onPressed: () {
        // Undo logic here
      },
    ),
    duration: const Duration(seconds: 3),
  ),
);
```

### Floating SnackBar (Modern Look)

```dart
ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
  SnackBar(
    content: const Text('This is a floating SnackBar'),
    behavior: SnackBarBehavior.floating,
    shape: RoundedRectangleBorder(borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(10)),
    margin: const EdgeInsets.all(16),
  ),
);
```

### Best Practices & Tips

1. **Always use `ScaffoldMessenger`** (not the old `Scaffold.of(context)`).
2. **Clear previous SnackBars** (optional):

```dart
ScaffoldMessenger.of(context)
  ..hideCurrentSnackBar()
  ..showSnackBar(snackBar);
```

3. **Access from anywhere** (even outside the widget tree):

```dart
final messenger = ScaffoldMessenger.of(context);
messenger.showSnackBar(...);
```

4. **Global styling** (in `MaterialApp`):

```dart
MaterialApp(
  scaffoldMessengerTheme: const ScaffoldMessengerThemeData(
    snackBarTheme: SnackBarThemeData(
      behavior: SnackBarBehavior.floating,
      backgroundColor: Colors.black87,
    ),
  ),
  // ...
)
```

### Common Mistake (Avoid)

```dart
// ❌ Deprecated / problematic in some cases
Scaffold.of(context).showSnackBar(...) // Don't use this anymore
```

**Summary**: Use `ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnack

### Question
How do I use TextField in Flutter?

### Answer
**Basic TextField Usage**

Here's the most common and practical way to use `TextField` in Flutter:

### 1. Simple TextField

```dart
TextField(
  decoration: InputDecoration(
    labelText: 'Username',
    hintText: 'Enter your username',
    border: OutlineInputBorder(),
  ),
)
```

### 2. Recommended: Using `TextEditingController`

Always use a controller when you need to read or manipulate the text:

```dart
class MyForm extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<MyForm> createState() => _MyFormState();
}

class _MyFormState extends State<MyForm> {
  final TextEditingController _controller = TextEditingController();

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _controller.dispose(); // Important!
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: Padding(
        padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16.0),
        child: Column(
          children: [
            TextField(
              controller: _controller,
              decoration: InputDecoration(
                labelText: 'Full Name',
                hintText: 'John Doe',
                prefixIcon: Icon(Icons.person),
                border: OutlineInputBorder(),
              ),
              onChanged: (value) {
                print('Current text: $value');
              },
              onSubmitted: (value) {
                print('Submitted: $value');
              },
            ),
            SizedBox(height: 20),
            ElevatedButton(
              onPressed: () {
                print('Button pressed: ${_controller.text}');
              },
              child: Text('Get Value'),
            ),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 3. Common Useful Properties

```dart
TextField(
  controller: _controller,
  keyboardType: TextInputType.emailAddress, // number, phone, etc.
  obscureText: true, // for passwords
  maxLength: 50,
  maxLines: 3, // or 1 for single line
  autofocus: true,
  readOnly: false,
  enabled: true,
  textInputAction: TextInputAction.done,
  decoration: InputDecoration(
    labelText: 'Email',
    hintText: 'example@email.com',
    errorText: 'Invalid email', // show validation error
    prefixIcon: Icon(Icons.email),
    suffixIcon: Icon(Icons.visibility),
    border: OutlineInputBorder(),
    focusedBorder: OutlineInputBorder(
      borderSide: BorderSide(color: Colors.blue, width: 2),
    ),
  ),
)
```

### 4. Password Field Example

```dart
TextField(
  controller: _passwordController,
  obscureText: _obscurePassword,
  decoration: InputDecoration(
    labelText: 'Password',
    suffixIcon: IconButton(
      icon: Icon(_obscurePassword ? Icons.visibility :

### Question
How do I use Form and FormField in Flutter?

### Answer
**Form** and **FormField** are Flutter's built-in way to manage groups of input fields with validation, saving, and resetting.

### Core Concepts

- **`Form`**: A container widget that groups `FormField` descendants. It uses a `GlobalKey<FormState>` to access validation and saving methods.
- **`FormField<T>`**: The base class for form fields. Most common inputs have convenience versions:
  - `TextFormField` (for `TextField`)
  - `DropdownButtonFormField`
  - `CheckboxListTile`, `RadioListTile`, etc.

### Basic Example: Login Form

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class LoginForm extends StatefulWidget {
  const LoginForm({super.key});

  @override
  State<LoginForm> createState() => _LoginFormState();
}

class _LoginFormState extends State<LoginForm> {
  final _formKey = GlobalKey<FormState>();
  String _email = '';
  String _password = '';

  void _submitForm() {
    if (_formKey.currentState!.validate()) {
      _formKey.currentState!.save(); // Triggers onSaved callbacks

      // Do something with the data
      ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
        SnackBar(content: Text('Logged in as $_email')),
      );
    }
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Form(
      key: _formKey,
      child: Column(
        children: [
          TextFormField(
            decoration: const InputDecoration(labelText: 'Email'),
            keyboardType: TextInputType.emailAddress,
            validator: (value) {
              if (value == null || value.isEmpty) {
                return 'Please enter your email';
              }
              if (!value.contains('@')) {
                return 'Please enter a valid email';
              }
              return null;
            },
            onSaved: (value) => _email = value ?? '',
          ),
          const SizedBox(height: 16),
          TextFormField(
            decoration: const InputDecoration(labelText: 'Password'),
            obscureText: true,
            validator: (value) {
              if (value == null || value.length < 6) {
                return 'Password must be at least 6 characters';
              }
              return null;
            },
            onSaved: (value) => _password = value ?? '',
          ),
          const SizedBox(height: 24),
          ElevatedButton(
            onPressed: _submitForm,
            child: const Text('Login'),
          ),
          TextButton(
            onPressed: () => _formKey.currentState!.reset(),
            child: const Text('Reset'),
          ),
        ],
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Methods

| Method                        | Description                                      |
|------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------|
| `formKey.currentState!.validate()` | Runs all validators, returns `true` if

### Question
How do I validate a form in Flutter?

### Answer
To validate a form in Flutter, use the `Form` widget combined with `TextFormField` and a `GlobalKey<FormState>`.

### Basic Form Validation Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: FormExample());
  }
}

class FormExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const FormExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<FormExample> createState() => _FormExampleState();
}

class _FormExampleState extends State<FormExample> {
  final _formKey = GlobalKey<FormState>();
  final _emailController = TextEditingController();
  final _passwordController = TextEditingController();

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _emailController.dispose();
    _passwordController.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  void _submitForm() {
    if (_formKey.currentState!.validate()) {
      // Form is valid - proceed with submission
      ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
        const SnackBar(content: Text('Form submitted successfully!')),
      );
    }
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Form Validation')),
      body: Padding(
        padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16.0),
        child: Form(
          key: _formKey,
          child: Column(
            children: [
              TextFormField(
                controller: _emailController,
                decoration: const InputDecoration(labelText: 'Email'),
                keyboardType: TextInputType.emailAddress,
                validator: (value) {
                  if (value == null || value.isEmpty) {
                    return 'Please enter your email';
                  }
                  if (!value.contains('@')) {
                    return 'Please enter a valid email';
                  }
                  return null;
                },
              ),
              const SizedBox(height: 16),
              TextFormField(
                controller: _passwordController,
                decoration: const InputDecoration(labelText: 'Password'),
                obscureText: true,
                validator: (value) {
                  if (value == null || value.length < 6) {
                    return 'Password must be at least 6 characters';
                  }
                  return null;
                },
              ),
              const SizedBox(height: 24),
              ElevatedButton(
                onPressed: _submitForm,
                child: const Text('Submit'),
              ),
            ],
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points

- **`GlobalKey<FormState>`**: Used to access the form's state and trigger validation.
- **`validator`**: Returns `null` if valid, or an error string if invalid.
- **`_formKey.currentState!.validate()`**: Runs all validators and returns `true

### Question
How do I use DropdownButton in Flutter?

### Answer
**Here's how to use `DropdownButton` in Flutter** with a clean, complete example.

### Basic `DropdownButton` Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('DropdownButton Example')),
        body: const Center(child: DropdownExample()),
      ),
    );
  }
}

class DropdownExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const DropdownExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<DropdownExample> createState() => _DropdownExampleState();
}

class _DropdownExampleState extends State<DropdownExample> {
  String? selectedValue = 'Apple';

  final List<String> items = ['Apple', 'Banana', 'Orange', 'Mango'];

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return DropdownButton<String>(
      value: selectedValue,
      hint: const Text('Select a fruit'),
      icon: const Icon(Icons.arrow_drop_down),
      iconSize: 24,
      elevation: 16,
      style: const TextStyle(color: Colors.deepPurple, fontSize: 18),
      underline: Container(
        height: 2,
        color: Colors.deepPurpleAccent,
      ),
      isExpanded: true, // Makes dropdown take full width
      items: items.map<DropdownMenuItem<String>>((String value) {
        return DropdownMenuItem<String>(
          value: value,
          child: Text(value),
        );
      }).toList(),
      onChanged: (String? newValue) {
        setState(() {
          selectedValue = newValue;
        });
      },
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Properties Explained

| Property       | Description                                      | Common Use                     |
|----------------|--------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------|
| `value`        | Currently selected value                         | Must match one of the items    |
| `items`        | List of `DropdownMenuItem`                       | Required                       |
| `onChanged`    | Callback when user selects an item               | Usually calls `setState()`     |
| `hint`         | Shown when `value` is null                       | Placeholder text               |
| `isExpanded`   | Makes dropdown take full available width         | Useful in forms                |
| `icon`         | Custom dropdown arrow                            | Change icon/color              |
| `dropdownColor`| Background color of the dropdown menu            | Theming                        |

### With Custom Objects (Best Practice)

Instead of using `String`, it's often better to work with model objects:

```dart
class Fruit {
  final String name;
  final String emoji;

  Fruit(this.name, this.emoji);
}

final List<Fruit> fruits = [
  Fruit('Apple', '🍎'),
  Fruit('Banana', '

### Question
How do I use Checkbox in Flutter?

### Answer
**Basic Checkbox Usage**

Here's the simplest way to use a `Checkbox` in Flutter:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: CheckboxExample());
  }
}

class CheckboxExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const CheckboxExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<CheckboxExample> createState() => _CheckboxExampleState();
}

class _CheckboxExampleState extends State<CheckboxExample> {
  bool isChecked = false;

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Checkbox Example')),
      body: Center(
        child: Checkbox(
          value: isChecked,
          onChanged: (bool? value) {
            setState(() {
              isChecked = value ?? false;
            });
          },
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### CheckboxListTile (Recommended)

For a better UX with a label, use `CheckboxListTile`:

```dart
bool isChecked = false;

CheckboxListTile(
  title: const Text('I agree to the terms'),
  value: isChecked,
  onChanged: (bool? value) {
    setState(() {
      isChecked = value ?? false;
    });
  },
  controlAffinity: ListTileControlAffinity.leading, // checkbox on left
)
```

### Tristate Checkbox (true / false / null)

```dart
bool? isChecked = false; // can be true, false, or null

Checkbox(
  value: isChecked,
  tristate: true,
  onChanged: (bool? value) {
    setState(() {
      isChecked = value;
    });
  },
)
```

### Customization

```dart
Checkbox(
  value: isChecked,
  onChanged: (value) => setState(() => isChecked = value!),
  activeColor: Colors.green,
  checkColor: Colors.white,
  shape: RoundedRectangleBorder(borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(4)),
  side: const BorderSide(color: Colors.grey, width: 2),
)
```

### Best Practices

- Always use `StatefulWidget`, Riverpod, Bloc, or another state management solution for real apps.
- Use `CheckboxListTile` when you need a label.
- Handle `null` safely when using `tristate: true`.

Would you like an example using **Riverpod** or **Provider** instead of `setState`?

### Question
How do I use Switch in Flutter?

### Answer
**Basic Usage of `Switch` in Flutter**

The `Switch` widget is used to toggle between two states (`true`/`false`). It requires a `value` and an `onChanged` callback.

### Minimal Working Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: const SwitchExample(),
    );
  }
}

class SwitchExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const SwitchExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<SwitchExample> createState() => _SwitchExampleState();
}

class _SwitchExampleState extends State<SwitchExample> {
  bool _isEnabled = false;

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Switch Example')),
      body: Center(
        child: Switch(
          value: _isEnabled,
          onChanged: (bool value) {
            setState(() {
              _isEnabled = value;
            });
          },
          activeColor: Colors.green,
          inactiveThumbColor: Colors.grey,
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Properties

| Property              | Description                              | Example                     |
|-----------------------|------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| `value`               | Current state                            | `true` / `false`            |
| `onChanged`           | Called when toggled                      | `(val) => setState(...)`    |
| `activeColor`         | Color of thumb when active               | `Colors.blue`               |
| `activeTrackColor`    | Track color when active                  | `Colors.blue.shade200`      |
| `inactiveThumbColor`  | Thumb color when inactive                | `Colors.grey`               |
| `inactiveTrackColor`  | Track color when inactive                | `Colors.grey.shade300`      |
| `thumbIcon`           | Custom icon on thumb (Flutter 3.10+)     | `WidgetStateProperty`       |

### Using `SwitchListTile` (Recommended for Lists)

```dart
SwitchListTile(
  title: const Text('Enable Notifications'),
  subtitle: const Text('Receive push notifications'),
  value: _isEnabled,
  onChanged: (bool value) {
