    setState(() => _isEnabled = value);
  },
  activeColor: Colors.green,
)
```

### Adaptive Switch (Material + Cupertino)

Use `Switch.adaptive` to automatically use `CupertinoSwitch` on iOS:

```dart
Switch.adaptive(
  value: _isEnabled,
  onChanged: (value) => setState(() => _isEnabled = value),
)
```

### Best Practices

- Always wrap state changes in `setState()` (or use a state management solution).


### Question
How do I use Slider in Flutter?

### Answer
**Slider in Flutter** is a Material widget that lets users select a value from a continuous or discrete range.

### Basic Continuous Slider

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: const SliderExample(),
    );
  }
}

class SliderExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const SliderExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<SliderExample> createState() => _SliderExampleState();
}

class _SliderExampleState extends State<SliderExample> {
  double _value = 50;

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Flutter Slider')),
      body: Center(
        child: Column(
          mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
          children: [
            Text(
              _value.toStringAsFixed(1),
              style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 40),
            ),
            Slider(
              value: _value,
              min: 0,
              max: 100,
              onChanged: (newValue) {
                setState(() {
                  _value = newValue;
                });
              },
            ),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Discrete Slider (with divisions)

Add the `divisions` parameter to create step-based values:

```dart
Slider(
  value: _value,
  min: 0,
  max: 100,
  divisions: 10,           // Creates 11 steps (0, 10, 20, ..., 100)
  label: _value.round().toString(), // Shows label above thumb
  onChanged: (newValue) {
    setState(() => _value = newValue);
  },
)
```

### Customization

```dart
Slider(
  value: _value,
  min: 0,
  max: 100,
  activeColor: Colors.deepPurple,
  inactiveColor: Colors.deepPurple.shade100,
  thumbColor: Colors.deepPurpleAccent,
  onChanged: (val) => setState(() => _value = val),
)
```

### Using `SliderTheme` for Advanced Styling

```dart
SliderTheme(
  data: SliderTheme.of(context).copyWith(
    trackHeight: 4,
    thumbShape: const RoundSliderThumbShape(enabledThumbRadius: 12),
    overlayShape: const RoundSliderOverlayShape(overlayRadius: 24),
    activeTrackColor: Colors.green,
    inactiveTrackColor: Colors.green.shade100,
    thumbColor: Colors.white,
    overlayColor: Colors.green.withOpacity(0.2),
  ),
  child: Slider(
    value: _value,
    min: 0,
    max: 100,
   

### Question
How do I use Image widget in Flutter?

### Answer
**The `Image` widget** is Flutter’s built-in way to display images. It supports multiple sources: assets, network, files, and raw bytes.

### 1. Basic Usage

#### A. Image from Assets (Most Common)

**pubspec.yaml**
```yaml
flutter:
  assets:
    - assets/images/
```

**Code**
```dart
Image.asset(
  'assets/images/logo.png',
  width: 200,
  height: 200,
  fit: BoxFit.cover,
)
```

#### B. Image from Network

```dart
Image.network(
  'https://picsum.photos/300/300',
  width: 300,
  height: 300,
  fit: BoxFit.cover,
  loadingBuilder: (context, child, loadingProgress) {
    if (loadingProgress == null) return child;
    return const Center(child: CircularProgressIndicator());
  },
  errorBuilder: (context, error, stackTrace) {
    return const Icon(Icons.error);
  },
)
```

#### C. Other Sources

```dart
// From file
Image.file(File('/path/to/image.png'))

// From memory (Uint8List)
Image.memory(bytes)
```

### 2. Common Properties

| Property       | Description                          | Example                     |
|----------------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| `width` / `height` | Size of the image                  | `width: 150`                |
| `fit`          | How image should be inscribed      | `BoxFit.cover`              |
| `alignment`    | Alignment within its box           | `Alignment.center`          |
| `color`        | Tint color                         | `Colors.blue`               |
| `colorBlendMode` | Blend mode with color            | `BlendMode.modulate`        |
| `repeat`       | Repeat pattern                     | `ImageRepeat.repeat`        |

### 3. Complete Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Image Widget')),
        body: Center(
          child: Column(
            mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
            children: [
              // Asset image
              Image.asset(
                'assets/images/flutter.png',
                width: 150,
                height: 150,
              ),
              const SizedBox(height: 20),
              // Network image with loading & error handling
              Image.network(
                'https://picsum.photos/id/1015/300/200',
                width: 300,
                height: 200,
                fit: BoxFit.cover,
                loadingBuilder: (context, child, progress) {
                  return progress == null
                      ? child
                      : const SizedBox

### Question
How do I use CircleAvatar in Flutter?

### Answer
**CircleAvatar** is a built-in Flutter widget used to display a circular avatar (commonly for user profile pictures). It supports images, text/initials, or custom child widgets.

### Basic Usage

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('CircleAvatar Example')),
        body: const Center(
          child: CircleAvatar(
            radius: 40,
            backgroundImage: NetworkImage(
              'https://i.pravatar.cc/150?img=47',
            ),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Variations

#### 1. With Image + Fallback Color
```dart
CircleAvatar(
  radius: 50,
  backgroundColor: Colors.grey[200],
  backgroundImage: const NetworkImage('https://i.pravatar.cc/150?img=12'),
  // Optional: shows while image loads
  // foregroundColor: Colors.white,
)
```

#### 2. With Initials/Text (No Image)
```dart
CircleAvatar(
  radius: 30,
  backgroundColor: Colors.deepPurple,
  child: const Text(
    'JD',
    style: TextStyle(fontSize: 24, color: Colors.white, fontWeight: FontWeight.bold),
  ),
)
```

#### 3. With Asset Image
```dart
CircleAvatar(
  radius: 35,
  backgroundImage: const AssetImage('assets/profile.jpg'),
)
```

#### 4. With Custom Child (e.g., Icon)
```dart
CircleAvatar(
  radius: 28,
  backgroundColor: Colors.green,
  child: const Icon(Icons.person, color: Colors.white, size: 30),
)
```

### Useful Properties

| Property           | Type           | Description                              |
|--------------------|----------------|------------------------------------------|
| `radius`           | `double?`      | Size of the avatar                       |
| `backgroundColor`  | `Color?`       | Background when no image                 |
| `backgroundImage`  | `ImageProvider?` | Main image (most common)               |
| `foregroundImage`  | `ImageProvider?` | Image drawn on top of background       |
| `child`            | `Widget?`      | Widget inside the circle                 |
| `foregroundColor`  | `Color?`       | Color applied to the `child`             |

### Pro Tips

- Use `radius` to control size instead of `width`/`height`.
- For lists, combine with `ListTile`:
  ```dart
  ListTile(
    leading: const CircleAvatar(
      backgroundImage: NetworkImage('...'),
    ),
    title: const Text('

### Question
How do I use Card widget in Flutter?

### Answer
**Card Widget in Flutter**

The `Card` widget is a Material Design component that displays content inside a rounded rectangle with a drop shadow (elevation). It's commonly used to group related information.

### Basic Usage

```dart
Card(
  child: Padding(
    padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16.0),
    child: Text('This is a Card'),
  ),
)
```

### Recommended: Card with ListTile (Most Common Pattern)

```dart
Card(
  elevation: 4,
  margin: const EdgeInsets.all(12),
  shape: RoundedRectangleBorder(
    borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(12),
  ),
  child: ListTile(
    leading: const Icon(Icons.album, size: 40),
    title: const Text('The Dark Side of the Moon'),
    subtitle: const Text('Pink Floyd • 1973'),
    trailing: const Icon(Icons.play_arrow),
    onTap: () {
      // Handle tap
    },
  ),
)
```

### Full Working Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Card Example')),
        body: Center(
          child: Card(
            elevation: 8,
            color: Colors.white,
            shadowColor: Colors.black54,
            shape: RoundedRectangleBorder(
              borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(16),
            ),
            margin: const EdgeInsets.all(16),
            child: Padding(
              padding: const EdgeInsets.all(20),
              child: Column(
                mainAxisSize: MainAxisSize.min,
                children: [
                  const Text(
                    'Flutter Card',
                    style: TextStyle(fontSize: 22, fontWeight: FontWeight.bold),
                  ),
                  const SizedBox(height: 12),
                  const Text('This is a customized Card widget'),
                  const SizedBox(height: 20),
                  ElevatedButton(
                    onPressed: () {},
                    child: const Text('Learn More'),
                  ),
                ],
              ),
            ),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Properties

| Property       | Type                    | Description                              |
|----------------|-------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| `child`        | `Widget`                | Content inside the card                  |
| `elevation`    | `double`                | Shadow depth (default: 1.0)              |
| `color`        | `Color`                 | Background color                         |
| `shadowColor`  | `Color`                 | Color of the shadow                      |
| `shape`        | `ShapeBorder`           | Custom shape (usually `RoundedRectangleBorder`) |
| `margin`       | `EdgeInsetsGeometry`    | Space around the card

### Question
How do I use ListTile in Flutter?

### Answer
**ListTile** is one of the most commonly used widgets in Flutter for creating list items. It provides a clean, Material Design-styled row with support for leading/trailing widgets, title, subtitle, and tap handling.

### Basic Usage

```dart
ListTile(
  leading: const Icon(Icons.person),
  title: const Text('John Doe'),
  subtitle: const Text('Software Engineer'),
  trailing: const Icon(Icons.arrow_forward_ios, size: 16),
  onTap: () {
    print('Tapped on John Doe');
  },
)
```

### Complete Working Example

Here's a full example showing `ListTile` inside a `ListView`:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('ListTile Example')),
        body: ListView(
          children: const [
            ListTile(
              leading: CircleAvatar(child: Icon(Icons.person)),
              title: Text('Alice Johnson'),
              subtitle: Text('alice@example.com'),
              trailing: Icon(Icons.chevron_right),
            ),
            Divider(),
            ListTile(
              leading: Icon(Icons.star, color: Colors.amber),
              title: Text('Favorite Item'),
              subtitle: Text('This is a subtitle'),
              trailing: Icon(Icons.favorite, color: Colors.red),
              onTap: null, // Can be used to disable tap
            ),
            Divider(),
            ListTile(
              title: Text('Just a title'),
              trailing: Icon(Icons.settings),
            ),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Using `ListView.builder` (Recommended for dynamic lists)

```dart
ListView.builder(
  itemCount: users.length,
  itemBuilder: (context, index) {
    final user = users[index];
    return ListTile(
      leading: CircleAvatar(
        backgroundImage: NetworkImage(user.avatarUrl),
      ),
      title: Text(user.name),
      subtitle: Text(user.email),
      trailing: const Icon(Icons.arrow_forward_ios, size: 16),
      onTap: () {
        Navigator.push(
          context,
          MaterialPageRoute(
            builder: (_) => UserDetailPage(user: user),
          ),
        );
      },
    );
  },
)
```

### Common Properties

| Property     | Type           | Description                              |
|--------------|----------------|------------------------------------------|
| `leading`    | `Widget?`      | Widget on the left (icon, avatar, etc.)  |
| `title`      | `Widget?`      | Main text (usually `Text`)               |
| `subtitle`   | `Widget?`      | Secondary text below the title           |
| `trailing`   | `Widget?`

### Question
How do I use Padding widget in Flutter?

### Answer
**Padding Widget in Flutter**

The `Padding` widget adds space around its child widget. It is one of the most commonly used layout widgets.

### Basic Usage

```dart
Padding(
  padding: EdgeInsets.all(16.0),
  child: Text('Hello Flutter'),
)
```

### Different Ways to Apply Padding

Here are the most common `EdgeInsets` options:

```dart
// 1. Same padding on all sides
Padding(
  padding: EdgeInsets.all(20.0),
  child: YourWidget(),
)

// 2. Padding on specific sides only
Padding(
  padding: EdgeInsets.only(
    left: 16.0,
    top: 8.0,
    right: 16.0,
    bottom: 8.0,
  ),
  child: YourWidget(),
)

// 3. Symmetric padding (horizontal & vertical)
Padding(
  padding: EdgeInsets.symmetric(
    horizontal: 24.0,
    vertical: 12.0,
  ),
  child: YourWidget(),
)

// 4. Zero padding on some sides
Padding(
  padding: EdgeInsets.only(left: 30.0), // other sides = 0
  child: YourWidget(),
)
```

### Complete Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Padding Example')),
        body: Center(
          child: Column(
            mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
            children: [
              // Uniform padding
              Padding(
                padding: const EdgeInsets.all(20.0),
                child: Container(
                  color: Colors.blue,
                  child: const Text('All sides padding'),
                ),
              ),

              // Horizontal + Vertical padding
              Padding(
                padding: const EdgeInsets.symmetric(
                  horizontal: 40.0,
                  vertical: 10.0,
                ),
                child: Container(
                  color: Colors.green,
                  child: const Text('Symmetric padding'),
                ),
              ),

              // Specific sides
              Padding(
                padding: const EdgeInsets.only(top: 30.0, left: 50.0),
                child: Container(
                  color: Colors.orange,
                  child: const Text('Only top & left'),
                ),
              ),
            ],
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Quick Tips

- Use `const EdgeInsets` whenever possible for better performance.
- `Padding` is often used inside `ListView`, `Column`, `Row`, or around buttons/cards.
- For more complex spacing, consider `SizedBox` or `Spacer` as alternatives.

Would you like examples of combining `Padding` with other widgets like `Container

### Question
How do I use Align widget in Flutter?

### Answer
**The `Align` widget** positions its child within the available space using an `AlignmentGeometry` value.

### Basic Usage

```dart
Align(
  alignment: Alignment.center,
  child: Text('I am centered'),
)
```

### Common Alignment Values

| Alignment              | Position                  |
|------------------------|---------------------------|
| `Alignment.topLeft`    | Top-left corner           |
| `Alignment.topCenter`  | Top center                |
| `Alignment.topRight`   | Top-right corner          |
| `Alignment.centerLeft` | Center-left               |
| `Alignment.center`     | Center (default)          |
| `Alignment.centerRight`| Center-right              |
| `Alignment.bottomLeft` | Bottom-left               |
| `Alignment.bottomCenter`| Bottom center            |
| `Alignment.bottomRight`| Bottom-right              |

You can also use custom values:

```dart
alignment: Alignment(0.5, -0.8), // x: -1(left) to 1(right), y: -1(top) to 1(bottom)
```

### Full Working Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Align Widget')),
        body: Container(
          color: Colors.grey[200],
          child: Align(
            alignment: Alignment.bottomRight,
            child: Container(
              width: 120,
              height: 60,
              color: Colors.blue,
              alignment: Alignment.center,
              child: const Text(
                'Bottom Right',
                style: TextStyle(color: Colors.white),
              ),
            ),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Controlling Size with `widthFactor` & `heightFactor`

By default, `Align` takes all available space. Use `widthFactor` and `heightFactor` to size it relative to the child:

```dart
Align(
  alignment: Alignment.center,
  widthFactor: 2.0,   // Width = child width × 2
  heightFactor: 1.5,  // Height = child height × 1.5
  child: const FlutterLogo(size: 60),
)
```

### Common Use Cases

- Centering a widget inside a `Stack`
- Positioning elements in a `Container` without using `padding`
- Creating custom layouts when `Center` is too limited

**Tip**: If you only need to center something, prefer `Center` widget for cleaner code. Use `Align` when you need non-center positioning.

### Question
How do I use Center widget in Flutter?

### Answer
**The `Center` widget** in Flutter centers its child both horizontally and vertically within the available space.

### Basic Usage

```dart
Center(
  child: Text(
    'This text is centered',
    style: TextStyle(fontSize: 24),
  ),
)
```

### Complete Example

Here's a minimal, runnable Flutter app:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Center Widget')),
        body: Center(
          child: Column(
            mainAxisSize: MainAxisSize.min,
            children: [
              const Icon(Icons.star, size: 80, color: Colors.amber),
              const SizedBox(height: 16),
              const Text(
                'Centered Content',
                style: TextStyle(fontSize: 24, fontWeight: FontWeight.bold),
              ),
              const SizedBox(height: 8),
              ElevatedButton(
                onPressed: () {},
                child: const Text('Click Me'),
              ),
            ],
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points

- `Center` takes only **one child**.
- It expands to fill all available space and places its child in the middle.
- You can control sizing using `widthFactor` and `heightFactor`:

```dart
Center(
  widthFactor: 2.0,   // Width will be 2x the child's width
  heightFactor: 2.0,  // Height will be 2x the child's height
  child: const Text('Custom Size'),
)
```

### Common Use Cases

| Use Case                    | Example                              |
|----------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| Center text/icon             | `Center(child: Text('Hello'))`       |
| Center inside Column/Row     | Use `Center` as a child of `Column`  |
| Center a widget with padding | Wrap `Center` inside `Padding`       |
| Full screen centered content | Use inside `Scaffold` body           |

**Tip**: If you're using `Center` inside a `Column` or `Row` and it's not behaving as expected, make sure the parent isn't constraining the space (e.g., using `mainAxisSize: MainAxisSize.min`).

### Question
How do I use Wrap widget in Flutter?

### Answer
The `Wrap` widget in Flutter lays out its children in **multiple runs** (lines), either horizontally or vertically. Unlike `Row` or `Column`, it automatically wraps children to the next line when they exceed the available space.

### Basic Usage

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class WrapExample extends StatelessWidget {
  const WrapExample({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Wrap Widget')),
      body: Padding(
        padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16.0),
        child: Wrap(
          spacing: 8.0,        // Horizontal space between children
          runSpacing: 12.0,    // Vertical space between lines
          children: List.generate(12, (index) {
            return Chip(
              label: Text('Item ${index + 1}'),
              backgroundColor: Colors.blue.shade100,
            );
          }),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Properties

| Property              | Type                | Description                                      | Default          |
|-----------------------|---------------------|--------------------------------------------------|------------------|
| `direction`           | `Axis`              | Main axis direction (`horizontal` or `vertical`) | `Axis.horizontal` |
| `alignment`           | `WrapAlignment`     | Alignment within each run                        | `start`          |
| `runAlignment`        | `WrapAlignment`     | Alignment of runs themselves                     | `start`          |
| `spacing`             | `double`            | Space between children in main axis              | `0.0`            |
| `runSpacing`          | `double`            | Space between runs                               | `0.0`            |
| `crossAxisAlignment`  | `WrapCrossAlignment`| Alignment in cross axis                          | `start`          |

### Example with Vertical Direction & Alignment

```dart
Wrap(
  direction: Axis.vertical,
  spacing: 8,
  runSpacing: 16,
  alignment: WrapAlignment.center,
  runAlignment: WrapAlignment.spaceBetween,
  children: [
    _buildBox('A'),
    _buildBox('B'),
    _buildBox('C'),
    _buildBox('D'),
    _buildBox('E'),
  ],
)
```

Helper widget:

```dart
Widget _buildBox(String text) {
  return Container(
    width: 80,
    height: 50,
    alignment: Alignment.center,
    color: Colors.teal,
    child: Text(text, style: const TextStyle(color: Colors.white)),
  );
}
```

### When to Use Wrap vs Row/Column

- Use **Wrap** when children should flow to multiple lines (tags, filters, buttons).
- Use **Row/Column** when you want strict single-line behavior (or use `SingleChildScrollView` for scrolling).

### Pro Tip:

### Question
How do I use SingleChildScrollView in Flutter?

### Answer
**`SingleChildScrollView`** is a Flutter widget that makes its single child scrollable when the content exceeds the available space.

### Basic Usage

Wrap any widget (commonly a `Column` or `Row`) with `SingleChildScrollView`:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class MyScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyScreen({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('SingleChildScrollView')),
      body: SingleChildScrollView(
        child: Column(
          children: List.generate(
            30,
            (index) => ListTile(
              leading: CircleAvatar(child: Text('${index + 1}')),
              title: Text('Item ${index + 1}'),
            ),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Properties

| Property          | Type              | Description                                      | Default          |
|-------------------|-------------------|--------------------------------------------------|------------------|
| `child`           | `Widget?`         | The widget to scroll                             | —                |
| `scrollDirection` | `Axis`            | `vertical` or `horizontal`                       | `Axis.vertical`  |
| `padding`         | `EdgeInsetsGeometry?` | Padding around the child                     | `null`           |
| `physics`         | `ScrollPhysics?`  | Scrolling behavior (e.g., `BouncingScrollPhysics`) | Platform default |
| `controller`      | `ScrollController?` | Control scroll position                      | `null`           |
| `reverse`         | `bool`            | Reverse scroll direction                         | `false`          |

### Practical Example with Controller & Padding

```dart
class ScrollableForm extends StatefulWidget {
  const ScrollableForm({super.key});

  @override
  State<ScrollableForm> createState() => _ScrollableFormState();
}

class _ScrollableFormState extends State<ScrollableForm> {
  final ScrollController _scrollController = ScrollController();

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _scrollController.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return SingleChildScrollView(
      controller: _scrollController,
      padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16),
      physics: const BouncingScrollPhysics(), // iOS-style bouncing
      child: Column(
        crossAxisAlignment: CrossAxisAlignment.stretch,
        children: [
          const TextField(decoration: InputDecoration(labelText: 'Name')),
          const TextField(decoration: InputDecoration(labelText: 'Email')),
          const SizedBox(height: 400), // Simulate long content
          ElevatedButton(
            onPressed: () {
              _scrollController.animateTo(
                0,
                duration: const Duration(milliseconds: 300),
                curve: Curves.easeOut,
              );
           

### Question
How do I navigate between screens in Flutter?

### Answer
**Navigation in Flutter** is primarily handled by the `Navigator` class, which manages a stack of `Route` objects (screens).

### 1. Basic Navigation (Imperative)

Use `Navigator.push()` to go forward and `Navigator.pop()` to go back.

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(
      home: HomeScreen(),
    );
  }
}

class HomeScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  const HomeScreen({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Home')),
      body: Center(
        child: ElevatedButton(
          onPressed: () {
            Navigator.push(
              context,
              MaterialPageRoute(builder: (context) => const SecondScreen()),
            );
          },
          child: const Text('Go to Second Screen'),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}

class SecondScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  const SecondScreen({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Second Screen')),
      body: Center(
        child: ElevatedButton(
          onPressed: () => Navigator.pop(context),
          child: const Text('Go Back'),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Named Routes (Recommended for Most Apps)

Define routes in `MaterialApp` for cleaner code:

```dart
MaterialApp(
  initialRoute: '/',
  routes: {
    '/': (context) => const HomeScreen(),
    '/second': (context) => const SecondScreen(),
    '/detail': (context) => const DetailScreen(),
  },
)
```

Usage:

```dart
// Navigate
Navigator.pushNamed(context, '/second');

// Go back
Navigator.pop(context);
```

### 3. Passing Data Between Screens

**Option A: Using Constructor (Most Common)**

```dart
Navigator.push(
  context,
  MaterialPageRoute(
    builder: (context) => SecondScreen(data: 'Hello from Home'),
  ),
);
```

**Option B: Using `arguments` with named routes**

```dart
// Sending
Navigator.pushNamed(
  context,
  '/detail',
  arguments: {'id': 42, 'name': 'Flutter'},
);

// Receiving
class DetailScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  const DetailScreen({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final args = ModalRoute.of(context)!.settings.arguments as Map;
    return Text('ID: ${args['id']}');
  }
}
```

### 4. Modern Approach: `go_router` (Recommended for Production)

For larger apps, use the `go_router` package instead of the built

### Question
How do I use go_router in Flutter?

### Answer
Here's a comprehensive guide to using `go_router` in Flutter:

## 1. Installation

Add to `pubspec.yaml`:
```yaml
dependencies:
  go_router: ^14.2.0
```

## 2. Basic Setup

Create a router configuration and use it in `MaterialApp`:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:go_router/go_router.dart';

// Define routes
final GoRouter _router = GoRouter(
  routes: [
    GoRoute(
      path: '/',
      builder: (context, state) => const HomeScreen(),
    ),
    GoRoute(
      path: '/details',
      builder: (context, state) => const DetailsScreen(),
    ),
  ],
);

void main() => runApp(MaterialApp.router(
  routerConfig: _router,
));
```

## 3. Navigation

```dart
// Navigate (replaces current route)
context.go('/details');

// Push onto stack (keeps previous routes)
context.push('/details');

// Go back
context.pop();

// With data
context.go('/details', extra: {'id': 123});
```

## 4. Route Parameters

```dart
GoRoute(
  path: '/user/:id',
  builder: (context, state) {
    final userId = state.pathParameters['id']!;
    return UserScreen(userId: userId);
  },
),
```

Navigate with:
```dart
context.go('/user/42');
```

## 5. Query Parameters

```dart
GoRoute(
  path: '/search',
  builder: (context, state) {
    final query = state.uri.queryParameters['q'] ?? '';
    return SearchScreen(query: query);
  },
),
```

Navigate with:
```dart
context.go('/search?q=flutter');
```

## 6. Nested Routes (ShellRoute)

For layouts with persistent UI (like bottom navigation):

```dart
final GoRouter _router = GoRouter(
  routes: [
    ShellRoute(
      builder: (context, state, child) {
        return ScaffoldWithNavBar(child: child);
      },
      routes: [
        GoRoute(
          path: '/home',
          builder: (context, state) => const HomeScreen(),
        ),
        GoRoute(
          path: '/profile',
          builder: (context, state) => const ProfileScreen(),
        ),
      ],
    ),
  ],
);
```

## 7. Error Handling

```dart
GoRouter(
  routes: [...],
  errorBuilder: (context, state) => ErrorScreen(error: state.error),
),
```

## 8. Redirects

```dart
GoRouter(
  routes: [...],
  redirect: (context, state) {
    final isLoggedIn = checkAuth();
    if (!isLoggedIn && state.uri.path != '/login') {
      return '/login';
    }
    return null; // No redirect
  },
),
```

## Best

### Question
How do I pass data between screens in Flutter?

### Answer
**There are several ways to pass data between screens in Flutter.** Here are the most common and recommended approaches:

### 1. Passing Data via Constructor (Most Common & Simple)

This is the easiest and most straightforward method.

```dart
// Screen 1 - HomeScreen
class HomeScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text('Home')),
      body: Center(
        child: ElevatedButton(
          child: Text('Go to Details'),
          onPressed: () {
            Navigator.push(
              context,
              MaterialPageRoute(
                builder: (context) => DetailScreen(
                  title: 'Flutter Tips',
                  description: 'Learn how to pass data',
                  id: 42,
                ),
              ),
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}

// Screen 2 - DetailScreen
class DetailScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  final String title;
  final String description;
  final int id;

  const DetailScreen({
    required this.title,
    required this.description,
    required this.id,
  });

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text(title)),
      body: Padding(
        padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16.0),
        child: Column(
          crossAxisAlignment: CrossAxisAlignment.start,
          children: [
            Text('ID: $id', style: TextStyle(fontSize: 18)),
            SizedBox(height: 8),
            Text(description),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Using Named Routes with Arguments (Recommended for Larger Apps)

```dart
// main.dart
void main() {
  runApp(MaterialApp(
    initialRoute: '/',
    routes: {
      '/': (context) => HomeScreen(),
      '/details': (context) => DetailScreen(),
    },
  ));
}

// Passing data
Navigator.pushNamed(
  context,
  '/details',
  arguments: {
    'title': 'Flutter Tips',
    'id': 42,
  },
);

// Receiving data
class DetailScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final args = ModalRoute.of(context)!.settings.arguments as Map;

    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text(args['title'])),
      body: Text('ID: ${args['id']}'),
    );
  }
}
```

### 3. Returning Data Back to Previous Screen

```dart
// From second screen
Navigator.pop(context, 'Data returned from second screen');

// From first screen
final result = await Navigator.push(
  context,
  MaterialPageRoute(builder: (context) => SecondScreen()),
);

print(result); // 'Data returned from second screen'
```

### Recommendation

| Use Case                        | Recommended Approach          |
|--------------------------------|-------------------------------|
|

### Question
How do I implement deep linking in Flutter?

### Answer
To implement **deep linking** in Flutter, the modern and recommended approach is to use **`go_router`** (for navigation) combined with the **`app_links`** package (to listen for incoming links).

Here’s a complete, production-ready setup:

### 1. Add Dependencies

```yaml
dependencies:
  go_router: ^14.2.0
  app_links: ^6.1.1
```

### 2. Configure Platform Deep Links

#### Android (`android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml`)

Add an intent filter inside your `<activity>`:

```xml
<activity ...>
    <intent-filter android:autoVerify="true">
        <action android:name="android.intent.action.VIEW" />
        <category android:name="android.intent.category.DEFAULT" />
        <category android:name="android.intent.category.BROWSABLE" />
        <data android:scheme="https" android:host="yourdomain.com" />
        <data android:scheme="myapp" android:host="open" /> <!-- Custom scheme -->
    </intent-filter>
</activity>
```

#### iOS (`ios/Runner/Info.plist`)

```xml
<key>CFBundleURLTypes</key>
<array>
  <dict>
    <key>CFBundleURLSchemes</key>
    <array>
      <string>myapp</string>
    </array>
  </dict>
</array>
```

Also set up **Universal Links** (App Links) by hosting an `apple-app-site-association` file.

### 3. Setup `go_router` with Deep Linking

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:go_router/go_router.dart';
import 'package:app_links/app_links.dart';

final _rootNavigatorKey = GlobalKey<NavigatorState>();

class AppRouter {
  static final AppLinks _appLinks = AppLinks();

  static final GoRouter router = GoRouter(
    navigatorKey: _rootNavigatorKey,
    initialLocation: '/',
    routes: [
      GoRoute(
        path: '/',
        builder: (context, state) => const HomeScreen(),
      ),
      GoRoute(
        path: '/product/:id',
        builder: (context, state) => ProductScreen(
          id: state.pathParameters['id']!,
        ),
      ),
    ],
  );

  /// Initialize deep link listener
  static Future<void> initDeepLinks() async {
    // Handle link when app is opened from terminated state
    final initialLink = await _appLinks.getInitialLink();
    if (initialLink != null) {
      _handleDeepLink(initialLink);
    }

    // Handle links when app is in background/foreground
    _appLinks.uriLinkStream.listen((uri) {
      _handleDeepLink(uri);
    });
  }

  static void _handleDeepLink(Uri uri) {
    // Convert incoming URI to go_router path
    final path = uri.path;
    final queryParams = uri.query

### Question
How do I use named routes in Flutter?

### Answer
**Named routes** in Flutter let you navigate between screens using string identifiers instead of pushing widgets directly. This is useful for larger apps.

### 1. Basic Setup (Recommended for Simple Apps)

Define your routes in the `MaterialApp` widget:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      title: 'Named Routes Demo',
      initialRoute: '/',                    // Starting route
      routes: {
        '/': (context) => const HomeScreen(),
        '/details': (context) => const DetailsScreen(),
        '/profile': (context) => const ProfileScreen(),
      },
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Navigating with Named Routes

```dart
// Push a new route
Navigator.pushNamed(context, '/details');

// Replace current route (useful for login flows)
Navigator.pushReplacementNamed(context, '/details');

// Clear all previous routes and go to new one
Navigator.pushNamedAndRemoveUntil(
  context, 
  '/home', 
  (route) => false,           // Remove all routes
);

// Go back
Navigator.pop(context);
```

### 3. Passing Arguments

#### Sending Arguments:

```dart
Navigator.pushNamed(
  context,
  '/details',
  arguments: {'id': 42, 'title': 'Flutter Tips'},
);
```

#### Receiving Arguments:

```dart
class DetailsScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  const DetailsScreen({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final args = ModalRoute.of(context)!.settings.arguments as Map<String, dynamic>;

    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text(args['title'])),
      body: Center(child: Text('ID: ${args['id']}')),
    );
  }
}
```

### 4. Using `onGenerateRoute` (More Powerful Approach)

When you need **dynamic routing**, argument validation, or dependency injection, use `onGenerateRoute`:

```dart
MaterialApp(
  initialRoute: '/',
  onGenerateRoute: (settings) {
    switch (settings.name) {
      case '/':
        return MaterialPageRoute(builder: (_) => const HomeScreen());
      
      case '/details':
        final args = settings.arguments as Map<String, dynamic>?;
        return MaterialPageRoute(
          builder: (_) => DetailsScreen(id: args?['id']),
        );
      
      default:
        return MaterialPageRoute(
          builder: (_) => const Scaffold(
            body: Center(child: Text('404 - Page not found')),
          ),
        );
    }
  },
)
```

### Best Practices

| Use Case                        | Recommended Approach       | Reason |
|--------------------------------|---------------------------|--------|
| Simple static routes           | `routes` map              | Clean and readable |
| Passing arguments             

### Question
How do I pop back to previous screen in Flutter?

### Answer
**To pop back to the previous screen in Flutter**, use `Navigator.pop()`.

### Basic Example

```dart
ElevatedButton(
  onPressed: () {
    Navigator.pop(context);
  },
  child: const Text('Go Back'),
)
```

### Full Working Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: FirstScreen());
  }
}

class FirstScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  const FirstScreen({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('First Screen')),
      body: Center(
        child: ElevatedButton(
          child: const Text('Go to Second Screen'),
          onPressed: () {
            Navigator.push(
              context,
              MaterialPageRoute(builder: (_) => const SecondScreen()),
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}

class SecondScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  const SecondScreen({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Second Screen')),
      body: Center(
        child: ElevatedButton(
          child: const Text('Go Back'),
          onPressed: () {
            Navigator.pop(context); // ← Pops back to FirstScreen
          },
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Variations

| Use Case                        | Code                                      | Description                              |
|--------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| Return data                    | `Navigator.pop(context, 'Some data');`    | Send result back to previous screen      |
| Check if can pop               | `if (Navigator.canPop(context)) ...`      | Prevent errors on root screen            |
| Pop until specific screen      | `Navigator.popUntil(context, ...)`        | Pop multiple screens                     |
| Replace current screen         | `Navigator.pushReplacement(...)`          | Don't keep current screen in stack       |

### Returning Data (with `await`)

```dart
final result = await Navigator.push(
  context,
  MaterialPageRoute(builder: (_) => const SecondScreen()),
);

print(result); // Data returned from pop
```

In `SecondScreen`:

```dart
Navigator.pop(context, 'Hello from Second Screen');
```

**Note**: `Navigator.pop()` requires a `BuildContext` that has a `Navigator` ancestor (usually from `MaterialApp` or `CupertinoApp`).

### Question
How do I replace current screen in Flutter?

### Answer
To replace the current screen (instead of pushing a new one on top of the navigation stack), use `Navigator.pushReplacement`.

### Basic Example

```dart
Navigator.pushReplacement(
  context,
  MaterialPageRoute(builder: (context) => const NewScreen()),
);
```

### With Named Routes (Recommended)

```dart
// In your MaterialApp
MaterialApp(
  routes: {
    '/home': (context) => const HomeScreen(),
    '/details': (context) => const DetailsScreen(),
  },
)

// Replace current screen
Navigator.pushReplacementNamed(context, '/details');
```

### Remove All Previous Screens (Optional)

If you also want to clear the entire back stack:

```dart
Navigator.pushAndRemoveUntil(
  context,
  MaterialPageRoute(builder: (context) => const NewScreen()),
  (route) => false, // removes all previous routes
);
```

**Note**: `pushReplacement` removes the current route from the stack, so the user won't be able to go back to it using the system back button.

### Question
What is Provider in Flutter?

### Answer
**Provider** is a state management library for Flutter that simplifies sharing and updating data across your widget tree. It’s essentially a wrapper around `InheritedWidget` that makes it easier to use, more performant, and less boilerplate-heavy.

It was created by the Flutter team (Remi Rousselet) and is still widely used, though many developers have now moved to **Riverpod** (its successor).

### Core Idea

Instead of passing data and callbacks down multiple levels of widgets (prop drilling), you "provide" a value (or object) at the top of the widget tree and then "consume" it wherever needed.

### Main Types of Providers

| Provider | Use Case | Rebuilds Widget? | Requires `notifyListeners()` |
|---------|----------|------------------|-----------------------------|
| `Provider` | Static values / services | No | No |
| `ChangeNotifierProvider` | Mutable state (most common) | Yes | Yes |
| `FutureProvider` | Async data from `Future` | Yes | - |
| `StreamProvider` | Async data from `Stream` | Yes | - |
| `MultiProvider` | Provide multiple values at once | - | - |

### Basic Example: Counter App

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:provider/provider.dart';

// 1. Create a model that extends ChangeNotifier
class Counter with ChangeNotifier {
  int _count = 0;
  int get count => _count;

  void increment() {
    _count++;
    notifyListeners(); // Important: tells listeners to rebuild
  }
}

void main() {
  runApp(
    // 2. Provide the model at the top of the widget tree
    ChangeNotifierProvider(
      create: (_) => Counter(),
      child: const MyApp(),
    ),
  );
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: const CounterPage(),
    );
  }
}

class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  const CounterPage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Provider Demo')),
      body: Center(
        child: Consumer<Counter>(
          builder: (context, counter, child) {
            return Text(
              '${counter.count}',
              style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 48),
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () {
          // 3. Access and update the model
          context.read<Counter>().increment();
        },
        child: const Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Alternative Ways to Read the Value

```dart
// Rebuilds when value changes
final counter = context.watch<Counter>();

// Does NOT rebuild (good for methods)


### Question
What is GetX in Flutter?

### Answer
GetX is a lightweight, high-performance state management solution for Flutter that also includes built-in support for **dependency injection**, **route management**, and other utilities. It’s popular because it minimizes boilerplate, doesn’t require `BuildContext` for most operations, and uses reactive programming with very little overhead.

### Core Features

- **State Management**: Reactive (`Rx`) and simple state updates.
- **Dependency Injection**: Easy service/controller registration and retrieval.
- **Navigation**: Context-free routing with named routes and transitions.
- **Internationalization** and other utilities (storage, logging, etc.).

### Basic Example: State Management

Here’s a typical GetX setup for managing and reacting to state:

```dart
import 'package:get/get.dart';

// Controller
class CounterController extends GetxController {
  var count = 0.obs; // Reactive variable

  void increment() => count++;
}

// UI
class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  final CounterController controller = Get.put(CounterController());

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text('GetX Counter')),
      body: Center(
        child: Obx(() => Text(
              'Count: ${controller.count}',
              style: TextStyle(fontSize: 24),
            )),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: controller.increment,
        child: Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Navigation Without Context

```dart
// Navigate to a new page
Get.to(() => SecondPage());

// Navigate and remove previous routes
Get.offAll(() => HomePage());

// Named routes
Get.toNamed('/details', arguments: {'id': 42});
```

### Dependency Injection

```dart
// Register once (usually in main or a binding)
Get.put(ApiService());

// Retrieve anywhere
final api = Get.find<ApiService>();
```

### When to Use GetX

Use GetX when you want:
- Minimal boilerplate compared to Provider or Riverpod.
- Fast reactive updates with `Obx`.
- Simple navigation and dependency management in the same package.

It’s especially useful in medium-sized apps where you want quick development without a steep learning curve.

Would you like examples for route management, GetX bindings, or how it compares to Riverpod?

### Question
What is Riverpod in Flutter?

### Answer
**Riverpod** is a modern, compile-safe state management library for Flutter, created by Rémi Rousselet (the author of Provider). It is designed to overcome many limitations of Provider while keeping a similar mental model.

### Why Riverpod?

| Feature                    | Provider                  | Riverpod                          |
|---------------------------|---------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| Compile-time safety       | No                        | Yes                               |
| No `BuildContext` needed  | No                        | Yes                               |
| Can be used outside widgets | Limited                 | Yes                               |
| Testing                   | Harder                    | Much easier                       |
| Multiple providers of same type | Error-prone         | Fully supported                   |
| Async handling            | Manual                    | Built-in (`FutureProvider`, `StreamProvider`) |

### Core Concepts

- **ProviderScope**: The root widget that holds all providers (similar to `MultiProvider`).
- **Providers**: Simple functions that expose state.
- **ConsumerWidget** / **Consumer**: Widgets that listen to providers.
- **ref**: Object used to read/watch providers.

### Basic Example: Counter App

**1. Add dependency**

```yaml
dependencies:
  flutter_riverpod: ^2.5.1
```

**2. Setup**

```dart
void main() {
  runApp(
    const ProviderScope(child: MyApp()),
  );
}
```

**3. Define a provider**

```dart
// counter_provider.dart
import 'package:flutter_riverpod/flutter_riverpod.dart';

final counterProvider = StateNotifierProvider<CounterNotifier, int>((ref) {
  return CounterNotifier();
});

class CounterNotifier extends StateNotifier<int> {
  CounterNotifier() : super(0);

  void increment() => state++;
  void decrement() => state--;
}
```

**4. Use the provider**

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:flutter_riverpod/flutter_riverpod.dart';
import 'counter_provider.dart';

class CounterPage extends ConsumerWidget {
  const CounterPage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context, WidgetRef ref) {
    // Watch the value
    final count = ref.watch(counterProvider);

    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Riverpod Counter')),
      body: Center(
        child: Text(
          '$count',
          style: Theme.of(context).textTheme.displayLarge,
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () => ref.read(counterProvider.notifier).increment(),
        child: const Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Other Common Providers

| Provider                  | Use Case                          | Example |
|--------------------------|-----------------------------------|-------|
| `Provider`               | Static values / services          | `final apiProvider = Provider((ref) => ApiService());` |
| `StateProvider`          | Simple mutable state              | `final countProvider = StateProvider((ref

### Question
What is BLoC pattern in Flutter?

### Answer
BLoC (Business Logic Component) is a **state management pattern** created by Google for Flutter. It separates business logic from the UI by using Dart `Stream`s, making the app more testable, maintainable, and scalable.

### Core Idea

- **UI** sends **events** (user actions)
- **BLoC** receives events, processes business logic, and emits **states**
- **UI** listens to state changes and rebuilds accordingly

This creates a unidirectional data flow:
```
UI → Events → BLoC → States → UI
```

### Simple BLoC Example (Without Package)

Here's a basic counter implementation using raw streams:

```dart
import 'dart:async';

class CounterBloc {
  int _count = 0;

  // Output stream (states)
  final _stateController = StreamController<int>.broadcast();
  Stream<int> get state => _stateController.stream;

  // Input sink (events)
  final _eventController = StreamController<void>();
  Sink<void> get increment => _eventController.sink;

  CounterBloc() {
    _eventController.stream.listen((_) {
      _count++;
      _stateController.add(_count);
    });
  }

  void dispose() {
    _stateController.close();
    _eventController.close();
  }
}
```

**Usage in UI:**

```dart
class CounterPage extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<CounterPage> createState() => _CounterPageState();
}

class _CounterPageState extends State<CounterPage> {
  final bloc = CounterBloc();

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: Center(
        child: StreamBuilder<int>(
          stream: bloc.state,
          initialData: 0,
          builder: (context, snapshot) {
            return Text(
              '${snapshot.data}',
              style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 48),
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () => bloc.increment.add(null),
        child: const Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    bloc.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }
}
```

### Recommended Approach: `bloc` Package

For real projects, use the official `bloc` + `flutter_bloc` packages. They provide better structure with `Event` and `State` classes.

**Add dependencies:**

```yaml
dependencies:
  flutter_bloc: ^8.1.3
```

**Example with `flutter_bloc`:**

```dart
// counter_event.dart
sealed class CounterEvent {}
class Increment extends CounterEvent {}

// counter_state.dart
class CounterState {
  final int count;
  const CounterState(this.count);
}

// counter_bloc.dart
class CounterBloc extends Bloc<CounterEvent, CounterState> {
  CounterBloc() : super(const CounterState(0)) {
    on<Increment

### Question
What is the difference between Provider and GetX?

### Answer
Provider and GetX are both state management solutions for Flutter, but they differ significantly in scope, architecture, and approach.

## Core Differences

| Aspect | Provider | GetX |
|--------|----------|------|
| **Scope** | State management only | Micro-framework (state + DI + routing + i18n) |
| **Pattern** | ChangeNotifier (InheritedWidget) | Reactive (Rx/Observables) |
| **Boilerplate** | Moderate | Minimal |
| **Rebuild scope** | All widgets under Consumer | Only specific widgets (`.obs` + `Obx`) |
| **Official** | Recommended by Flutter team | Community-driven |

## Code Comparison

### Provider (ChangeNotifier)
```dart
// Model
class Counter extends ChangeNotifier {
  int value = 0;
  
  void increment() {
    value++;
    notifyListeners();
  }
}

// Usage
class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: Center(
        child: Consumer<Counter>(
          builder: (context, counter, _) => Text('${counter.value}'),
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () => context.read<Counter>().increment(),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### GetX (Reactive)
```dart
// Controller
class CounterController extends GetxController {
  var count = 0.obs; // Observable
  
  void increment() => count.value++;
}

// Usage
class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  final controller = Get.put(CounterController());
  
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: Center(
        child: Obx(() => Text('${controller.count}')), // Only this rebuilds
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: controller.increment,
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

## Key Trade-offs

**Provider pros:**
- Explicit and testable
- Simpler mental model
- Better for large teams (less "magic")

**GetX pros:**
- Less boilerplate
- Built-in dependency injection and routing
- Fine-grained rebuilds (only `Obx` widgets update)
- `Get.find()` for accessing controllers anywhere without context

**GetX cons:**
- Can hide dependencies (harder to trace where state comes from)
- Less predictable performance characteristics
- Not officially supported by Flutter team

## When to Choose

- **Choose Provider** if you want official support, explicit dependencies, and are already using `package:provider`.
- **Choose GetX** if you want minimal code, need built-in routing/DI, and are comfortable with reactive patterns.

Many teams use Provider for state and GetIt for DI, or stick with GetX entirely for its all-in-one approach.

### Question
How do I use ChangeNotifier in Flutter?

### Answer
**ChangeNotifier** is Flutter's built-in class for simple state management. It allows objects to notify listeners when their state changes.

### 1. Basic Usage (Extending ChangeNotifier)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/foundation.dart';

class Counter extends ChangeNotifier {
  int _count = 0;
  int get count => _count;

  void increment() {
    _count++;
    notifyListeners(); // Notifies all listeners
  }

  void decrement() {
    _count--;
    notifyListeners();
  }
}
```

### 2. Using ChangeNotifier in Widgets

#### Option A: With `ListenableBuilder` (Recommended for simple cases)

```dart
class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  final Counter counter = Counter();

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text('ChangeNotifier Demo')),
      body: Center(
        child: ListenableBuilder(
          listenable: counter,
          builder: (context, child) {
            return Text(
              'Count: ${counter.count}',
              style: TextStyle(fontSize: 40),
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: counter.increment,
        child: Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

#### Option B: With `Provider` (Most Common Approach)

This is the recommended way for most apps:

```dart
// 1. Add dependency
// provider: ^6.1.1
```

```dart
import 'package:provider/provider.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(
    ChangeNotifierProvider(
      create: (_) => Counter(),
      child: MyApp(),
    ),
  );
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: CounterPage(),
    );
  }
}

class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text('Provider + ChangeNotifier')),
      body: Center(
        child: Consumer<Counter>(
          builder: (context, counter, child) {
            return Text(
              'Count: ${counter.count}',
              style: TextStyle(fontSize: 40),
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () => context.read<Counter>().increment(),
        child: Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Methods

| Method              | Purpose                              |
|---------------------|--------------------------------------|
| `notifyListeners()` | Notifies all listeners of changes    |
| `addListener()`     | Adds a listener (usually automatic)  |
| `removeListener()`  | Removes a listener                   |
| `dispose()`         | Cleans up when no longer needed      |

### Best Practices

- Always call

### Question
How do I use ValueNotifier in Flutter?

### Answer
**ValueNotifier** is a simple, lightweight state management solution in Flutter for holding a single value and notifying listeners when it changes. It’s ideal for basic reactive UI updates without needing a full state management package.

### Basic Usage

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

final ValueNotifier<int> counter = ValueNotifier<int>(0);
```

### Recommended Way: `ValueListenableBuilder`

Use `ValueListenableBuilder` to rebuild only the parts of the UI that depend on the value:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: const CounterPage(),
    );
  }
}

class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  const CounterPage({super.key});

  // Can be defined here or in a separate file/service
  static final ValueNotifier<int> counter = ValueNotifier<int>(0);

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('ValueNotifier Example')),
      body: Center(
        child: ValueListenableBuilder<int>(
          valueListenable: counter,
          builder: (context, value, child) {
            return Text(
              'Count: $value',
              style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 32),
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () => counter.value++, // Triggers rebuild
        child: const Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points

- **Updating the value**: Just assign to `.value`:
  ```dart
  counter.value = 10;
  counter.value++;           // Works for int, bool, etc.
  ```
- **Only the `ValueListenableBuilder` rebuilds** — not the whole widget tree.
- You can have multiple `ValueListenableBuilder`s listening to the same notifier.
- **No need to call `notifyListeners()` manually** — setting `.value` does it automatically.

### Listening Manually (Less Common)

If you need to react to changes without rebuilding a widget:

```dart
void initState() {
  super.initState();
  counter.addListener(() {
    print('Counter changed: ${counter.value}');
  });
}

void dispose() {
  counter.removeListener(/*...*/);
  super.dispose();
}
```

### Best Practices

| Use Case                        | Recommendation                     |
|--------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| Simple value (count, bool, etc.) | `ValueNotifier` + `ValueListenableBuilder` |
| Multiple related values        | Use multiple notifiers or switch to `ChangeNotifier` |
| App-wide state                 | Consider Riverpod, Provider, or Bloc |
| Need to dispose notifier       | Usually not required for simple use cases

### Question
How do I use StreamController in Flutter?

### Answer
**StreamController** is a core class from `dart:async` that lets you manually create and control a `Stream`. It's commonly used for state management, event handling, or bridging non-stream APIs to streams.

### Basic Usage

```dart
import 'dart:async';

void main() {
  // 1. Create a controller
  final controller = StreamController<int>();

  // 2. Listen to the stream
  controller.stream.listen(
    (data) => print('Received: $data'),
    onError: (error) => print('Error: $error'),
    onDone: () => print('Stream closed'),
  );

  // 3. Add data
  controller.add(1);
  controller.add(2);
  controller.addError(Exception('Something went wrong'));
  controller.add(3);

  // 4. Close when done (very important!)
  controller.close();
}
```

### Recommended Pattern in Flutter

Use a class to encapsulate the controller:

```dart
import 'dart:async';
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class CounterService {
  final _controller = StreamController<int>.broadcast(); // broadcast = multiple listeners

  Stream<int> get stream => _controller.stream;

  int _count = 0;

  void increment() {
    _count++;
    _controller.add(_count);
  }

  void dispose() {
    _controller.close();
  }
}
```

### Using with `StreamBuilder`

```dart
class CounterPage extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<CounterPage> createState() => _CounterPageState();
}

class _CounterPageState extends State<CounterPage> {
  final CounterService _service = CounterService();

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _service.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('StreamController Demo')),
      body: Center(
        child: StreamBuilder<int>(
          stream: _service.stream,
          initialData: 0,
          builder: (context, snapshot) {
            return Text(
              'Count: ${snapshot.data}',
              style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 40),
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: _service.increment,
        child: const Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points

| Feature                    | Code                              | Notes |
|---------------------------|-----------------------------------|-------|
| **Single listener**       | `StreamController<int>()`         | Default behavior |
| **Multiple listeners**    | `StreamController<int>.broadcast()` | Required for `StreamBuilder` + other listeners |
| **Add data**              | `controller.add(value)`           | Preferred over `.sink.add()` |
| **Add error**             | `controller.addError(error)`      | Triggers `

### Question
What is Cubit in Flutter BLoC?

### Answer
**Cubit** is a simpler state management solution provided by the `bloc` package (used alongside `flutter_bloc`). It is essentially a **lightweight version of Bloc** that removes the concept of events.

### Bloc vs Cubit

| Feature       | **Bloc**                          | **Cubit**                          |
|---------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| Events        | Yes (Event classes)               | No                                 |
| State changes | Triggered via `add(Event)`        | Triggered via method calls         |
| Complexity    | Higher (good for complex flows)   | Lower (simpler and more direct)    |
| Use case      | Complex business logic            | Simple to medium logic             |

### What is a Cubit?

A `Cubit` is a class that:
- Extends `Cubit<State>`
- Holds a single state
- Exposes **methods** that can `emit()` new states
- Does **not** use events

### Basic Example: Counter Cubit

```dart
import 'package:bloc/bloc.dart';

// Define the Cubit
class CounterCubit extends Cubit<int> {
  CounterCubit() : super(0); // Initial state

  void increment() => emit(state + 1);
  void decrement() => emit(state - 1);
  void reset() => emit(0);
}
```

### Using Cubit in Flutter (with `BlocBuilder`)

```dart
class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return BlocProvider(
      create: (_) => CounterCubit(),
      child: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Cubit Counter')),
        body: Center(
          child: BlocBuilder<CounterCubit, int>(
            builder: (context, count) {
              return Text(
                '$count',
                style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 48),
              );
            },
          ),
        ),
        floatingActionButton: Column(
          mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.end,
          children: [
            FloatingActionButton(
              onPressed: () => context.read<CounterCubit>().increment(),
              child: const Icon(Icons.add),
            ),
            const SizedBox(height: 8),
            FloatingActionButton(
              onPressed: () => context.read<CounterCubit>().decrement(),
              child: const Icon(Icons.remove),
            ),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### When to Use Cubit?

Use **Cubit** when:
- Your logic is relatively simple
- You don’t need event tracking or complex event-to-state mapping
- You want less boilerplate

Use **Bloc** when:
- You have complex business logic
- You need to handle multiple types of events
- You want better traceability (events can be logged)

### Summary

> **Cubit** = Bloc without events.  
> It allows you to

### Question
How do I use context.watch in Flutter?

### Answer
**`context.watch<T>()`** is a method from the [provider](https://pub.dev/packages/provider) package that lets you **listen** to a value and rebuild the widget when that value changes.

### Basic Usage

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:provider/provider.dart';

// 1. Create a model that extends ChangeNotifier
class Counter extends ChangeNotifier {
  int count = 0;

  void increment() {
    count++;
    notifyListeners();
  }
}

void main() {
  runApp(
    ChangeNotifierProvider(
      create: (_) => Counter(),
      child: const MyApp(),
    ),
  );
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('context.watch Example')),
        body: const CounterPage(),
      ),
    );
  }
}

class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  const CounterPage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    // 2. Use context.watch to listen and rebuild
    final counter = context.watch<Counter>();

    return Center(
      child: Column(
        mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
        children: [
          Text(
            '${counter.count}',
            style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 48),
          ),
          const SizedBox(height: 20),
          ElevatedButton(
            onPressed: () => context.read<Counter>().increment(),
            child: const Text('Increment'),
          ),
        ],
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points

| Method            | Rebuilds widget? | Use case                              |
|-------------------|------------------|---------------------------------------|
| `context.watch<T>()`  | Yes              | When you need the value and want to rebuild |
| `context.read<T>()`   | No               | Calling methods (like `increment()`)  |
| `context.select<T, R>()` | Only when selected value changes | Performance optimization |

### Example with `select` (Recommended for performance)

```dart
final count = context.select<Counter, int>((counter) => counter.count);
```

This only rebuilds when `count` changes, not when other properties in `Counter` change.

### Common Pattern

```dart
@override
Widget build(BuildContext context) {
  final user = context.watch<User>();
  final theme = context.watch<ThemeModel>();

  return Text(user.name);
}
```

**Note**: You can only call `context.watch` inside the `build` method (or methods called from `build`). Using it inside event handlers or `initState` will throw an error.

### Question
What is Dart async/await?

### Answer
Dart's `async`/`await` is syntactic sugar built on top of Dart's `Future` API. It lets you write asynchronous code that looks and behaves like synchronous code, while still being non-blocking.

### Core Rules

- A function marked with `async` **always returns a `Future`**.
- `await` pauses execution of the current async function until the `Future` completes.
- You can only use `await` inside a function marked `async`.
- Exceptions thrown inside an async function are automatically wrapped in the returned `Future` (you can catch them with `try`/`catch`).

### Basic Example

```dart
Future<String> fetchUserName() async {
  // Simulating network delay
  await Future.delayed(const Duration(seconds: 2));
  return 'John Doe';
}

void main() async {
  print('Start');
  
  final name = await fetchUserName();   // Waits here for 2 seconds
  print('Hello, $name');
  
  print('End');
}
```

**Output:**
```
Start
Hello, John Doe
End
```

### Error Handling

```dart
Future<int> fetchUserAge() async {
  await Future.delayed(const Duration(seconds: 1));
  throw Exception('Failed to fetch age');
}

void main() async {
  try {
    final age = await fetchUserAge();
    print('Age: $age');
  } catch (e) {
    print('Error: $e');
  }
}
```

### Real Flutter Example (HTTP Request)

```dart
import 'package:http/http.dart' as http;
import 'dart:convert';

class ApiService {
  Future<Map<String, dynamic>> fetchUser(int id) async {
    final response = await http.get(
      Uri.parse('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users/$id'),
    );

    if (response.statusCode == 200) {
      return jsonDecode(response.body);
    } else {
      throw Exception('Failed to load user');
    }
  }
}

// Usage in a widget
class UserScreen extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<UserScreen> createState() => _UserScreenState();
}

class _UserScreenState extends State<UserScreen> {
  Map<String, dynamic>? user;
  bool isLoading = true;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    loadUser();
  }

  Future<void> loadUser() async {
    try {
      final data = await ApiService().fetchUser(1);
      setState(() {
        user = data;
        isLoading = false;
      });
    } catch (e) {
      setState(() => isLoading = false);
      // Handle error (show snackbar, etc.)
    }
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    // ...
  }
}
```

### Key Takeaways

- `async` functions return `Future

### Question
What is a Future in Dart?

### Answer
A `Future<T>` in Dart represents a **value (or error) that will be available at some point in the future**. It's Dart's core abstraction for asynchronous programming.

### Why Futures Exist

Many operations in Flutter (network requests, database access, file I/O, timers, etc.) take time. Instead of blocking the main thread, these operations return a `Future` immediately. You can later react when the result is ready.

### Basic Concepts

- `Future<T>` → will eventually complete with a value of type `T`
- `Future<void>` → completes with no value (just signals completion)
- A `Future` can complete **successfully** or with an **error**

### Code Examples

#### 1. Creating a Future

```dart
Future<String> fetchUserName() {
  return Future.delayed(const Duration(seconds: 2), () => 'John Doe');
}
```

#### 2. Consuming a Future (Recommended: `async/await`)

```dart
Future<void> loadUser() async {
  try {
    final name = await fetchUserName();
    print('User: $name');
  } catch (e) {
    print('Error: $e');
  }
}
```

#### 3. Using `.then()` and `.catchError()` (less preferred)

```dart
fetchUserName()
    .then((name) => print('User: $name'))
    .catchError((error) => print('Error: $error'));
```

#### 4. Real Flutter Example (HTTP Request)

```dart
Future<User> fetchUser(int id) async {
  final response = await http.get(
    Uri.parse('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users/$id'),
  );

  if (response.statusCode == 200) {
    return User.fromJson(jsonDecode(response.body));
  } else {
    throw Exception('Failed to load user');
  }
}
```

Usage in a widget:

```dart
class UserScreen extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<UserScreen> createState() => _UserScreenState();
}

class _UserScreenState extends State<UserScreen> {
  late Future<User> userFuture;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    userFuture = fetchUser(1);
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return FutureBuilder<User>(
      future: userFuture,
      builder: (context, snapshot) {
        if (snapshot.connectionState == ConnectionState.waiting) {
          return const CircularProgressIndicator();
        }
        if (snapshot.hasError) {
          return Text('Error: ${snapshot.error}');
        }
        return Text('Name: ${snapshot.data!.name}');
      },
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Rules

- Always use `async/await` when possible — it's more readable.
- Handle errors (either with `try/catch` or `.

### Question
What is a Stream in Dart?

### Answer
A **Stream** in Dart is an asynchronous sequence of data or events. Unlike a `Future`, which returns a single value (or error) at some point in the future, a `Stream` can emit multiple values over time.

### Key Characteristics

- **Asynchronous data flow**: Data arrives over time (e.g., user input, network responses, sensor data).
- **Two types**:
  - **Single-subscription streams**: Only one listener allowed (default).
  - **Broadcast streams**: Multiple listeners allowed (created via `StreamController.broadcast()`).

### Basic Example

```dart
import 'dart:async';

void main() async {
  // Creating a stream that emits numbers every second
  Stream<int> numberStream = Stream.periodic(
    Duration(seconds: 1),
    (count) => count,
  ).take(5); // Limit to 5 events

  // Listening to the stream
  await for (int number in numberStream) {
    print('Received: $number');
  }

  print('Stream completed');
}
```

### Using StreamController (Most Common Pattern)

```dart
class CounterService {
  final _controller = StreamController<int>();
  int _count = 0;

  Stream<int> get stream => _controller.stream;

  void increment() {
    _count++;
    _controller.add(_count); // Emit new value
  }

  void dispose() {
    _controller.close();
  }
}

// Usage in Flutter
class CounterWidget extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<CounterWidget> createState() => _CounterWidgetState();
}

class _CounterWidgetState extends State<CounterWidget> {
  final CounterService _service = CounterService();

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return StreamBuilder<int>(
      stream: _service.stream,
      builder: (context, snapshot) {
        if (!snapshot.hasData) return CircularProgressIndicator();
        return Text('Count: ${snapshot.data}');
      },
    );
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _service.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }
}
```

### Common Stream Methods

```dart
stream
  .where((value) => value > 5)           // Filter
  .map((value) => value * 2)             // Transform
  .distinct()                            // Remove duplicates
  .listen((value) => print(value));      // Subscribe
```

**In Flutter**, `StreamBuilder` is the standard widget for reacting to stream updates in the UI.

### Question
How do I use FutureBuilder in Flutter?

### Answer
**FutureBuilder** is a Flutter widget that builds itself based on the latest snapshot of a `Future`. It's commonly used to handle asynchronous operations like API calls, database queries, or file I/O.

### Basic Usage

```dart
FutureBuilder<T>(
  future: myFuture,           // The Future to listen to
