  initialData: null,          // Optional initial data
  builder: (context, snapshot) {
    // Build UI based on snapshot state
  },
)
```

### Recommended Pattern (Avoid Rebuilding Issues)

**Never** create the `Future` directly inside the `build` method. This causes the future to restart on every rebuild.

Instead, store the future in a `StatefulWidget`:

```dart
class MyScreen extends StatefulWidget {
  const MyScreen({super.key});

  @override
  State<MyScreen> createState() => _MyScreenState();
}

class _MyScreenState extends State<MyScreen> {
  late Future<String> _dataFuture;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _dataFuture = fetchData(); // Initialize once
  }

  Future<String> fetchData() async {
    await Future.delayed(const Duration(seconds: 2));
    // Simulate API call
    return 'Hello from Future!';
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('FutureBuilder Example')),
      body: Center(
        child: FutureBuilder<String>(
          future: _dataFuture,
          builder: (context, snapshot) {
            if (snapshot.connectionState == ConnectionState.waiting) {
              return const CircularProgressIndicator();
            }

            if (snapshot.hasError) {
              return Text('Error: ${snapshot.error}');
            }

            if (snapshot.hasData) {
              return Text(
                snapshot.data!,
                style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 24),
              );
            }

            return const Text('No data available');
          },
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Handling All Connection States

Here's a more complete version that covers all states clearly:

```dart
builder: (context, snapshot) {
  switch (snapshot.connectionState) {
    case ConnectionState.none:
      return const Text('Future not started');
    case ConnectionState.waiting:
      return const CircularProgressIndicator();
    case ConnectionState.active:
      return const Text('Active (rare with Future)');
    case ConnectionState.done:
      if (snapshot.hasError) {
        return Text('Error: ${snapshot.error}');
      }
      return Text('Data: ${snapshot.data}');
  }
}
```

### Key Tips

- **Use `snapshot.hasData`** instead of checking `snapshot.data != null` in most cases.
- Always handle **error states** (`snapshot.hasError`).
- If you need to refresh the data, just call `setState`

### Question
How do I use StreamBuilder in Flutter?

### Answer
**StreamBuilder** is a Flutter widget that listens to a `Stream` and rebuilds its UI whenever new data is emitted.

### Basic Usage

```dart
StreamBuilder<T>(
  stream: yourStream,
  initialData: initialValue, // optional
  builder: (BuildContext context, AsyncSnapshot<T> snapshot) {
    // Return widget based on snapshot
  },
)
```

### Complete Working Example

Here's a practical example using a `StreamController`:

```dart
import 'dart:async';
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: StreamDemo());
  }
}

class StreamDemo extends StatefulWidget {
  const StreamDemo({super.key});

  @override
  State<StreamDemo> createState() => _StreamDemoState();
}

class _StreamDemoState extends State<StreamDemo> {
  final StreamController<int> _controller = StreamController<int>();
  int _count = 0;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    // Emit a value every second
    Timer.periodic(const Duration(seconds: 1), (_) {
      _count++;
      _controller.add(_count);
    });
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _controller.close(); // Always close the controller
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('StreamBuilder Example')),
      body: Center(
        child: StreamBuilder<int>(
          stream: _controller.stream,
          builder: (context, snapshot) {
            if (snapshot.hasError) {
              return Text('Error: ${snapshot.error}');
            }

            switch (snapshot.connectionState) {
              case ConnectionState.none:
                return const Text('No stream');
              case ConnectionState.waiting:
                return const CircularProgressIndicator();
              case ConnectionState.active:
              case ConnectionState.done:
                return Text(
                  'Count: ${snapshot.data}',
                  style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 32),
                );
            }
          },
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Handling Different States (Recommended Pattern)

```dart
builder: (context, snapshot) {
  if (snapshot.connectionState == ConnectionState.waiting) {
    return const CircularProgressIndicator();
  }

  if (snapshot.hasError) {
    return Text('Error: ${snapshot.error}');
  }

  if (!snapshot.hasData) {
    return const Text('No data');
  }

  // Safe to use snapshot.data here
  return Text('Data: ${snapshot.data}');
}
```

### Key Points

- Always close `StreamController` in `dispose()`.
- Use `snapshot.connectionState` to

### Question
How do I handle errors in Future in Dart?

### Answer
**Handling Errors in `Future` in Dart/Flutter**

Dart provides two main ways to handle errors in `Future`s:

1. **`async/await` + `try-catch`** (Recommended)
2. **`.then()` + `.catchError()`**

---

### 1. Using `async/await` + `try-catch` (Best Practice)

This is the cleanest and most readable approach.

```dart
Future<String> fetchUserData() async {
  try {
    final response = await http.get(Uri.parse('https://api.example.com/user'));
    
    if (response.statusCode == 200) {
      return response.body;
    } else {
      throw Exception('Failed to load user: ${response.statusCode}');
    }
  } catch (e) {
    print('Error occurred: $e');
    rethrow; // Optional: rethrow if you want the caller to handle it
  }
}
```

**Usage:**

```dart
void loadData() async {
  try {
    final data = await fetchUserData();
    print('Success: $data');
  } catch (e) {
    print('Handled error: $e');
  }
}
```

---

### 2. Using `.catchError()`

```dart
fetchUserData()
  .then((data) {
    print('Success: $data');
  })
  .catchError((error) {
    print('Error: $error');
  });
```

You can also handle specific error types:

```dart
fetchUserData().catchError((error, stackTrace) {
  if (error is TimeoutException) {
    print('Request timed out');
  } else {
    print('Unknown error: $error');
  }
}, test: (error) => error is Exception);
```

---

### 3. Error Handling in `FutureBuilder` (Flutter)

```dart
FutureBuilder<String>(
  future: fetchUserData(),
  builder: (context, snapshot) {
    if (snapshot.connectionState == ConnectionState.waiting) {
      return const CircularProgressIndicator();
    }

    if (snapshot.hasError) {
      return Center(
        child: Text('Error: ${snapshot.error}'),
      );
    }

    if (snapshot.hasData) {
      return Text(snapshot.data!);
    }

    return const Text('No data');
  },
)
```

---

### 4. Best Practices

| Practice | Recommendation |
|---------|----------------|
| **Prefer** | `try-catch` with `async/await` |
| **Always** | Use `rethrow` if you want to propagate the error |
| **Avoid** | Swallowing errors silently |
| **Use** | Custom exceptions for better error handling |
| **Log** | Use `stackTrace` when available |

**Example with custom exception:**

```dart
class ApiException implements Exception {
  final String message;
  ApiException(this.message);
  
  @override
  String to

### Question
How do I use isolates in Dart?

### Answer
**Isolates** in Dart are independent workers with their own memory. They communicate only through message passing (no shared memory).

### 1. Simple One-Way Communication

```dart
import 'dart:isolate';

void main() async {
  final receivePort = ReceivePort();

  await Isolate.spawn(_isolateEntry, receivePort.sendPort);

  // Listen for messages from the isolate
  receivePort.listen((message) {
    print('Main received: $message');
    receivePort.close();
  });
}

void _isolateEntry(SendPort sendPort) {
  // Heavy computation here
  sendPort.send('Hello from isolate!');
}
```

### 2. Two-Way Communication (Recommended Pattern)

```dart
import 'dart:isolate';

Future<void> main() async {
  final result = await runInIsolate(1000000000);
  print('Result: $result');
}

Future<int> runInIsolate(int n) async {
  final receivePort = ReceivePort();
  await Isolate.spawn(_heavyTask, _IsolateMessage(n, receivePort.sendPort));
  
  return await receivePort.first as int;
}

class _IsolateMessage {
  final int value;
  final SendPort sendPort;
  _IsolateMessage(this.value, this.sendPort);
}

void _heavyTask(_IsolateMessage message) {
  int sum = 0;
  for (int i = 0; i < message.value; i++) {
    sum += i;
  }
  message.sendPort.send(sum);
}
```

### 3. Using `compute()` (Flutter Recommended)

For simple one-off tasks, use `compute` from `package:flutter/foundation.dart`:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/foundation.dart';

Future<int> calculateSum(int n) async {
  return await compute(_sumTask, n);
}

int _sumTask(int n) {
  int sum = 0;
  for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    sum += i;
  }
  return sum;
}
```

### Key Points

- **Use isolates** for CPU-intensive work (JSON parsing, image processing, heavy loops).
- **Don't use isolates** for I/O operations (use `async/await` instead).
- `compute()` is just a convenience wrapper around `Isolate.spawn`.
- Always close `ReceivePort` when done to avoid memory leaks.
- In Flutter, heavy work in isolates keeps the UI smooth.

Would you like an example with error handling or using `Isolate.run` (Dart 2.19+)?

### Question
What is compute() in Flutter?

### Answer
`compute()` is a Flutter utility function that runs an expensive computation in a separate **isolate** (a separate Dart thread) to prevent blocking the main UI thread, which would cause jank or dropped frames.

## Basic Concept

```dart
Future<R> compute<Q, R>(
  ComputeCallback<Q, R> callback, 
  Q message, 
  {String? debugLabel}
)
```

It takes a top-level or static function and runs it in a background isolate, then returns the result to the main isolate.

## Practical Example

**Without `compute()` (blocks UI):**
```dart
// Heavy computation on main thread = jank
String parseLargeJson(String jsonString) {
  // Expensive parsing logic
  final data = jsonDecode(jsonString);
  // ... heavy processing ...
  return processedResult;
}
```

**With `compute()` (non-blocking):**
```dart
import 'dart:convert';
import 'package:flutter/foundation.dart';

// Must be a top-level or static function
String _parseJson(String jsonString) {
  final List<dynamic> data = jsonDecode(jsonString);
  // Heavy processing...
  return data.map((e) => e.toString()).join(', ');
}

class MyWidget extends StatelessWidget {
  Future<void> loadData() async {
    final jsonString = await fetchLargeJson();
    
    // Runs in background isolate
    final result = await compute(_parseJson, jsonString);
    
    print(result);
  }
}
```

## Key Rules & Limitations

1. **Must be top-level or static** — Closures/lambdas won't work because the function must be serializable to send to another isolate.
2. **Data must be sendable** — Arguments and return values must be primitives or objects that can be serialized via Dart's messaging protocol (no complex objects with methods unless using packages like `isolator` or `worker_manager`).
3. **Overhead** — Spawning an isolate has cost, so only use `compute()` for truly expensive operations (parsing large JSON, image processing, cryptography, etc.).

## When to Use

- Parsing large JSON/XML
- Image filtering/processing
- Cryptographic operations
- Heavy mathematical calculations
- Database batch operations

For more complex scenarios requiring persistent isolates or object passing, consider using `Isolate.spawn()` directly or packages like `flutter_isolate`.

### Question
How do I make HTTP requests in Flutter?

### Answer
**Making HTTP Requests in Flutter**

The recommended way is to use the official `http` package.

### 1. Add the Dependency

```yaml
# pubspec.yaml
dependencies:
  http: ^1.2.0
```

Run `flutter pub get`.

### 2. Basic Example (GET + POST)

```dart
import 'dart:convert';
import 'package:http/http.dart' as http;

class ApiService {
  final String _baseUrl = 'https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com';

  // GET Request
  Future<List<Post>> getPosts() async {
    final response = await http.get(
      Uri.parse('$_baseUrl/posts'),
      headers: {'Accept': 'application/json'},
    );

    if (response.statusCode == 200) {
      final List<dynamic> jsonList = jsonDecode(response.body);
      return jsonList.map((json) => Post.fromJson(json)).toList();
    } else {
      throw Exception('Failed to load posts: ${response.statusCode}');
    }
  }

  // POST Request
  Future<Post> createPost(Post post) async {
    final response = await http.post(
      Uri.parse('$_baseUrl/posts'),
      headers: {
        'Content-Type': 'application/json; charset=UTF-8',
      },
      body: jsonEncode(post.toJson()),
    );

    if (response.statusCode == 201) {
      return Post.fromJson(jsonDecode(response.body));
    } else {
      throw Exception('Failed to create post: ${response.statusCode}');
    }
  }
}

// Simple model
class Post {
  final int? id;
  final int userId;
  final String title;
  final String body;

  Post({this.id, required this.userId, required this.title, required this.body});

  factory Post.fromJson(Map<String, dynamic> json) {
    return Post(
      id: json['id'],
      userId: json['userId'],
      title: json['title'],
      body: json['body'],
    );
  }

  Map<String, dynamic> toJson() => {
        'userId': userId,
        'title': title,
        'body': body,
      };
}
```

### 3. Usage in a Widget (with `FutureBuilder`)

```dart

  State<PostsScreen> createState() => _PostsScreenState();

    return FutureBuilder<List<Post>>(
      future: _apiService.getPosts(),
      builder: (context, snapshot) {
        if (snapshot.connectionState == ConnectionState.waiting) {
          return const Center(child: CircularProgressIndicator());
        }
        
        if (snapshot.has

### Question
How do I use dio package in Flutter?

### Answer
**How to Use the `dio` Package in Flutter**

`dio` is a powerful HTTP client for Dart/Flutter that supports interceptors, global config, file uploads, timeouts, and cancellation.

### 1. Installation

Add `dio` to your `pubspec.yaml`:

```yaml
dependencies:
  dio: ^5.4.0
```

Then run:

```bash
flutter pub get
```

### 2. Basic Usage

```dart
import 'package:dio/dio.dart';

final dio = Dio();

Future<void> fetchData() async {
  try {
    final response = await dio.get('https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/posts/1');
    print(response.data);
  } on DioException catch (e) {
    print('Error: ${e.message}');
  }
}
```

### 3. Recommended Setup (Best Practice)

Create a centralized Dio instance with base configuration:

```dart
import 'package:dio/dio.dart';

class ApiService {
  final Dio _dio;

  ApiService()
      : _dio = Dio(
          BaseOptions(
            baseUrl: 'https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com',
            connectTimeout: const Duration(seconds: 10),
            receiveTimeout: const Duration(seconds: 10),
            headers: {'Content-Type': 'application/json'},
          ),
        ) {
    // Add interceptors
    _dio.interceptors.add(LogInterceptor(responseBody: true));
    _dio.interceptors.add(AuthInterceptor());
  }

  Future<Response> get(String path, {Map<String, dynamic>? queryParameters}) async {
    return await _dio.get(path, queryParameters: queryParameters);
  }

  Future<Response> post(String path, {dynamic data}) async {
    return await _dio.post(path, data: data);
  }
}

// Example Auth Interceptor
class AuthInterceptor extends Interceptor {
  @override
  void onRequest(RequestOptions options, RequestInterceptorHandler handler) {
    options.headers['Authorization'] = 'Bearer YOUR_TOKEN';
    handler.next(options);
  }
}
```

### 4. Usage Example

```dart
final api = ApiService();

Future<void> getPost() async {
  try {
    final res = await api.get('/posts/1');
    print(res.data);
  } on DioException catch (e) {
    if (e.response != null) {
      print('Status: ${e.response?.statusCode}');
    } else {
      print('Network error: ${e.message}');
    }
  }
}
```

  'userId': 1,
});

### Question
How do I parse JSON in Flutter?

### Answer
**Parsing JSON in Flutter** is straightforward using Dart’s built-in `dart:convert` library.

### 1. Basic JSON Parsing (Map)

```dart
import 'dart:convert';

void main() {
  String jsonString = '{"name": "John Doe", "age": 30, "isAdmin": false}';

  Map<String, dynamic> data = jsonDecode(jsonString);

  print(data['name']);    // John Doe
  print(data['age']);     // 30
}
```

### 2. Recommended Approach: Model Class + `fromJson`

For real apps, always create a model class:

```dart
class User {
  final String name;
  final int age;
  final bool isAdmin;

  const User({
    required this.name,
    required this.age,
    required this.isAdmin,
  });

  factory User.fromJson(Map<String, dynamic> json) {
    return User(
      name: json['name'] as String,
      age: json['age'] as int,
      isAdmin: json['isAdmin'] as bool,
    );
  }

  Map<String, dynamic> toJson() => {
        'name': name,
        'age': age,
        'isAdmin': isAdmin,
      };
}
```

**Usage:**

```dart
String jsonString = '{"name": "John Doe", "age": 30, "isAdmin": true}';

Map<String, dynamic> jsonMap = jsonDecode(jsonString);
User user = User.fromJson(jsonMap);

print(user.name); // John Doe
```

### 3. Parsing a List of Objects

```dart
String jsonList = '''
[
  {"name": "Alice", "age": 25, "isAdmin": false},
  {"name": "Bob", "age": 32, "isAdmin": true}
]
''';

List<dynamic> decoded = jsonDecode(jsonList);
List<User> users = decoded.map((json) => User.fromJson(json)).toList();
```

### 4. Production Approach (Recommended)

For larger projects, use **code generation** with `json_serializable`:

```yaml
dependencies:
  json_annotation: ^4.9.0

dev_dependencies:
  build_runner: ^2.4.9
  json_serializable: ^6.8.0
```

Then annotate your model:

```dart
import 'package:json_annotation/json_annotation.dart';

part 'user.g.dart';

@JsonSerializable()
class User {
  final String name;
  final int age;
  final bool isAdmin;

  const User({required this.name, required this.age, required this.isAdmin});

  factory User.fromJson(Map<String, dynamic> json) => _$UserFromJson(json);
  Map<String, dynamic> toJson() => _$UserToJson(this);
}
```

Generate the code with:

```bash
dart run build_runner build
```

### Question
How do I handle API errors in Flutter?

### Answer
**Handling API Errors in Flutter (Best Practices)**

Here's a clean, production-ready approach to handling API errors in Flutter.

### 1. Create Custom Exception Classes

```dart
// exceptions/api_exception.dart
class ApiException implements Exception {
  final String message;
  final int? statusCode;
  final dynamic data;

  ApiException(this.message, {this.statusCode, this.data});

  @override
  String toString() => 'ApiException: $message (Status: $statusCode)';
}

class NetworkException extends ApiException {
  NetworkException() : super('No internet connection');
}

class TimeoutException extends ApiException {
  TimeoutException() : super('Request timed out');
}
```

### 2. API Service with Proper Error Handling

Using the `http` package:

```dart
import 'dart:convert';
import 'dart:io';
import 'package:http/http.dart' as http;

class ApiService {
  static const String _baseUrl = 'https://api.example.com';

  Future<T> _handleRequest<T>(
    Future<http.Response> request,
    T Function(dynamic json) parser,
  ) async {
    try {
      final response = await request.timeout(const Duration(seconds: 15));

      if (response.statusCode >= 200 && response.statusCode < 300) {
        final data = jsonDecode(response.body);
        return parser(data);
      } else {
        throw ApiException(
          _getErrorMessage(response.statusCode),
          statusCode: response.statusCode,
          data: response.body,
        );
      }
    } on SocketException {
      throw NetworkException();
    } on http.ClientException {
      throw NetworkException();
    } on FormatException {
      throw ApiException('Invalid response format');
    } catch (e) {
      if (e is ApiException) rethrow;
      throw ApiException('Unexpected error: ${e.toString()}');
    }
  }

  String _getErrorMessage(int statusCode) {
    switch (statusCode) {
      case 400: return 'Bad request';
      case 401: return 'Unauthorized';
      case 403: return 'Forbidden';
      case 404: return 'Not found';
      case 500: return 'Server error';
      default: return 'Something went wrong';
    }
  }

  Future<User> getUser(int id) async {
    final response = http.get(Uri.parse('$_baseUrl/users/$id'));
    return _handleRequest(response, (json) => User.fromJson(json));
  }
}
```

### 3. Usage in UI Layer

```dart
class UserRepository {
  final ApiService _apiService;

  UserRepository(this._apiService);

  Future<User> fetchUser(int id) async {
    try {
      return await _apiService.getUser(id);
    } on ApiException catch (e) {
      // Log error here if needed
      rethrow; // Let UI decide

### Question
How do I add headers to HTTP requests in Flutter?

### Answer
**Adding Headers to HTTP Requests in Flutter**

The most common way is using the `http` package.

### 1. Add Dependency

```yaml
dependencies:
  http: ^1.2.2
```

### 2. Basic Example (GET & POST)

```dart
import 'dart:convert';
import 'package:http/http.dart' as http;

class ApiService {
  static const String baseUrl = 'https://api.example.com';

  // Common headers (e.g., auth token)
  static Map<String, String> get _headers => {
        'Content-Type': 'application/json',
        'Accept': 'application/json',
        'Authorization': 'Bearer YOUR_TOKEN_HERE',
      };

  // GET request with headers
  static Future<dynamic> get(String endpoint) async {
    final response = await http.get(
      Uri.parse('$baseUrl$endpoint'),
      headers: _headers,
    );

    return _handleResponse(response);
  }

  // POST request with headers
  static Future<dynamic> post(String endpoint, Map<String, dynamic> body) async {
    final response = await http.post(
      Uri.parse('$baseUrl$endpoint'),
      headers: _headers,
      body: jsonEncode(body),
    );

    return _handleResponse(response);
  }

  static dynamic _handleResponse(http.Response response) {
    if (response.statusCode >= 200 && response.statusCode < 300) {
      return jsonDecode(response.body);
    } else {
      throw Exception('Failed with status: ${response.statusCode}');
    }
  }
}
```

### 3. Usage Example

```dart
void main() async {
  try {
    // GET
    final users = await ApiService.get('/users');
    print(users);

    // POST
    final result = await ApiService.post('/users', {
      'name': 'John Doe',
      'email': 'john@example.com',
    });
    print(result);
  } catch (e) {
    print('Error: $e');
  }
}
```

### 4. Alternative: Per-Request Headers

You can also override or add headers on individual calls:

```dart
final response = await http.get(
  Uri.parse('https://api.example.com/data'),
  headers: {
    ...ApiService._headers,           // existing headers
    'X-Custom-Header': 'value',       // additional header
  },
);
```

### 5. Using Dio (Optional)

If you're using **Dio**, headers can be set globally or per request:

```dart
final dio = Dio(BaseOptions(
  baseUrl: 'https://api.example.com',
  headers: {
    'Authorization': 'Bearer YOUR_TOKEN',
    'Content-Type': 'application/json',
  },
));

// Per request
final response = await dio.get('/users', options: Options(
  headers: {'X-Custom': 'value'},
));
```

### Question
How do I implement authentication tokens in Flutter?

### Answer
**Implementing Authentication Tokens in Flutter (Best Practices)**

Here's a clean, production-ready approach using **Dio** + **flutter_secure_storage**.

### 1. Required Packages

```yaml
dependencies:
  dio: ^5.4.0
  flutter_secure_storage: ^9.0.0
```

### 2. Secure Token Storage

```dart
import 'package:flutter_secure_storage/flutter_secure_storage.dart';

class TokenStorage {
  static const _storage = FlutterSecureStorage();
  static const _accessTokenKey = 'access_token';
  static const _refreshTokenKey = 'refresh_token';

  static Future<void> saveTokens(String accessToken, String refreshToken) async {
    await _storage.write(key: _accessTokenKey, value: accessToken);
    await _storage.write(key: _refreshTokenKey, value: refreshToken);
  }

  static Future<String?> getAccessToken() async {
    return await _storage.read(key: _accessTokenKey);
  }

  static Future<String?> getRefreshToken() async {
    return await _storage.read(key: _refreshTokenKey);
  }

  static Future<void> clearTokens() async {
    await _storage.deleteAll();
  }
}
```

### 3. Dio Client with Token Interceptor

```dart
import 'package:dio/dio.dart';

class ApiClient {
  static final Dio _dio = Dio(
    BaseOptions(
      baseUrl: 'https://your-api.com/api',
      connectTimeout: const Duration(seconds: 10),
    ),
  );

  static Dio get dio {
    _dio.interceptors.clear();
    _dio.interceptors.add(
      InterceptorsWrapper(
        onRequest: (options, handler) async {
          final token = await TokenStorage.getAccessToken();
          if (token != null) {
            options.headers['Authorization'] = 'Bearer $token';
          }
          handler.next(options);
        },
        onError: (error, handler) async {
          if (error.response?.statusCode == 401) {
            // Token expired → attempt refresh
            final refreshed = await _refreshToken();
            if (refreshed) {
              // Retry original request
              final opts = error.requestOptions;
              opts.headers['Authorization'] =
                  'Bearer ${await TokenStorage.getAccessToken()}';
              return handler.resolve(await _dio.fetch(opts));
            }
          }
          handler.next(error);
        },
      ),
    );
    return _dio;
  }

  static Future<bool> _refreshToken() async {
    try {
      final refreshToken = await TokenStorage.getRefreshToken();
      if (refreshToken == null) return false;

      final response = await _dio.post('/auth/refresh', data: {
        'refresh_token': refreshToken,
      });

      final newAccessToken = response.data['access_token'];
      final newRefreshToken = response.data['refresh_token'];

      await TokenStorage.saveTokens

### Question
How do I use interceptors in dio?

### Answer
**Using Interceptors in Dio (Flutter/Dart)**

Dio allows you to intercept requests, responses, and errors using the `interceptors` property. This is useful for adding authentication headers, logging, refreshing tokens, error handling, etc.

### 1. Basic Setup with `InterceptorsWrapper`

The easiest way is to use `InterceptorsWrapper`:

```dart
import 'package:dio/dio.dart';

final dio = Dio(BaseOptions(
  baseUrl: 'https://api.example.com',
  connectTimeout: const Duration(seconds: 10),
));

void setupInterceptors() {
  dio.interceptors.add(
    InterceptorsWrapper(
      onRequest: (options, handler) {
        // Add auth token
        options.headers['Authorization'] = 'Bearer YOUR_TOKEN';
        print('→ REQUEST[${options.method}] => PATH: ${options.path}');
        handler.next(options); // continue
      },
      onResponse: (response, handler) {
        print('← RESPONSE[${response.statusCode}] => PATH: ${response.requestOptions.path}');
        handler.next(response); // continue
        // or handler.resolve(response); to resolve early
      },
      onError: (DioException e, handler) {
        print('ERROR[${e.response?.statusCode}] => ${e.message}');
        handler.next(e); // continue with error
        // or handler.resolve(...) / handler.reject(...) to handle differently
      },
    ),
  );
}
```

### 2. Adding Multiple Interceptors

```dart
dio.interceptors.addAll([
  LogInterceptor(
    request: true,
    requestHeader: true,
    responseHeader: false,
    responseBody: true,
  ),
  AuthInterceptor(), // custom interceptor
]);
```

### 3. Custom Interceptor Class (Recommended for complex logic)

```dart
class AuthInterceptor extends Interceptor {
  @override
  void onRequest(RequestOptions options, RequestInterceptorHandler handler) {
    options.headers['Authorization'] = 'Bearer ${getToken()}';
    handler.next(options);
  }

  @override
  void onError(DioException err, ErrorInterceptorHandler handler) {
    if (err.response?.statusCode == 401) {
      // Handle token refresh logic here
      print('Token expired, refreshing...');
    }
    handler.next(err);
  }

  String getToken() => 'your_access_token';
}
```

### 4. Token Refresh Example (Common Pattern)

```dart
dio.interceptors.add(
  InterceptorsWrapper(
    onRequest: (options, handler) async {
      final token = await getAccessToken();
      if (token != null) {
        options.headers['Authorization'] = 'Bearer $token';
      }
      handler.next(options);
    },
    onError: (error, handler) async {
      if (error.response?.statusCode == 401) {
        // Try refreshing token
        final newToken = await

### Question
How do I use SharedPreferences in Flutter?

### Answer
**SharedPreferences** is the standard way to store simple key-value data persistently in Flutter (e.g., user settings, flags, small strings/numbers).

### 1. Add Dependency

```yaml
dependencies:
  shared_preferences: ^2.2.3
```

Run:
```bash
flutter pub get
```

### 2. Basic Usage

```dart
import 'package:shared_preferences/shared_preferences.dart';

// Save data
final prefs = await SharedPreferences.getInstance();
await prefs.setString('username', 'john_doe');
await prefs.setInt('age', 28);
await prefs.setBool('isDarkMode', true);
await prefs.setStringList('tags', ['flutter', 'dart']);

// Read data
final username = prefs.getString('username') ?? 'Guest';
final age = prefs.getInt('age') ?? 0;
final isDarkMode = prefs.getBool('isDarkMode') ?? false;
final tags = prefs.getStringList('tags') ?? [];

// Remove a key
await prefs.remove('username');

// Clear all
await prefs.clear();
```

### 3. Complete Example (Counter with Persistence)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:shared_preferences/shared_preferences.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: CounterPage());
  }
}

class CounterPage extends StatefulWidget {
  const CounterPage({super.key});

  @override
  State<CounterPage> createState() => _CounterPageState();
}

class _CounterPageState extends State<CounterPage> {
  int _counter = 0;
  static const String _counterKey = 'counter';

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _loadCounter();
  }

  Future<void> _loadCounter() async {
    final prefs = await SharedPreferences.getInstance();
    setState(() {
      _counter = prefs.getInt(_counterKey) ?? 0;
    });
  }

  Future<void> _incrementCounter() async {
    final prefs = await SharedPreferences.getInstance();
    setState(() {
      _counter++;
    });
    await prefs.setInt(_counterKey, _counter);
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('SharedPreferences Demo')),
      body: Center(
        child: Text(
          'Counter: $_counter',
          style: const TextStyle(fontSize: 24),
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: _incrementCounter,
        child: const Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points & Best Practices

- **Always** use `await SharedPreferences.getInstance()` — it's cheap after the

### Question
How do I use Hive database in Flutter?

### Answer
Here's a complete guide to using **Hive** (a lightweight NoSQL database) in Flutter:

## 1. Add Dependencies

```yaml
dependencies:
  hive: ^2.2.3
  hive_flutter: ^1.1.0

dev_dependencies:
  build_runner: ^2.4.6
  hive_generator: ^2.0.1
```

## 2. Initialize Hive

In your `main.dart`:

```dart
import 'package:hive_flutter/hive_flutter.dart';

void main() async {
  WidgetsFlutterBinding.ensureInitialized();
  await Hive.initFlutter(); // Initializes Hive for Flutter
  runApp(MyApp());
}
```

## 3. Create a Model

```dart
import 'package:hive/hive.dart';

part 'person.g.dart'; // Generated file

@HiveType(typeId: 0)
class Person extends HiveObject {
  @HiveField(0)
  String name;

  @HiveField(1)
  int age;

  @HiveField(2)
  String? address;

  Person({required this.name, required this.age, this.address});
}
```

## 4. Generate the Adapter

Run this command to generate `person.g.dart`:

```bash
flutter packages pub run build_runner build
```

## 5. Register Adapter & Open Box

```dart
void main() async {
  WidgetsFlutterBinding.ensureInitialized();
  await Hive.initFlutter();
  
  // Register adapter
  Hive.registerAdapter(PersonAdapter());
  
  // Open box (like opening a table)
  await Hive.openBox<Person>('people');
  
  runApp(MyApp());
}
```

## 6. CRUD Operations

```dart
class HiveService {
  // Get the box
  Box<Person> get _box => Hive.box<Person>('people');

  // CREATE
  Future<void> addPerson(Person person) async {
    await _box.add(person); // Auto-increment key
    // Or: await _box.put('custom_key', person);
  }

  // READ
  List<Person> getAllPeople() {
    return _box.values.toList();
  }

  Person? getPerson(int index) {
    return _box.getAt(index);
  }

  // UPDATE
  Future<void> updatePerson(int index, Person person) async {
    await _box.putAt(index, person);
  }

  // DELETE
  Future<void> deletePerson(int index) async {
    await _box.deleteAt(index);
  }

  // Watch for changes (reactive)
  Stream<BoxEvent> watchBox() {
    return _box.watch();
  }
}
```

## 7. Using in a Widget

```dart
class PeopleList extends StatelessWidget {
  final HiveService _hiveService = HiveService();

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return ValueListenableBuilder(
      valueListenable: Hive.box<Person>('people').listenable(),


### Question
How do I use SQLite in Flutter?

### Answer
To use SQLite in Flutter, use the **`sqflite`** package (the most widely used and maintained solution).

### 1. Add Dependencies

```yaml
dependencies:
  sqflite: ^2.3.0
  path_provider: ^2.1.1
  path: ^1.8.3
```

### 2. Create a Database Helper (Recommended Pattern)

Create a `DatabaseHelper` class to manage database creation, opening, and CRUD operations:

```dart
import 'package:sqflite/sqflite.dart';
import 'package:path/path.dart';
import 'package:path_provider/path_provider.dart';

class DatabaseHelper {
  static final DatabaseHelper instance = DatabaseHelper._init();
  static Database? _database;

  DatabaseHelper._init();

  Future<Database> get database async {
    if (_database != null) return _database!;
    _database = await _initDB('app.db');
    return _database!;
  }

  Future<Database> _initDB(String filePath) async {
    final dbPath = await getDatabasesPath();
    final path = join(dbPath, filePath);
    return await openDatabase(
      path,
      version: 1,
      onCreate: _createDB,
    );
  }

  Future _createDB(Database db, int version) async {
    await db.execute('''
      CREATE TABLE users (
        id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
        name TEXT NOT NULL,
        age INTEGER NOT NULL
      )
    ''');
  }

  // ==================== CRUD Operations ====================

  Future<int> insertUser(Map<String, dynamic> user) async {
    final db = await instance.database;
    return await db.insert('users', user);
  }

  Future<List<Map<String, dynamic>>> getAllUsers() async {
    final db = await instance.database;
    return await db.query('users');
  }

  Future<int> updateUser(Map<String, dynamic> user) async {
    final db = await instance.database;
    return await db.update(
      'users',
      user,
      where: 'id = ?',
      whereArgs: [user['id']],
    );
  }

  Future<int> deleteUser(int id) async {
    final db = await instance.database;
    return await db.delete('users', where: 'id = ?', whereArgs: [id]);
  }

  Future close() async {
    final db = await instance.database;
    db.close();
  }
}
```

### 3. Usage Example

```dart
void main() async {
  WidgetsFlutterBinding.ensureInitialized();
  
  // Insert
  final id = await DatabaseHelper.instance.insertUser({
    'name': 'John Doe',
    'age': 30,
  });
  print('Inserted user with id: $id');

  // Read
  final users = await DatabaseHelper.instance.getAllUsers();
  print(users);

  // Update
  await DatabaseHelper.instance.update

### Question
How do I store files in Flutter?

### Answer
**To store files in Flutter**, use the `path_provider` package to get safe directories + Dart’s `dart:io` `File` class to read/write.

### 1. Add Dependencies

```yaml
dependencies:
  path_provider: ^2.1.3
  permission_handler: ^11.3.1   # only needed for external/public storage
```

### 2. Basic Example – Save & Read a File

```dart
import 'dart:io';
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:path_provider/path_provider.dart';

class FileStorageExample extends StatefulWidget {
  const FileStorageExample({super.key});

  @override
  State<FileStorageExample> createState() => _FileStorageExampleState();
}

class _FileStorageExampleState extends State<FileStorageExample> {
  String _content = '';

  // Get the app's private documents directory (recommended)
  Future<String> _getLocalPath() async {
    final directory = await getApplicationDocumentsDirectory();
    return directory.path;
  }

  Future<File> _getLocalFile() async {
    final path = await _getLocalPath();
    return File('$path/my_file.txt');
  }

  Future<void> writeFile(String text) async {
    final file = await _getLocalFile();
    await file.writeAsString(text);
    setState(() => _content = text);
  }

  Future<void> readFile() async {
    try {
      final file = await _getLocalFile();
      final contents = await file.readAsString();
      setState(() => _content = contents);
    } catch (e) {
      setState(() => _content = 'Error reading file: $e');
    }
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('File Storage')),
      body: Padding(
        padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16),
        child: Column(
          children: [
            TextField(
              onSubmitted: writeFile,
              decoration: const InputDecoration(
                labelText: 'Enter text to save',
                border: OutlineInputBorder(),
              ),
            ),
            const SizedBox(height: 20),
            ElevatedButton(
              onPressed: () => writeFile('Hello from Flutter!'),
              child: const Text('Save Sample Text'),
            ),
            ElevatedButton(
              onPressed: readFile,
              child: const Text('Read File'),
            ),
            const SizedBox(height: 20),
            Text('File Content:\n$_content'),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 3. Different Storage Locations

| Method                              | Use Case                          | Notes                              |
|-------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| `getApplicationDocumentsDirectory()`| User data, settings               | Private to app (recommended)       |
| `getTemporaryDirectory()`           | Cache files

### Question
How do I use secure storage in Flutter?

### Answer
**Secure Storage in Flutter** uses the `flutter_secure_storage` package, which stores data in the platform’s secure enclave:

- **iOS/macOS**: Keychain
- **Android**: EncryptedSharedPreferences / Android Keystore
- **Windows/Linux**: Uses DPAPI / libsecret (still reasonably secure)
- **Web**: Falls back to browser storage (less secure)

### 1. Installation

```yaml
dependencies:
  flutter_secure_storage: ^9.2.2
```

Run:

```bash
flutter pub get
```

### 2. Basic Usage

```dart
import 'package:flutter_secure_storage/flutter_secure_storage.dart';

final storage = FlutterSecureStorage();

// Write
await storage.write(key: 'auth_token', value: 'your_jwt_token');

// Read
final token = await storage.read(key: 'auth_token');

// Delete one key
await storage.delete(key: 'auth_token');

// Delete everything
await storage.deleteAll();
```

### 3. Recommended: Create a Secure Storage Service

```dart
import 'package:flutter_secure_storage/flutter_secure_storage.dart';

class SecureStorageService {
  static const _storage = FlutterSecureStorage(
    aOptions: AndroidOptions(
      encryptedSharedPreferences: true,
    ),
    iOptions: IOSOptions(
      accessibility: KeychainAccessibility.first_unlock,
    ),
  );

  // Save data
  Future<void> write(String key, String value) async {
    await _storage.write(key: key, value: value);
  }

  // Read data
  Future<String?> read(String key) async {
    return await _storage.read(key: key);
  }

  // Delete data
  Future<void> delete(String key) async {
    await _storage.delete(key: key);
  }

  // Check if key exists
  Future<bool> containsKey(String key) async {
    return await _storage.containsKey(key: key);
  }

  // Clear all
  Future<void> clearAll() async {
    await _storage.deleteAll();
  }
}
```

### 4. Usage Example

```dart
final secureStorage = SecureStorageService();

// Save token after login
await secureStorage.write('access_token', token);

// Retrieve token
final token = await secureStorage.read('access_token');

// Delete on logout
await secureStorage.delete('access_token');
```

### 5. Important Options

| Platform | Option | Recommendation |
|--------|--------|----------------|
| **Android** | `encryptedSharedPreferences: true` | Always enable |
| **iOS** | `accessibility` | Use `first_unlock` or `first_unlock_this_device` |
| **iOS** | `synchronizable` | Set to `false` unless you want iCloud sync |

### 6. Error Handling Tip

Wrap operations in try-catch because storage can fail on some devices:

```dart
try {
  await storage.write(key: 'token', value: token);
} catch (e) {


### Question
How do I use Firebase in Flutter?

### Answer
To use Firebase in Flutter, follow this modern, recommended approach using the **FlutterFire CLI**.

### 1. Create a Firebase Project

```

Run this command in your project root:

```bash
flutterfire configure
```

This command:
- Connects your Flutter app to your Firebase project
- Automatically adds the required configuration files (`google-services.json`, `GoogleService-Info.plist`)
- Creates `lib/firebase_options.dart`

### 4. Add Dependencies

Add these packages:

```bash
flutter pub add firebase_core
```

For specific services, add what you need:

```bash
flutter pub add firebase_auth cloud_firestore firebase_storage
```

import 'package:cloud_firestore/cloud_firestore.dart';

class HomePage extends StatelessWidget {

            return const Center(child: Text('Something went wrong'));

            return const Center(child: CircularProgressIndicator());
          }

          return ListView(
            children: snapshot.data!.docs.map((DocumentSnapshot document) {
              Map<String, dynamic> data = document.data()! as Map<String, dynamic>;
              return ListTile(
                title: Text(data['name'] ?? ''),

### Question
How do I implement Firebase Auth in Flutter?

### Answer
**Firebase Auth in Flutter (2024)**

Here's a clean, production-ready implementation using **email/password** authentication.

### 1. Add Dependencies

```yaml
dependencies:
  firebase_core: ^3.3.0
  firebase_auth: ^5.1.4
```

Run:
```bash
flutter pub get
```

### 2. Firebase Setup

- Create a Firebase project
- Add **Android** and **iOS** apps in Firebase Console
- Download `google-services.json` (Android) and `GoogleService-Info.plist` (iOS)
- Place them in the correct folders

### 3. Initialize Firebase

**`main.dart`**
```dart
import 'package:firebase_core/firebase_core.dart';
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'firebase_options.dart'; // Generated by FlutterFire CLI

void main() async {
  WidgetsFlutterBinding.ensureInitialized();
  await Firebase.initializeApp(
    options: DefaultFirebaseOptions.currentPlatform,
  );
  runApp(const MyApp());
}
```

> **Tip**: Generate `firebase_options.dart` using:
> ```bash
> dart pub global activate flutterfire_cli
> flutterfire configure
> ```

### 4. Create Auth Service

**`services/auth_service.dart`**
```dart
import 'package:firebase_auth/firebase_auth.dart';

class AuthService {
  final FirebaseAuth _auth = FirebaseAuth.instance;

  // Auth state stream
  Stream<User?> get authStateChanges => _auth.authStateChanges();

  // Sign Up
  Future<User?> signUp({
    required String email,
    required String password,
  }) async {
    try {
      final credential = await _auth.createUserWithEmailAndPassword(
        email: email.trim(),
        password: password,
      );
      return credential.user;
    } on FirebaseAuthException catch (e) {
      throw _handleAuthException(e);
    }
  }

  // Sign In
  Future<User?> signIn({
    required String email,
    required String password,
  }) async {
    try {
      final credential = await _auth.signInWithEmailAndPassword(
        email: email.trim(),
        password: password,
      );
      return credential.user;
    } on FirebaseAuthException catch (e) {
      throw _handleAuthException(e);
    }
  }

  // Sign Out
  Future<void> signOut() async {
    await _auth.signOut();
  }

  String _handleAuthException(FirebaseAuthException e) {
    switch (e.code) {
      case 'weak-password':
        return 'The password is too weak.';
      case 'email-already-in-use':
        return 'An account already exists with this email.';
      case 'user-not-found':
        return 'No user found with this email.';
      case 'wrong-password':
        return 'Wrong password provided.';
      default:
        return 'Authentication failed. Please try again.';
    }

### Question
How do I use Firebase Storage in Flutter?

### Answer
Here's a complete, production-ready guide to using **Firebase Storage** in Flutter.

### 1. Setup

**Add dependencies:**

```yaml
dependencies:
  firebase_core: ^3.3.0
  firebase_storage: ^12.1.3
  image_picker: ^1.1.2          # For picking images/files
  path_provider: ^2.1.4        # Optional: for local file paths
```

**Initialize Firebase** in `main.dart`:

```dart
import 'package:firebase_core/firebase_core.dart';
import 'firebase_options.dart'; // Generated by `flutterfire configure`

void main() async {
  WidgetsFlutterBinding.ensureInitialized();
  await Firebase.initializeApp(
    options: DefaultFirebaseOptions.currentPlatform,
  );
  runApp(const MyApp());
}
```

---

### 2. Basic Firebase Storage Helper

Create a service class for clean usage:

```dart
import 'dart:io';
import 'package:firebase_storage/firebase_storage.dart';
import 'package:image_picker/image_picker.dart';

class FirebaseStorageService {
  final FirebaseStorage _storage = FirebaseStorage.instance;

  /// Upload a file and return its download URL
  Future<String> uploadFile({
    required File file,
    required String path,
  }) async {
    try {
      final ref = _storage.ref().child(path);
      final uploadTask = ref.putFile(file);

      final snapshot = await uploadTask.whenComplete(() {});
      final downloadUrl = await snapshot.ref.getDownloadURL();
      return downloadUrl;
    } on FirebaseException catch (e) {
      throw Exception('Upload failed: ${e.message}');
    }
  }

  /// Pick and upload an image
  Future<String?> pickAndUploadImage({
    required ImageSource source,
    required String folder,
  }) async {
    final picker = ImagePicker();
    final pickedFile = await picker.pickImage(source: source);

    if (pickedFile == null) return null;

    final file = File(pickedFile.path);
    final fileName = '${DateTime.now().millisecondsSinceEpoch}.jpg';
    final path = '$folder/$fileName';

    return await uploadFile(file: file, path: path);
  }

  /// Delete a file using its URL
  Future<void> deleteFile(String url) async {
    try {
      final ref = _storage.refFromURL(url);
      await ref.delete();
    } catch (e) {
      throw Exception('Delete failed: $e');
    }
  }
}
```

---

### 3. Usage Example

```dart
final storageService = FirebaseStorageService();

Future<void> uploadProfilePicture() async {
  try {
    final url = await storageService.pickAndUploadImage(
      source: ImageSource.gallery,
      folder: 'profile_pictures',
    );

    if (url != null) {
      print('Uploaded! URL: $url');
      // Save URL to Firestore if needed


### Question
How do I use Firebase Cloud Messaging in Flutter?

### Answer
**Firebase Cloud Messaging (FCM) in Flutter** is handled through the official `firebase_messaging` package. Below is a complete, production-ready setup.

### 1. Setup & Dependencies

```yaml
dependencies:
  firebase_core: ^3.3.0
  firebase_messaging: ^15.0.4
```

Run:
```bash
flutter pub get
```

### 2. Firebase Configuration

1. Create a Firebase project and add your Android/iOS apps.
2. Download `google-services.json` (Android) and `GoogleService-Info.plist` (iOS).
3. Place them in the correct locations:
   - Android: `android/app/google-services.json`
   - iOS: `ios/Runner/GoogleService-Info.plist`

### 3. Basic Initialization (Recommended Pattern)

```dart
import 'package:firebase_core/firebase_core.dart';
import 'package:firebase_messaging/firebase_messaging.dart';
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

@pragma('vm:entry-point')
Future<void> _firebaseMessagingBackgroundHandler(RemoteMessage message) async {
  await Firebase.initializeApp();
  debugPrint("Background message: ${message.messageId}");
}

Future<void> main() async {
  WidgetsFlutterBinding.ensureInitialized();
  await Firebase.initializeApp();

  // Background handler must be registered before runApp
  FirebaseMessaging.onBackgroundMessage(_firebaseMessagingBackgroundHandler);

  runApp(const MyApp());
}
```

### 4. Request Permission & Get Token

```dart
class FCMService {
  static final FirebaseMessaging _messaging = FirebaseMessaging.instance;

  static Future<void> initialize() async {
    // Request permission (required on iOS & Android 13+)
    NotificationSettings settings = await _messaging.requestPermission(
      alert: true,
      badge: true,
      sound: true,
    );

    if (settings.authorizationStatus == AuthorizationStatus.authorized) {
      debugPrint('User granted permission');
    }

    // Get FCM token
    String? token = await _messaging.getToken();
    debugPrint("FCM Token: $token");

    // Listen for token refresh
    _messaging.onTokenRefresh.listen((newToken) {
      debugPrint("FCM Token refreshed: $newToken");
    });
  }
}
```

Call this in `main()` or after login:

```dart
await FCMService.initialize();
```

### 5. Handling Messages

```dart
void setupFCMListeners() {
  // Foreground messages
  FirebaseMessaging.onMessage.listen((RemoteMessage message) {
    debugPrint('Foreground message: ${message.notification?.title}');
    // Show local notification if needed (see below)
  });

  // When app is opened from terminated state via notification
  FirebaseMessaging.onMessageOpenedApp.listen((RemoteMessage message) {
    debugPrint('Opened from terminated: ${message.data}');
    // Navigate to specific screen
  });

  // Check if

### Question
How do I add animations in Flutter?

### Answer
Here's how to add animations in Flutter, ranging from simple to more controlled approaches.

### 1. Implicit Animations (Easiest)

Use widgets like `AnimatedContainer`, `AnimatedOpacity`, `AnimatedAlign`, etc. They automatically animate when their properties change.

```dart
class ImplicitAnimationExample extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<ImplicitAnimationExample> createState() => _ImplicitAnimationExampleState();
}

class _ImplicitAnimationExampleState extends State<ImplicitAnimationExample> {
  bool _expanded = false;

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return GestureDetector(
      onTap: () => setState(() => _expanded = !_expanded),
      child: AnimatedContainer(
        duration: const Duration(milliseconds: 300),
        curve: Curves.easeInOut,
        width: _expanded ? 200 : 100,
        height: _expanded ? 200 : 100,
        decoration: BoxDecoration(
          color: _expanded ? Colors.blue : Colors.red,
          borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(_expanded ? 20 : 8),
        ),
        child: const Center(child: Text('Tap me', style: TextStyle(color: Colors.white))),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Explicit Animations (More Control)

Use `AnimationController` + `Tween` when you need precise control (e.g., repeating animations, custom curves, or coordinating multiple animations).

```dart
class ExplicitAnimationExample extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<ExplicitAnimationExample> createState() => _ExplicitAnimationExampleState();
}

class _ExplicitAnimationExampleState extends State<ExplicitAnimationExample>
    with SingleTickerProviderStateMixin {
  late AnimationController _controller;
  late Animation<double> _animation;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _controller = AnimationController(
      duration: const Duration(seconds: 2),
      vsync: this,
    )..repeat(reverse: true);

    _animation = Tween<double>(begin: 0.0, end: 1.0).animate(
      CurvedAnimation(parent: _controller, curve: Curves.easeInOut),
    );
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _controller.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return AnimatedBuilder(
      animation: _animation,
      builder: (context, child) {
        return Transform.scale(
          scale: _animation.value,
          child: Container(
            width: 100,
            height: 100,
            color: Colors.green,
          ),
        );
      },
    );
  }
}
```

### 3. Quick Tips

- **Hero animations** (screen transitions): Wrap widgets with the `Hero` widget using matching `tag`s.
- **Staggered / complex animations**: Use multiple `Tween`s with `Interval` or

### Question
What is AnimationController in Flutter?

### Answer
**AnimationController** is the core class in Flutter for managing animations. It controls the animation's progress over time (typically from `0.0` to `1.0`) and acts as the "brain" that drives all animation widgets.

### Key Characteristics

- Extends `Animation<double>`
- Produces values between a `lowerBound` (default `0.0`) and `upperBound` (default `1.0`)
- Requires a `vsync` (TickerProvider) to sync with the screen refresh rate
- Must be disposed when no longer needed to avoid memory leaks

### Basic Usage

```dart
class _MyWidgetState extends State<MyWidget> 
    with SingleTickerProviderStateMixin {
  
  late AnimationController _controller;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    
    _controller = AnimationController(
      duration: const Duration(milliseconds: 500),
      vsync: this,                    // Required
      lowerBound: 0.0,
      upperBound: 1.0,
    );
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _controller.dispose();            // Always dispose!
    super.dispose();
  }

  void _startAnimation() {
    _controller.forward();            // 0.0 → 1.0
    // _controller.reverse();         // 1.0 → 0.0
    // _controller.repeat();          // Loops
    // _controller.repeat(reverse: true);
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return GestureDetector(
      onTap: _startAnimation,
      child: AnimatedBuilder(
        animation: _controller,
        builder: (context, child) {
          return Opacity(
            opacity: _controller.value,
            child: const FlutterLogo(size: 100),
          );
        },
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Methods

| Method              | Description                              |
|---------------------|------------------------------------------|
| `forward()`         | Animates from current value to `upperBound` |
| `reverse()`         | Animates from current value to `lowerBound` |
| `repeat()`          | Loops the animation                      |
| `reset()`           | Sets value back to `lowerBound`          |
| `stop()`            | Stops the animation                      |
| `animateTo()`       | Animates to a specific value             |
| `fling()`           | Uses physics simulation                  |

### Important Notes

- Always use `SingleTickerProviderStateMixin` (or `TickerProviderStateMixin` for multiple controllers) in your `State` class.
- Never forget to call `dispose()` on the controller.
- Use `AnimatedBuilder` or `AnimatedWidget` to rebuild only the animated part efficiently.

**In short**: `AnimationController` is the object you interact with to *control* when and how an animation runs, while `Animation<double>`

### Question
How do I use Hero animation in Flutter?

### Answer
**Hero Animation in Flutter**

Hero animations create smooth transitions of a widget (like an image, icon, or container) between two screens. Flutter automatically animates the widget from its position and size on the first screen to its position and size on the second screen.

### Basic Implementation

Wrap the widget you want to animate with the `Hero` widget on **both** screens, using the **same** `tag`.

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(
      home: FirstPage(),
    );
  }
}

class FirstPage extends StatelessWidget {
  const FirstPage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('First Page')),
      body: Center(
        child: GestureDetector(
          onTap: () {
            Navigator.of(context).push(
              MaterialPageRoute(builder: (_) => const SecondPage()),
            );
          },
          child: const Hero(
            tag: 'hero-tag', // Must match on both screens
            child: FlutterLogo(size: 100),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}

class SecondPage extends StatelessWidget {
  const SecondPage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Second Page')),
      body: const Center(
        child: Hero(
          tag: 'hero-tag', // Same tag as first page
          child: FlutterLogo(size: 200),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points

- **Same `tag`**: Required on both `Hero` widgets.
- **Same widget tree position**: The widget should be a direct child of `Hero`.
- **Unique tags**: Use unique tags when you have multiple heroes on the same screen.
- Works automatically with `Navigator.push` / `pop`.

### Advanced Example (with Custom Flight Animation)

You can customize how the widget flies using `flightShuttleBuilder`:

```dart
Hero(
  tag: 'hero-tag',
  flightShuttleBuilder: (
    BuildContext flightContext,
    Animation<double> animation,
    HeroFlightDirection flightDirection,
    BuildContext fromHeroContext,
    BuildContext toHeroContext,
  ) {
    return RotationTransition(
      turns: animation,
      child: toHeroContext.widget,
    );
  },
  child: const FlutterLogo(size: 100),
)
```

### Best Practices

| Tip | Recommendation |
|-----|----------------|
| **Tags** | Use constants or unique IDs (e.g., `hero-$id`) |
| **Size changes** | Hero handles different sizes automatically |
| **Multiple heroes** | Each must have a unique

### Question
How do I use Tween in Flutter?

### Answer
**Tween** in Flutter defines a linear interpolation between a beginning and ending value. It is the foundation of most animations and works together with an `AnimationController`.

### Basic Usage Pattern

```dart
class TweenDemo extends StatefulWidget {
  const TweenDemo({super.key});

  @override
  State<TweenDemo> createState() => _TweenDemoState();
}

class _TweenDemoState extends State<TweenDemo>
    with SingleTickerProviderStateMixin {
  late AnimationController _controller;
  late Animation<double> _animation;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();

    _controller = AnimationController(
      duration: const Duration(seconds: 2),
      vsync: this,
    );

    // Create a Tween and animate it
    _animation = Tween<double>(begin: 0.0, end: 300.0).animate(_controller);

    _controller.forward(); // Start the animation
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _controller.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: Center(
        child: AnimatedBuilder(
          animation: _animation,
          builder: (context, child) {
            return Container(
              width: _animation.value,
              height: _animation.value,
              color: Colors.blue,
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Common Tween Types

| Tween Type       | Use Case                     | Example |
|------------------|------------------------------|---------|
| `Tween<double>`  | Size, opacity, rotation      | `Tween<double>(begin: 0, end: 1)` |
| `ColorTween`     | Color transitions            | `ColorTween(begin: Colors.red, end: Colors.blue)` |
| `IntTween`       | Integer values               | `IntTween(begin: 0, end: 100)` |
| `SizeTween`      | Widget size                  | `SizeTween(begin: Size(0,0), end: Size(200,200))` |
| `RectTween`      | Hero animations              | `RectTween(begin: rect1, end: rect2)` |

### Example with ColorTween + Curve

```dart
_animation = ColorTween(
  begin: Colors.red,
  end: Colors.blue,
).animate(
  CurvedAnimation(
    parent: _controller,
    curve: Curves.easeInOut,
  ),
);
```

### Using `Tween.animate()` vs Direct Tween

```dart
// Recommended pattern
final animation = Tween<double>(begin: 0, end: 1).animate(controller);

// You can also chain multiple tweens
final curved = Tween<double>(begin: 0, end: 1).animate(
  CurvedAnimation(parent: controller, curve: Curves.easeOut),
);
```

### Key Points

- Always call `controller.dispose()` in `dispose

### Question
What is AnimatedBuilder in Flutter?

### Answer
**AnimatedBuilder** is a widget in Flutter that rebuilds its child whenever a given `Animation` changes. It’s a lightweight and efficient way to separate animation logic from your widget tree.

### Why Use AnimatedBuilder?

- It listens to an `Animation` (usually driven by an `AnimationController`) and only rebuilds the part of the UI that needs to change.
- It avoids rebuilding large portions of the widget tree on every animation tick.
- It’s especially useful when you want to apply transformations (rotation, scale, position, opacity, etc.) based on animation values.

### Basic Syntax

```dart
AnimatedBuilder(
  animation: animation,           // Animation or AnimationController
  builder: (context, child) {
    return Transform.rotate(
      angle: animation.value,
      child: child,
    );
  },
  child: const Icon(Icons.star, size: 100), // Reused, not rebuilt
)
```

### Complete Example: Rotating Box

```dart
class RotatingBox extends StatefulWidget {
  const RotatingBox({super.key});

  @override
  State<RotatingBox> createState() => _RotatingBoxState();
}

class _RotatingBoxState extends State<RotatingBox>
    with SingleTickerProviderStateMixin {
  late final AnimationController _controller;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _controller = AnimationController(
      duration: const Duration(seconds: 2),
      vsync: this,
    )..repeat(); // Loops forever
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _controller.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: Center(
        child: AnimatedBuilder(
          animation: _controller,
          builder: (context, child) {
            return Transform.rotate(
              angle: _controller.value * 2 * 3.14159, // Full rotation
              child: child,
            );
          },
          child: Container(
            width: 100,
            height: 100,
            color: Colors.blue,
            child: const Icon(Icons.star, color: Colors.white, size: 60),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Points

- The `child` parameter is **not** rebuilt on every animation tick. This is a performance optimization.
- You can listen to either an `AnimationController` or a specific `Animation<T>` (e.g., `Tween`).
- `AnimatedBuilder` is a subclass of `AnimatedWidget`.

### When to Use It

| Use Case                        | Recommended Widget          |
|--------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| Simple value animation         | `TweenAnimationBuilder`     |
| Complex/multiple animations    | `AnimatedBuilder`           |
| Reusing same widget in builder | `AnimatedBuilder` (with `child`) |
| Very custom animation logic    |

### Question
How do I use implicit animations in Flutter?

### Answer
Implicit animations in Flutter let you animate widget property changes automatically. You simply update a property (via `setState` or a reactive state solution), and the widget handles the animation for you.

### Common Implicit Animation Widgets

| Widget                    | What it animates                  | Use Case                          |
|---------------------------|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| `AnimatedContainer`       | size, color, padding, margin, etc | Most common general-purpose       |
| `AnimatedOpacity`         | opacity                           | Fade in/out                       |
| `AnimatedAlign`           | alignment                         | Moving a child inside a parent    |
| `AnimatedPositioned`      | position (inside Stack)           | Moving widgets in a Stack         |
| `TweenAnimationBuilder`   | any value                         | Custom implicit animations        |

### Example: AnimatedContainer

Here's a complete, runnable example that animates size, color, and border radius:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: AnimatedContainerDemo());
  }
}

class AnimatedContainerDemo extends StatefulWidget {
  const AnimatedContainerDemo({super.key});

  @override
  State<AnimatedContainerDemo> createState() => _AnimatedContainerDemoState();
}

class _AnimatedContainerDemoState extends State<AnimatedContainerDemo> {
  double _width = 100;
  double _height = 100;
  Color _color = Colors.blue;
  BorderRadius _borderRadius = BorderRadius.circular(8);

  void _animate() {
    setState(() {
      _width = _width == 100 ? 200 : 100;
      _height = _height == 100 ? 200 : 100;
      _color = _color == Colors.blue ? Colors.red : Colors.blue;
      _borderRadius = _borderRadius == BorderRadius.circular(8)
          ? BorderRadius.circular(40)
          : BorderRadius.circular(8);
    });
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Implicit Animation')),
      body: Center(
        child: Column(
          mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
          children: [
            AnimatedContainer(
              width: _width,
              height: _height,
              decoration: BoxDecoration(
                color: _color,
                borderRadius: _borderRadius,
              ),
              duration: const Duration(milliseconds: 500),
              curve: Curves.easeInOut, // optional
            ),
            const SizedBox(height: 40),
            ElevatedButton(
              onPressed: _animate,
              child: const Text('Animate'),
            ),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Parameters

- `duration`: Required — how long

### Question
What is the difference between final and const in Dart?

### Answer
In Dart, both `final` and `const` prevent reassignment, but they differ in **when** the value is determined and **how** the object behaves.

## Key Differences

| Feature | `final` | `const` |
|---------|---------|---------|
| **Value determined** | Runtime | Compile-time |
| **Can use runtime values** | Yes | No |
| **Object mutability** | Can mutate internal state | Fully immutable |
| **Memory** | New instance each time | Canonicalized (shared instance) |

## Code Examples

### 1. Basic Assignment
```dart
void main() {
  final String name = 'Alice';        // OK - runtime value
  const String greeting = 'Hello';    // OK - compile-time value
  
  // final can use DateTime.now() (runtime)
  final timestamp = DateTime.now();   // Works
  
  // const cannot (value unknown at compile time)
  // const timestamp = DateTime.now(); // Compile error
}
```

### 2. Object Immutability
```dart
void main() {
  final list1 = [1, 2, 3];
  list1.add(4);        // ✅ Allowed - mutating the list
  // list1 = [5];      // ❌ Error - cannot reassign

  const list2 = [1, 2, 3];
  // list2.add(4);     // ❌ Error - const list is immutable
}
```

### 3. Flutter Widget Optimization (Most Important)
```dart
class MyWidget extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const Text('Hello');  // ✅ Reuses same instance across rebuilds
    // return Text('Hello');     // Creates new Text widget each rebuild
  }
}
```

Use `const` for widgets that don't change to avoid unnecessary rebuilds.

### 4. Const Constructors
```dart
class Point {
  final int x;
  final int y;
  
  const Point(this.x, this.y);  // Const constructor
}

void main() {
  const p1 = Point(0, 0);
  const p2 = Point(0, 0);
  print(identical(p1, p2));  // true - same instance in memory
}
```

## Quick Rule of Thumb

- Use **`const`** when the value is known before the app runs (literals, const constructors)
- Use **`final`** when the value is determined when the code executes but won't change afterward

### Question
What is null safety in Dart?

### Answer
Null safety is Dart's type system feature (introduced in Dart 2.12 and enabled by default) that eliminates null reference errors at compile time. It makes types **non-nullable by default**, so the compiler guarantees that a non-nullable variable can never hold `null`.

### Core Concepts

- **Non-nullable by default**: `String name;` cannot be `null`.
- **Nullable types**: Add `?` to allow `null`, e.g., `String? name;`.
- **Sound null safety**: The type checker ensures no null dereference can occur at runtime.

### Code Examples

#### 1. Basic Declaration

```dart
// Non-nullable (default)
String name = 'Flutter';     // OK
// name = null;              // Compile error

// Nullable
String? nickname;            // OK (implicitly null)
nickname = 'Dash';
nickname = null;             // OK
```

#### 2. Null-Aware Operators

```dart
String? userName;

// Safe property access
final length = userName?.length;           // int? (null if userName is null)

// Default value
final displayName = userName ?? 'Guest';   // String

// Null assertion (use only when you know it's not null)
final nameLength = userName!.length;       // int (throws if null)
```

#### 3. Flow Analysis & Promotion

Dart promotes nullable types to non-nullable after a null check:

```dart
void greet(String? name) {
  if (name != null) {
    print('Hello, ${name.toUpperCase()}'); // name is promoted to String
  } else {
    print('Hello, Guest');
  }
}
```

#### 4. Late Initialization

Use `late` when you know a value will be assigned before use:

```dart
class User {
  late String id;                    // Must be assigned before reading

  void initialize(String userId) {
    id = userId;
  }
}
```

#### 5. Required Parameters in Flutter Widgets

```dart
class ProfileCard extends StatelessWidget {
  final String name;           // Non-nullable
  final String? bio;           // Nullable

  const ProfileCard({
    super.key,
    required this.name,        // Must be provided
    this.bio,
  });

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Column(
      children: [
        Text(name),
        if (bio != null) Text(bio!),   // or use bio ?? ''
      ],
    );
  }
}
```

### Why It Matters in Flutter

- Fewer runtime crashes in production.
- Better IDE support and refactoring safety.
- Required for modern packages (many have dropped null-safety support).

**Migration tip**: Run `dart migrate` (or use the analyzer) when upgrading older projects. Always prefer non-nullable types unless `null` is a valid state.

### Question
How do I use mixins in Dart?

### Answer
**Mixins** in Dart let you reuse code across multiple class hierarchies without inheritance. They’re especially useful in Flutter for sharing behavior like theming, logging, animations, or validation logic.

### Basic Syntax

Define a mixin with the `mixin` keyword and apply it using `with`:

```dart
mixin CanFly {
  void fly() => print('Flying...');
}

mixin CanSwim {
  void swim() => print('Swimming...');
}

class Duck with CanFly, CanSwim {}

void main() {
  final duck = Duck();
  duck.fly();   // Flying...
  duck.swim();  // Swimming...
}
```

### Restricting a Mixin with `on`

Use `on` to limit which classes can use the mixin:

```dart
class Animal {
  void breathe() => print('Breathing...');
}

mixin Walker on Animal {
  void walk() => print('Walking...');
}

class Dog extends Animal with Walker {}

void main() {
  final dog = Dog();
  dog.breathe();
  dog.walk();
}
```

If you try to apply `Walker` to a class that doesn’t extend `Animal`, you’ll get a compile error.

### Accessing the Superclass (`super`)

Since Dart 2.7, mixins can call methods on the class they’re mixed into:

```dart
mixin Logger on Object {
  void log(String message) {
    print('[${runtimeType}] $message');
  }
}

class User with Logger {
  void save() {
    log('Saving user...');
    // actual save logic
  }
}
```

### Real Flutter Example

Here’s a practical Flutter mixin for managing loading state and showing SnackBars:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

mixin LoadingMixin<T extends StatefulWidget> on State<T> {
  bool isLoading = false;

  void setLoading(bool value) {
    if (mounted) {
      setState(() => isLoading = value);
    }
  }

  void showError(String message) {
    ScaffoldMessenger.of(context).showSnackBar(
      SnackBar(content: Text(message)),
    );
  }
}

class MyForm extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<MyForm> createState() => _MyFormState();
}

class _MyFormState extends State<MyForm> with LoadingMixin<MyForm> {
  Future<void> submit() async {
    setLoading(true);
    try {
      await Future.delayed(const Duration(seconds: 2));
      // success logic
    } catch (e) {
      showError('Something went wrong');
    } finally {
      setLoading(false);
    }
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return ElevatedButton(
      onPressed: isLoading ? null : submit,
      child: isLoading
          ? const CircularProgressIndicator()
          : const Text('Submit'),
    );
  }
}
```

### Key Rules

- Mixins cannot have

### Question
What is the difference between abstract class and interface in Dart?

### Answer
In Dart, the distinction is nuanced because **Dart has no separate `interface` keyword**. Every class implicitly defines an interface. The difference lies in how you use `extends` (abstract classes) versus `implements` (interfaces).

## Abstract Classes

Abstract classes are for **"is-a" relationships** with shared code. They can contain:
- Abstract methods (no implementation)
- Concrete methods (with implementation)
- Instance variables and constructors

```dart
abstract class Animal {
  String name;
  
  Animal(this.name);
  
  void eat(); // Abstract method
  
  void sleep() { // Concrete method
    print('$name is sleeping');
  }
}

class Dog extends Animal {
  Dog(String name) : super(name);
  
  @override
  void eat() => print('$name is eating bones');
}
```

## Interfaces

Any class can act as an interface. When you use `implements`, you must provide your own implementation for **every** method and getter—even if the original class had concrete implementations.

```dart
class Flyable {
  void fly() => print('Flying');
}

class Swimmable {
  void swim() => print('Swimming');
}

// Bird implements Flyable as an interface (not inheriting implementation)
class Bird implements Flyable {
  @override
  void fly() => print('Bird is flapping wings');
}

// Duck implements multiple interfaces
class Duck implements Flyable, Swimmable {
  @override
  void fly() => print('Duck flying');
  
  @override
  void swim() => print('Duck paddling');
}
```

## Key Differences

| Feature | Abstract Class (`extends`) | Interface (`implements`) |
|---------|---------------------------|-------------------------|
| **Inheritance** | Single inheritance only | Multiple allowed |
| **Implementation** | Inherits concrete methods | Must reimplement everything |
| **State** | Can have instance variables | Typically none (pure contract) |
| **Constructors** | Can define constructors | Cannot call super constructor |
| **Use Case** | Share code among related classes | Define contracts for unrelated classes |

## Practical Example

```dart
// Abstract class with shared logic
abstract class Repository {
  void log(String message) => print('[LOG] $message');
  Future<void> save(String data);
}

// Interface defining a contract
abstract class Cacheable {
  void clearCache();
  bool get isCached;
}

class UserRepository extends Repository implements Cacheable {
  @override
  Future<void> save(String data) async {
    log('Saving user...'); // Inherited from abstract class
    // implementation
  }
  
  @override
  void clearCache() { /* ... */ }
  
  @override
  bool get isCached => true;
}
```

**Rule of thumb**: Use `abstract class` when you want to share implementation; use `implements` when you need a class to satisfy multiple contracts without inheriting behavior.

### Question
How do I use extension methods in Dart?

### Answer
Extension methods in Dart let you add new methods (and getters/setters) to existing types without modifying their source code. They were introduced in Dart 2.7 and are widely used in Flutter for cleaner, more readable code.

### Basic Syntax

```dart
extension ExtensionName on Type {
  // instance members
  ReturnType methodName() { ... }

  // static members (Dart 2.13+)
  static ReturnType staticMethod() { ... }
}
```

### Practical Example (Flutter-friendly)

A very common pattern is extending `BuildContext` so you don’t have to write `Theme.of(context)` and `MediaQuery.of(context)` everywhere.

```dart
// lib/extensions/context_extensions.dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

extension ContextExtensions on BuildContext {
  ThemeData get theme => Theme.of(this);
  ColorScheme get colorScheme => theme.colorScheme;
  TextTheme get textTheme => theme.textTheme;

  double get screenWidth => MediaQuery.sizeOf(this).width;
  double get screenHeight => MediaQuery.sizeOf(this).height;

  /// Show a snackbar easily
  void showSnackBar(String message) {
    ScaffoldMessenger.of(this).showSnackBar(
