      SnackBar(content: Text(message)),
    );
  }
}
```

### Usage

```dart
import 'package:your_app/extensions/context_extensions.dart';

class MyWidget extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return ElevatedButton(
      onPressed: () {
        context.showSnackBar('Hello from extension!');
      },
      child: Text(
        'Press me',
        style: context.textTheme.titleLarge?.copyWith(
          color: context.colorScheme.primary,
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Another Common Example: String Extensions

```dart
extension StringExtensions on String {
  String get capitalize =>
      isNotEmpty ? '${this[0].toUpperCase()}${substring(1)}' : this;

  bool get isEmail => RegExp(r'^[\w-\.]+@([\w-]+\.)+[\w-]{2,4}$').hasMatch(this);
}
```

Usage:

```dart
final name = 'john'.capitalize; // "John"
final valid = 'test@email.com'.isEmail; // true
```

### Important Rules

1. **Import required**: The extension only becomes visible after you import the file where it’s defined.
2. **No conflicts**: If two extensions define the same member, you’ll get a compile error. You can resolve it by using the extension explicitly:
   ```dart
   StringExtensions('hello').capitalize();
   ```
3. **Null safety**: Extensions on nullable types (`String?`) are allowed, but you must handle null inside the extension.

### Best Practices in Flutter

- Keep extensions in a dedicated `extensions/` folder.
- Name them clearly (`ContextExtensions`, `StringExtensions`, etc.).


### Question
What is the difference between Map and List in Dart?

### Answer
**List vs Map in Dart**

### Quick Comparison

| Feature              | **List**                              | **Map**                                      |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|
| **Structure**        | Ordered collection of values          | Collection of **key-value** pairs            |
| **Access**           | By **index** (0, 1, 2...)             | By **key** (String, int, etc.)               |
| **Duplicates**       | Allowed                               | Keys must be **unique**                      |
| **Order**            | Maintains insertion order             | Maintains insertion order (`LinkedHashMap`)  |
| **Use Case**         | Arrays, lists of items                | Dictionaries, JSON objects, lookups          |

---

### 1. List Example

A `List` is an **ordered** collection accessed by index.

```dart
void main() {
  List<String> fruits = ['Apple', 'Banana', 'Mango'];

  print(fruits[0]);        // Apple
  print(fruits.length);    // 3

  // Adding items
  fruits.add('Orange');
  fruits.insert(1, 'Grapes');

  // Iterating
  for (var fruit in fruits) {
    print(fruit);
  }

  // Using map() on List
  List<String> upper = fruits.map((f) => f.toUpperCase()).toList();
}
```

---

### 2. Map Example

A `Map` stores data as **key-value** pairs.

```dart
void main() {
  Map<String, dynamic> user = {
    'name': 'John',
    'age': 30,
    'isAdmin': true,
  };

  print(user['name']);     // John
  print(user['age']);      // 30

  // Adding / Updating
  user['city'] = 'New York';
  user['age'] = 31;

  // Iterating
  user.forEach((key, value) {
    print('$key: $value');
  });

  // Using entries
  for (var entry in user.entries) {
    print('${entry.key} → ${entry.value}');
  }
}
```

---

### 3. Common Real-World Usage in Flutter

**List** – Displaying multiple items:

```dart
ListView.builder(
  itemCount: users.length,
  itemBuilder: (context, index) => Text(users[index].name),
)
```

**Map** – Working with JSON / API responses:

```dart
final Map<String, dynamic> response = jsonDecode(jsonString);
String name = response['name'];
```

---

### Summary

- Use **List** when you need an **ordered** collection and want to access items by position.
- Use **Map** when you need fast lookups using a **key** (especially with JSON data).

Would you like examples of converting between `List` and `Map

### Question
How do I use generics in Dart?

### Answer
**Generics in Dart** let you write reusable, type-safe code that works with different types. They are heavily used in Flutter (e.g., `List<T>`, `Future<T>`, `StatefulBuilder`, `ValueNotifier<T>`, etc.).

### 1. Generic Classes

```dart
class Box<T> {
  final T value;
  const Box(this.value);

  T get content => value;

  // You can also have methods that return the generic type
  Box<T> copyWith(T newValue) => Box(newValue);
}
```

**Usage:**

```dart
void main() {
  final intBox = Box<int>(42);
  final stringBox = Box<String>("Hello");

  print(intBox.content);     // 42
  print(stringBox.content);  // Hello
}
```

### 2. Generic Functions / Methods

```dart
T first<T>(List<T> items) {
  return items.first;
}

// Or with multiple type parameters
Map<K, V> zip<K, V>(List<K> keys, List<V> values) {
  return Map.fromIterables(keys, values);
}
```

**Usage:**

```dart
final names = first<String>(['Alice', 'Bob']);
final ages = first([25, 30]); // Type inferred as int
```

### 3. Type Constraints (`extends`)

You can restrict which types are allowed:

```dart
class NumberBox<T extends num> {
  final T value;
  NumberBox(this.value);

  T add(T other) => (value + other) as T;
}
```

```dart
final intBox = NumberBox<int>(10);
final doubleBox = NumberBox<double>(3.14);
// NumberBox<String>(...) // Compile error
```

You can also use `implements` or `extends` with multiple types using `&`:

```dart
class Something<T extends Object & Comparable<T>> {}
```

### 4. Generic Flutter Example

Here's a practical pattern often used in Flutter apps:

```dart
// Generic API Response wrapper
class ApiResponse<T> {
  final T? data;
  final String? error;
  final bool isLoading;

  const ApiResponse({this.data, this.error, this.isLoading = false});

  factory ApiResponse.loading() => const ApiResponse(isLoading: true);
  factory ApiResponse.success(T data) => ApiResponse(data: data);
  factory ApiResponse.failure(String error) => ApiResponse(error: error);
}

// Usage with a User model
class User {
  final String name;
  User(this.name);
}

void main() {
  final response = ApiResponse.success(User("John"));
  print(response.data?.name); // John
}
```

### 5. Using Generics with `typedef`

```dart
typedef JsonParser<T> = T Function(Map<String, dynamic> json);

class Repository<T> {
  final JsonParser<T> fromJson;
  Repository

### Question
What is a factory constructor in Dart?

### Answer
A factory constructor in Dart is a constructor that doesn't necessarily create a new instance of the class. It's declared with the `factory` keyword and must return an instance of the class (or a subtype).

Unlike generative constructors, factory constructors can:
- Return an existing (cached) instance
- Return a subtype of the class
- Perform logic before deciding what to return

## Basic Syntax

```dart
class Database {
  static Database? _instance;
  
  Database._internal(); // Private constructor
  
  factory Database() {
    _instance ??= Database._internal();
    return _instance!;
  }
}
```

## Common Use Cases

### 1. Singleton Pattern

```dart
class Logger {
  static final Logger _instance = Logger._internal();
  
  factory Logger() => _instance;
  
  Logger._internal();
  
  void log(String message) => print(message);
}
```

### 2. Returning Cached Instances

```dart
class Color {
  final int red, green, blue;
  static final Map<String, Color> _cache = {};
  
  Color._internal(this.red, this.green, this.blue);
  
  factory Color.rgb(int r, int g, int b) {
    final key = '$r,$g,$b';
    return _cache.putIfAbsent(key, () => Color._internal(r, g, b));
  }
}
```

### 3. Returning Subtypes (Polymorphism)

```dart
abstract class Animal {
  factory Animal(String type) {
    switch (type) {
      case 'dog':
        return Dog();
      case 'cat':
        return Cat();
      default:
        return UnknownAnimal();
    }
  }
  
  void speak();
}

class Dog implements Animal {
  @override
  void speak() => print('Woof');
}

class Cat implements Animal {
  @override
  void speak() => print('Meow');
}

class UnknownAnimal implements Animal {
  @override
  void speak() => print('...');
}
```

### 4. JSON Deserialization

```dart
class User {
  final String name;
  
  User(this.name);
  
  factory User.fromJson(Map<String, dynamic> json) {
    return User(json['name'] as String);
  }
}
```

**Key rule**: A factory constructor must return an instance—it cannot initialize fields directly with `this.fieldName`.

### Question
How do I use named constructors in Dart?

### Answer
**Named constructors** in Dart allow a class to have multiple constructors with different names. This is useful when you need several ways to create an object (e.g., from JSON, from another object, with default values, etc.).

### Basic Syntax

```dart
class User {
  final String name;
  final int age;

  // Unnamed (default) constructor
  User(this.name, this.age);

  // Named constructor
  User.guest()
      : name = 'Guest',
        age = 0;

  // Named constructor with parameters
  User.withAge(this.name, {required this.age});

  @override
  String toString() => 'User(name: $name, age: $age)';
}
```

### Usage Examples

```dart
void main() {
  // Using the unnamed constructor
  final user1 = User('Alice', 28);
  print(user1); // User(name: Alice, age: 28)

  // Using named constructors
  final guest = User.guest();
  print(guest); // User(name: Guest, age: 0)

  final user2 = User.withAge('Bob', age: 35);
  print(user2); // User(name: Bob, age: 35)
}
```

### Real-World Flutter Example

Here's a more practical example using a model class commonly seen in Flutter apps:

```dart
class Product {
  final String id;
  final String name;
  final double price;
  final String? imageUrl;

  Product({
    required this.id,
    required this.name,
    required this.price,
    this.imageUrl,
  });

  // Named constructor from JSON (very common in Flutter)
  factory Product.fromJson(Map<String, dynamic> json) {
    return Product(
      id: json['id'] as String,
      name: json['name'] as String,
      price: (json['price'] as num).toDouble(),
      imageUrl: json['imageUrl'] as String?,
    );
  }

  // Named constructor for an empty/default product
  Product.empty()
      : id = '',
        name = 'Unknown',
        price = 0.0,
        imageUrl = null;

  Map<String, dynamic> toJson() => {
        'id': id,
        'name': name,
        'price': price,
        'imageUrl': imageUrl,
      };
}
```

**Usage:**

```dart
final product = Product.fromJson({
  'id': 'p123',
  'name': 'Wireless Headphones',
  'price': 89.99,
  'imageUrl': 'https://example.com/headphones.jpg',
});

final emptyProduct = Product.empty();
```

### Key Points

- Named constructors **do not** replace the main constructor — they are additional.
- You can combine them with `factory` constructors (especially for JSON parsing).
- Use **initializer lists** (`: ...`)

### Question
What is the difference between == and identical() in Dart?

### Answer
In Dart, `==` and `identical()` serve different purposes:

*   `==` checks for **value equality** (by calling the `operator ==` method)
*   `identical()` checks for **reference identity** (whether two variables point to the exact same object in memory)

## Key Differences

### 1. Default Behavior
When a class doesn't override `==`, both behave the same way (reference equality):

```dart
class Person {
  final String name;
  Person(this.name);
}

void main() {
  var p1 = Person('Alice');
  var p2 = Person('Alice');
  var p3 = p1;

  print(p1 == p2);           // false (different instances)
  print(identical(p1, p2));  // false
  
  print(p1 == p3);           // true (same reference)
  print(identical(p1, p3));  // true
}
```

### 2. Overridden `==`
Many built-in types override `==` to compare values instead of references:

```dart
void main() {
  String a = 'hello';
  String b = 'hello';
  String c = String.fromCharCodes([104, 101, 108, 108, 111]); // 'hello'

  print(a == b);           // true (value equality)
  print(identical(a, b));  // true (often true due to string interning)

  print(a == c);           // true (value equality)
  print(identical(a, c));  // false (different objects)
}
```

### 3. Collections
Lists, Maps, and Sets compare references by default (even though they contain values):

```dart
void main() {
  var list1 = [1, 2, 3];
  var list2 = [1, 2, 3];

  print(list1 == list2);           // false (different list objects)
  print(identical(list1, list2));  // false
}
```

**Note:** Use `listEquals()`, `mapEquals()`, etc. from `package:flutter/foundation.dart` for deep equality checks.

## When to Use Which

| Use Case | Operator |
|----------|----------|
| Comparing primitive values (int, String, bool) | `==` |
| Checking if two variables reference the same object | `identical()` |
| Comparing custom objects by value | Override `==` (and `hashCode`) |
| Verifying object instantiation/caching | `identical()` |

## Summary

```dart
// Most common: use ==
if (user.id == 42) { ... }

// Rare: use identical() for reference checks
if (identical(singleton, instance)) { ... }
```

### Question
How do I use spread operator in Dart?

### Answer
The spread operator (`...`) was introduced in Dart 2.3. It allows you to insert multiple elements from one collection into another.

## Basic Syntax

```dart
// Without spread
List<int> listA = [1, 2, 3];
List<int> listB = [0, ...listA, 4]; // [0, 1, 2, 3, 4]

// With multiple spreads
List<int> combined = [...listA, ...listB];
```

## Null-Aware Spread (`...?`)

Use this when the collection might be null:

```dart
List<int>? nullableList;

List<int> result = [1, 2, ...?nullableList]; // Safe, won't throw if null
```

## Flutter Widget Examples

### 1. Conditional Widgets in a List

```dart
Column(
  children: [
    HeaderWidget(),
    ...showDetails ? [DetailWidget(), FooterWidget()] : [],
    // or using null-aware
    ...?showDetails ? [DetailWidget(), FooterWidget()] : null,
  ],
)
```

### 2. Combining Lists of Widgets

```dart
final List<Widget> staticItems = [Item1(), Item2()];
final List<Widget> dynamicItems = items.map((item) => ItemWidget(item)).toList();

return ListView(
  children: [
    ...staticItems,
    ...dynamicItems,
    AddButton(),
  ],
);
```

### 3. With Maps and Sets

```dart
Map<String, dynamic> defaults = {'color': 'blue', 'size': 12};
Map<String, dynamic> overrides = {'size': 16};

Map<String, dynamic> config = {...defaults, ...overrides}; // {'color': 'blue', 'size': 16}
```

## Best Practices

- **Prefer null-aware** (`...?`) when the source might be null to avoid runtime errors
- **Use for conditional rendering** instead of `if` statements inside collections when the logic is simple
- **Avoid deep nesting** — spread operators are most readable when combining flat lists

```dart
// Clean and readable
children: [
  ...headerWidgets,
  MainContent(),
  ...footerWidgets,
]
```

### Question
What is late keyword in Dart?

### Answer
The `late` keyword in Dart is used for **lazy initialization** of variables. It tells the compiler: "This variable will be initialized later, before it's used."

## Primary Use Cases

### 1. Non-nullable variables initialized later
This is the most common use—declaring non-nullable instance variables that aren't set in the constructor or initializer list.

```dart
class UserService {
  late String userId; // Will be set before use

  void initialize(String id) {
    userId = id;
  }

  void printUser() {
    print(userId); // Safe if initialize() was called first
  }
}
```

### 2. Lazy initialization (performance optimization)
The variable isn't initialized until it's first accessed. This is useful for expensive operations.

```dart
class DataProcessor {
  late final List<int> _expensiveData = _loadData(); // Only runs when first accessed

  List<int> _loadData() {
    print('Loading data...'); // Demonstrates lazy behavior
    return List.generate(1000000, (i) => i);
  }

  void process() {
    print(_expensiveData.length); // _loadData() executes here, not at construction
  }
}
```

### 3. Flutter example (StatefulWidget)
Common pattern for accessing `widget` properties or `context` before `initState`:

```dart
class ProfileScreen extends StatefulWidget {
  final String userId;
  const ProfileScreen({super.key, required this.userId});

  @override
  State<ProfileScreen> createState() => _ProfileScreenState();
}

class _ProfileScreenState extends State<ProfileScreen> {
  late final UserRepository _repository; // Initialized in initState
  late String displayName; // Set after fetching data

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _repository = UserRepository(widget.userId);
    displayName = 'Loading...';
  }

  @override
  void didChangeDependencies() {
    super.didChangeDependencies();
    // Safe to use context here
    final theme = Theme.of(context);
  }
}
```

## Important Warnings

- **Runtime error**: Accessing a `late` variable before initialization throws `LateInitializationError`
- **No null safety**: Dart skips null checks on `late` variables, so improper use causes crashes
- **Final with late**: You can combine them (`late final`) to ensure single assignment

```dart
late String name;
void main() {
  print(name); // ❌ LateInitializationError: Field 'name' has not been initialized.
}
```

### Question
How do I use typedef in Dart?

### Answer
**Typedef in Dart** is used to create a **type alias** — most commonly for function types. This makes your code more readable, especially when dealing with callbacks.

### Basic Syntax (Modern Way)

```dart
typedef MyCallback = void Function(String value);
```

### Practical Example

Here's a clean example of using `typedef` in a Flutter widget:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

// Define a typedef for a callback
typedef OnItemSelected = void Function(String item, int index);

class ItemList extends StatelessWidget {
  final List<String> items;
  final OnItemSelected onItemSelected;

  const ItemList({
    super.key,
    required this.items,
    required this.onItemSelected,
  });

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return ListView.builder(
      itemCount: items.length,
      itemBuilder: (context, index) {
        return ListTile(
          title: Text(items[index]),
          onTap: () => onItemSelected(items[index], index),
        );
      },
    );
  }
}

// Usage
class HomePage extends StatelessWidget {
  const HomePage({super.key});

  void _handleSelection(String item, int index) {
    print('Selected: $item at index $index');
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: ItemList(
        items: ['Apple', 'Banana', 'Cherry'],
        onItemSelected: _handleSelection,
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### Other Useful Examples

**1. Typedef with return value**
```dart
typedef Validator = String? Function(String? value);

final Validator emailValidator = (value) {
  if (value == null || value.isEmpty) return 'Required';
  return null;
};
```

**2. Generic typedef**
```dart
typedef Mapper<T, R> = R Function(T input);

final Mapper<String, int> stringToLength = (s) => s.length;
```

**3. Multiple parameters**
```dart
typedef OnUserAction = void Function({
  required String action,
  Map<String, dynamic>? data,
});
```

### Old vs New Syntax

| Style | Syntax | Recommended |
|-------|--------|-------------|
| **New** (Dart 2.13+) | `typedef MyType = void Function(int);` | Yes |
| **Old** | `typedef void MyType(int);` | No |

### When to Use Typedef

- When you have complex function signatures used in multiple places
- To improve code readability
- When passing callbacks through multiple widgets
- For better documentation of expected function shapes

Would you like examples of using `typedef` with `Future`, `Stream`, or in combination with `typedef` for classes?

### Question
What is cascade notation in Dart?

### Answer
**Cascade notation** (`..`) in Dart allows you to perform a sequence of operations on the same object without repeating the object reference. It returns the original object, enabling method chaining and property assignments in a fluent style.

### Basic Syntax

```dart
object
  ..property = value
  ..method()
  ..anotherMethod();
```

### Example Without Cascade (Repetitive)

```dart
final paint = Paint();
paint.color = Colors.blue;
paint.strokeWidth = 4.0;
paint.style = PaintingStyle.stroke;
```

### Example With Cascade (Cleaner)

```dart
final paint = Paint()
  ..color = Colors.blue
  ..strokeWidth = 4.0
  ..style = PaintingStyle.stroke;
```

### Common Flutter Use Cases

**1. Configuring `TextStyle`**

```dart
Text(
  'Hello Flutter',
  style: TextStyle()
    ..color = Colors.white
    ..fontSize = 24
    ..fontWeight = FontWeight.bold
    ..letterSpacing = 1.2,
)
```

**2. Building complex objects**

```dart
final button = ElevatedButton(
  onPressed: () {},
  style: ButtonStyle()
    ..backgroundColor = MaterialStateProperty.all(Colors.blue)
    ..padding = MaterialStateProperty.all(const EdgeInsets.all(16))
    ..shape = MaterialStateProperty.all(
      RoundedRectangleBorder(borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(8)),
    ),
  child: const Text('Submit'),
);
```

**3. Null-aware cascade (`?..`)**

Use `?..` when the object might be null:

```dart
user
  ?..updateProfile()
  ?..sendNotification()
  ?..logActivity();
```

If `user` is `null`, the entire chain is skipped safely.

### Key Points

- The cascade returns the **original object**, not the result of the last operation.
- You can mix property assignments and method calls.
- It improves readability when initializing or configuring objects with many properties.
- Avoid overusing it — simple cases are often clearer with regular assignment.

**Tip**: In Flutter, cascades are especially useful when working with `Paint`, `TextStyle`, `Path`, `Canvas`, and widget styling objects.

### Question
How do I sort a list in Dart?

### Answer
To sort a list in Dart, use the `sort()` method. Here are the most common approaches:

## Basic Sorting

```dart
List<int> numbers = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9];
numbers.sort();
print(numbers); // [1, 1, 3, 4, 5, 9]
```

## Descending Order

```dart
numbers.sort((a, b) => b.compareTo(a));
// or
numbers.sort((a, b) => -a.compareTo(b));
```

## Sorting Objects

```dart
class Person {
  final String name;
  final int age;
  
  Person(this.name, this.age);
  
  @override
  String toString() => '$name ($age)';
}

List<Person> people = [
  Person('Alice', 30),
  Person('Bob', 25),
  Person('Charlie', 35),
];

// Sort by age ascending
people.sort((a, b) => a.age.compareTo(b.age));

// Sort by name alphabetically
people.sort((a, b) => a.name.compareTo(b.name));
```

## Multiple Criteria

```dart
people.sort((a, b) {
  int ageCompare = a.age.compareTo(b.age);
  if (ageCompare != 0) return ageCompare;
  return a.name.compareTo(b.name); // Secondary sort
});
```

## Non-Mutating Sort (Create New List)

```dart
List<int> original = [3, 1, 4, 1, 5];
List<int> sorted = [...original]..sort();
// or
List<int> sorted2 = List.of(original)..sort();
```

## Sorting Strings Case-Insensitive

```dart
List<String> words = ['Banana', 'apple', 'Cherry'];
words.sort((a, b) => a.toLowerCase().compareTo(b.toLowerCase()));
```

**Note:** `sort()` modifies the list in place and returns `void`. If you need to preserve the original order, create a copy first.

### Question
How do I filter a list in Dart?

### Answer
**Filtering a list in Dart** is done with the `where` method. It returns an `Iterable`, so you usually call `.toList()` at the end if you need a `List`.

### Basic Example

```dart
void main() {
  List<int> numbers = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8];

  // Filter even numbers
  List<int> evens = numbers.where((n) => n % 2 == 0).toList();

  print(evens); // [2, 4, 6, 8]
}
```

### Real-World Flutter Example (Filtering a List of Objects)

```dart
class Product {
  final String name;
  final double price;
  final bool inStock;

  Product({required this.name, required this.price, required this.inStock});
}

void main() {
  List<Product> products = [
    Product(name: 'Laptop', price: 999.99, inStock: true),
    Product(name: 'Mouse', price: 29.99, inStock: false),
    Product(name: 'Keyboard', price: 79.99, inStock: true),
    Product(name: 'Monitor', price: 199.99, inStock: true),
  ];

  // Filter products that are in stock and cost less than $100
  List<Product> affordableInStock = products
      .where((p) => p.inStock && p.price < 100)
      .toList();

  for (var product in affordableInStock) {
    print('${product.name} - \$${product.price}');
  }
}
```

### Other Useful Filtering Methods

| Method          | Use Case                              | Returns          |
|-----------------|---------------------------------------|------------------|
| `where()`       | Filter by condition                   | `Iterable`       |
| `whereType<T>()`| Filter by type                        | `Iterable<T>`    |
| `firstWhere()`  | Get first matching item               | Single item      |
| `lastWhere()`   | Get last matching item                | Single item      |
| `singleWhere()` | Get single matching item (must exist) | Single item      |

**Example with `whereType`:**

```dart
List<dynamic> mixed = [1, 'hello', 3.14, 42, 'world'];

List<int> onlyInts = mixed.whereType<int>().toList();
// Result: [1, 42]
```

### Pro Tips

- Use **arrow functions** (`=>`) for simple filters.
- For complex logic, use a **block body**:

```dart
.where((product) {
  final isExpensive = product.price > 500;
  final isPopular = product.name.length > 5;
  return isExpensive || isPopular;
}).toList();
```

- If you want to **mutate** the original list

### Question
How do I map a list in Dart?

### Answer
**Mapping a List in Dart**

In Dart, you use the `map()` method to transform every element in a list. It returns an `Iterable`, so you usually call `.toList()` at the end if you need a `List`.

### Basic Example

```dart
void main() {
  List<int> numbers = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5];

  // Double each number
  List<int> doubled = numbers.map((n) => n * 2).toList();

  print(doubled); // [2, 4, 6, 8, 10]
}
```

### Mapping to a Different Type

```dart
List<String> names = ['Alice', 'Bob', 'Charlie'];

List<String> upperNames = names.map((name) => name.toUpperCase()).toList();
print(upperNames); // [ALICE, BOB, CHARLIE]
```

### Real-World Flutter Example (Mapping to Widgets)

```dart
List<User> users = [
  User(id: 1, name: 'Alice'),
  User(id: 2, name: 'Bob'),
];

List<Widget> userTiles = users.map((user) {
  return ListTile(
    title: Text(user.name),
    subtitle: Text('ID: ${user.id}'),
  );
}).toList();
```

### Using Index (with `asMap()` or `enumerate`)

Dart doesn't have a built-in index in `map()`, but you can do this:

```dart
List<String> items = ['Apple', 'Banana', 'Cherry'];

List<String> indexed = items
    .asMap()
    .entries
    .map((entry) => '${entry.key}: ${entry.value}')
    .toList();

print(indexed); // [0: Apple, 1: Banana, 2: Cherry]
```

### Important Notes

- `map()` is **lazy** — it doesn't execute until you consume the result (e.g., `.toList()`, `.forEach()`, etc.).
- Always use `.toList()` when you need a `List` instead of an `Iterable`.
- For simple transformations, prefer `map()`. For side effects, use `forEach()`.

Would you like examples for more complex cases like filtering + mapping (`where().map()`) or using `map()` with async operations?

### Question
How do I use Set in Dart?

### Answer
**Set in Dart** is an unordered collection of **unique** items (no duplicates allowed). It's part of Dart's core `dart:core` library.

### 1. Creating a Set

```dart
// Using set literal (recommended)
Set<String> fruits = {'apple', 'banana', 'orange'};

// Using Set constructor
Set<int> numbers = Set<int>();

// From a list (duplicates are automatically removed)
Set<String> uniqueNames = {'John', 'Jane', 'John'}.toSet();
```

### 2. Basic Operations

```dart
void main() {
  Set<String> colors = {'red', 'green', 'blue'};

  // Add elements
  colors.add('yellow');
  colors.addAll({'black', 'white'});

  // Check if contains
  print(colors.contains('red')); // true

  // Remove elements
  colors.remove('green');
  colors.removeAll({'black', 'white'});

  // Length and emptiness
  print(colors.length);      // 4
  print(colors.isEmpty);     // false
  print(colors.isNotEmpty);  // true

  // Clear all
  // colors.clear();
}
```

### 3. Iterating Over a Set

```dart
Set<String> fruits = {'apple', 'banana', 'orange'};

// Using for-in
for (var fruit in fruits) {
  print(fruit);
}

// Using forEach
fruits.forEach((fruit) => print(fruit));

// Using iterator
for (var it = fruits.iterator; it.moveNext();) {
  print(it.current);
}
```

### 4. Set Operations (Union, Intersection, Difference)

```dart
void main() {
  Set<int> setA = {1, 2, 3, 4};
  Set<int> setB = {3, 4, 5, 6};

  // Union (all elements)
  print(setA.union(setB));        // {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}

  // Intersection (common elements)
  print(setA.intersection(setB)); // {3, 4}

  // Difference (elements in A but not in B)
  print(setA.difference(setB));   // {1, 2}
}
```

### 5. Useful Methods

| Method              | Description                          | Example                     |
|---------------------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------|
| `add()`             | Add single element                   | `set.add(10)`               |
| `addAll()`          | Add multiple elements                | `set.addAll([1,2,3])`       |
| `remove()`          | Remove element                       | `set.remove(5)`             |
| `contains()`        | Check existence                      | `set.contains(3)`           |
| `lookup()`          | Get element or null                  | `set.lookup

### Question
How do I use Map in Dart?

### Answer
Here's how to use `Map` in Dart effectively:

## Basic Usage

```dart
// Using map literal (most common)
final Map<String, int> ages = {
  'Alice': 30,
  'Bob': 25,
};

// Type inference works too
final scores = <String, double>{'math': 95.5, 'science': 88.0};

// Empty map
final Map<String, String> emptyMap = {};
```

## Common Operations

```dart
void main() {
  final user = <String, dynamic>{
    'name': 'John',
    'age': 30,
    'isActive': true,
  };

  // Add/Update
  user['email'] = 'john@example.com';
  user['age'] = 31; // Updates existing key

  // Access (returns null if key doesn't exist)
  print(user['name']); // John

  // Safe access with default
  final city = user['city'] ?? 'Unknown';

  // Check existence
  if (user.containsKey('email')) {
    print('Has email');
  }

  // Remove
  user.remove('isActive');

  // Bulk operations
  user.addAll({'phone': '123-456', 'city': 'NYC'});
}
```

## Iterating

```dart
final products = {'apple': 1.5, 'banana': 0.75, 'orange': 2.0};

// Using entries (recommended)
for (final entry in products.entries) {
  print('${entry.key}: \$${entry.value}');
}

// Using forEach
products.forEach((key, value) {
  print('$key = $value');
});

// Transform with map()
final discounted = products.map((key, value) => 
  MapEntry(key, value * 0.9));
```

## Useful Methods

```dart
final map = {'a': 1, 'b': 2, 'c': 3};

// Get keys/values as iterables
print(map.keys);   // (a, b, c)
print(map.values); // (1, 2, 3)

// Filter
final filtered = Map.fromEntries(
  map.entries.where((e) => e.value > 1)
);

// Merge maps (Dart 2.3+)
final map1 = {'a': 1};
final map2 = {'b': 2};
final merged = {...map1, ...map2}; // {'a': 1, 'b': 2}
```

## Flutter Example

```dart
// Parsing JSON response
Map<String, dynamic> parseUser(Map<String, dynamic> json) {
  return {
    'id': json['id'] as int,
    'name': json['name'] as String,
    'tags': (json['tags'] as List).cast<String>(),
  };
}
```

**Note:** Use `Map<String, dynamic>` when

### Question
What is the difference between dynamic and Object in Dart?

### Answer
The key difference between `dynamic` and `Object` in Dart lies in **static type safety**:

- `Object` is the root of the Dart type system. It is **statically typed** — the compiler enforces that you can only call methods that exist on `Object` unless you explicitly cast.
- `dynamic` disables static type checking. The compiler allows **any** method call, and errors only occur at runtime if the method doesn't exist.

### Comparison Table

| Aspect                    | `Object`                              | `dynamic`                              |
|---------------------------|---------------------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| Static type checking      | Yes                                   | No                                     |
| Method calls              | Only methods from `Object` allowed    | Any method allowed (checked at runtime)|
| Compile-time safety       | High                                  | Low                                    |
| Runtime error risk        | Low                                   | High (if method doesn't exist)         |
| Default when no type      | Not default                           | Default for untyped variables          |

### Code Examples

```dart
void main() {
  // Using Object
  Object obj = 'Hello World';
  
  // Allowed (defined on Object)
  print(obj.toString());
  
  // Not allowed at compile time
  // obj.toUpperCase(); // Error: The method 'toUpperCase' isn't defined for 'Object'
  
  // Must cast to call String methods
  if (obj is String) {
    print(obj.toUpperCase()); // Hello WORLD
  }

  // Using dynamic
  dynamic dyn = 'Hello World';
  
  // Allowed at compile time (no type checking)
  print(dyn.toUpperCase()); // Hello WORLD
  
  // Dangerous - compiles but fails at runtime
  dynamic number = 42;
  // number.toUpperCase(); // Runtime error: NoSuchMethodError
}
```

### Practical Example (Common in Flutter)

```dart
// Parsing JSON (very common pattern)
Map<String, dynamic> json = {
  'name': 'John',
  'age': 30,
};

// 'dynamic' is useful here because values can be different types
String name = json['name']; // Works
int age = json['age'];      // Works
```

### When to Use Which?

- **Use `Object`** when you want type safety but need to accept multiple types (e.g., a function parameter that accepts any object but still wants compile-time protection).
- **Use `dynamic`** when you genuinely don't know the type at compile time (JSON parsing, platform channels, working with JavaScript interop, etc.).

**Best Practice**: Avoid `dynamic` when possible. Use `Object?` or specific types instead, and only fall back to `dynamic` when you have no other choice.

### Question
What is Clean Architecture in Flutter?

### Answer
Clean Architecture in Flutter is a way of organizing code so that business logic is completely independent of frameworks, UI, databases, or external services. It follows the principles popularized by Robert C. Martin (Uncle Bob), with the key rule that **dependencies point inward**.

### Core Idea

The architecture is divided into layers (from inside to outside):

| Layer          | Responsibility                          | Dependencies          | Example Contents                     |
|----------------|-----------------------------------------|-----------------------|--------------------------------------|
| **Domain**     | Business rules & entities               | None                  | Entities, Use Cases, Repository interfaces, Failures |
| **Data**       | Data sources & implementation           | Domain                | Repository implementations, Remote/Local data sources, Models |
| **Presentation**| UI & state management                   | Domain                | Widgets, Pages, BLoC/Cubit/Riverpod, ViewModels |

### Recommended Folder Structure

```
lib/
├── core/
│   ├── error/           # Failures
│   ├── usecases/        # Base UseCase class
│   └── utils/
├── features/
│   └── auth/
│       ├── data/
│       │   ├── datasources/
│       │   ├── models/
│       │   └── repositories/
│       ├── domain/
│       │   ├── entities/
│       │   ├── repositories/   # Abstract repository
│       │   └── usecases/
│       └── presentation/
│           ├── bloc/
│           └── pages/
└── main.dart
```

### Key Components with Code Examples

#### 1. Domain Layer (Pure Dart - No Flutter dependencies)

**Entity**
```dart
class User {
  final String id;
  final String name;
  final String email;

  const User({required this.id, required this.name, required this.email});
}
```

**Repository Interface**
```dart
abstract class AuthRepository {
  Future<Either<Failure, User>> login(String email, String password);
}
```

**Use Case**
```dart
class LoginUseCase {
  final AuthRepository repository;

  LoginUseCase(this.repository);

  Future<Either<Failure, User>> call(String email, String password) {
    return repository.login(email, password);
  }
}
```

#### 2. Data Layer

```dart
class AuthRepositoryImpl implements AuthRepository {
  final AuthRemoteDataSource remoteDataSource;
  final AuthLocalDataSource localDataSource;

  AuthRepositoryImpl({
    required this.remoteDataSource,
    required this.localDataSource,
  });

  @override
  Future<Either<Failure, User>> login(String email, String password) async {
    try {
      final userModel = await remoteDataSource.login(email, password);
      await localDataSource.cacheUser(userModel);
      return Right(userModel.toEntity());
    } on ServerException {
      return Left(ServerFailure());
    }
  }
}
```

#### 3. Presentation Layer (using BLoC)

```dart
class LoginBloc

### Question
What is MVVM in Flutter?

### Answer
MVVM (Model-View-ViewModel) is an architectural pattern that separates your UI (View) from business logic and state (ViewModel). In Flutter, it helps manage state predictably and makes widgets easier to test.

**The Three Layers:**

- **Model**: Pure data classes (no business logic)
- **ViewModel**: Holds state, business logic, and exposes data to the View. Extends `ChangeNotifier` or uses `StateNotifier`
- **View**: Flutter widgets that observe the ViewModel and rebuild when notified

**Practical Implementation**

Here's a counter example using `ChangeNotifier` (built-in) with the `provider` package:

```dart
// 1. MODEL
class Counter {
  final int value;
  Counter(this.value);
}

// 2. VIEWMODEL
class CounterViewModel extends ChangeNotifier {
  int _count = 0;
  
  int get count => _count;
  bool get isEven => _count % 2 == 0;
  
  void increment() {
    _count++;
    notifyListeners(); // Triggers UI rebuild
  }
  
  void reset() {
    _count = 0;
    notifyListeners();
  }
}

// 3. VIEW
class CounterPage extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text('MVVM Counter')),
      body: Center(
        child: Consumer<CounterViewModel>(
          builder: (context, vm, child) {
            return Column(
              mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
              children: [
                Text('${vm.count}', style: TextStyle(fontSize: 48)),
                Text(vm.isEven ? 'Even' : 'Odd'),
              ],
            );
          },
        ),
      ),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () => context.read<CounterViewModel>().increment(),
        child: Icon(Icons.add),
      ),
    );
  }
}

// Setup in main.dart
void main() {
  runApp(
    ChangeNotifierProvider(
      create: (_) => CounterViewModel(),
      child: MaterialApp(home: CounterPage()),
    ),
  );
}
```

**Key Points:**

- The View knows nothing about how state is stored—it only consumes the ViewModel
- Business logic lives in the ViewModel, making it unit-testable without widgets
- Use `context.watch<T>()` for automatic rebuilds or `context.read<T>()` for one-time actions
- For more complex apps, consider **Riverpod** (modern alternative) or **Stacked** package which provides a more opinionated MVVM implementation

### Question
How do I implement repository pattern in Flutter?

### Answer
The Repository Pattern abstracts data sources (API, local DB, cache) from your business logic. Here's a practical Flutter implementation:

## 1. Define the Abstract Repository

```dart
abstract class UserRepository {
  Future<List<User>> getUsers();
  Future<User> getUserById(String id);
  Future<void> createUser(User user);
}
```

## 2. Implement with Data Sources

```dart
class UserRepositoryImpl implements UserRepository {
  final UserRemoteDataSource _remote;
  final UserLocalDataSource _local;

  UserRepositoryImpl({
    required UserRemoteDataSource remote,
    required UserLocalDataSource local,
  })  : _remote = remote,
        _local = local;

  @override
  Future<List<User>> getUsers() async {
    try {
      // Try network first
      final users = await _remote.fetchUsers();
      await _local.cacheUsers(users);
      return users;
    } catch (_) {
      // Fallback to cache
      return _local.getCachedUsers();
    }
  }

  @override
  Future<User> getUserById(String id) async {
    try {
      return await _remote.fetchUserById(id);
    } catch (_) {
      return _local.getCachedUserById(id);
    }
  }

  @override
  Future<void> createUser(User user) async {
    await _remote.createUser(user);
    await _local.cacheUser(user);
  }
}
```

## 3. Data Source Interfaces

```dart
abstract class UserRemoteDataSource {
  Future<List<User>> fetchUsers();
  Future<User> fetchUserById(String id);
  Future<void> createUser(User user);
}

abstract class UserLocalDataSource {
  Future<List<User>> getCachedUsers();
  Future<User> getCachedUserById(String id);
  Future<void> cacheUsers(List<User> users);
  Future<void> cacheUser(User user);
}
```

## 4. Usage with Riverpod

```dart
final userRepositoryProvider = Provider<UserRepository>((ref) {
  return UserRepositoryImpl(
    remote: ref.watch(remoteDataSourceProvider),
    local: ref.watch(localDataSourceProvider),
  );
});

final usersProvider = FutureProvider<List<User>>((ref) async {
  final repo = ref.watch(userRepositoryProvider);
  return repo.getUsers();
});
```

## 5. Concrete Implementations (Example)

```dart
class UserRemoteDataSourceImpl implements UserRemoteDataSource {
  final Dio _dio;
  UserRemoteDataSourceImpl(this._dio);

  @override
  Future<List<User>> fetchUsers() async {
    final response = await _dio.get('/users');
    return (response.data as List).map((e) => User.fromJson(e)).toList();
  }
}

class UserLocalDataSourceImpl implements UserLocalDataSource {
  final Hive.Box<User> _box;
  UserLocalDataSourceImpl

### Question
How do I use dependency injection in Flutter?

### Answer
**Dependency Injection in Flutter** is typically done with packages, since Flutter has no built-in DI container. The most common approaches are:

- **Provider** (simple, official, great for widget tree)
- **get_it** (service locator, excellent for non-widget classes)
- **Riverpod** (modern, powerful, recommended for new projects)

Below are practical, working examples of the two most widely used approaches.

### 1. Using `provider` (Recommended for most apps)

```yaml
dependencies:
  provider: ^6.1.1
```

**Define a service**

```dart
class ApiService {
  Future<String> fetchData() async => 'Data from API';
}

class UserRepository {
  final ApiService api;
  UserRepository(this.api);

  Future<String> getUser() => api.fetchData();
}
```

**Provide dependencies at the top of the widget tree**

```dart
void main() {
  runApp(
    MultiProvider(
      providers: [
        Provider<ApiService>(create: (_) => ApiService()),
        ProxyProvider<ApiService, UserRepository>(
          update: (_, api, __) => UserRepository(api),
        ),
      ],
      child: const MyApp(),
    ),
  );
}
```

**Consume dependencies**

```dart
class HomePage extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final userRepo = context.read<UserRepository>();

    return Scaffold(
      body: FutureBuilder(
        future: userRepo.getUser(),
        builder: (context, snapshot) {
          return Text(snapshot.data ?? 'Loading...');
        },
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Using `get_it` (Service Locator Pattern)

Best when you need dependencies outside the widget tree (e.g., in repositories, use cases, BLoCs).

```yaml
dependencies:
  get_it: ^7.6.3
```

**Setup (`service_locator.dart`)**

```dart
import 'package:get_it/get_it.dart';

final getIt = GetIt.instance;

void setupDependencies() {
  getIt.registerLazySingleton<ApiService>(() => ApiService());
  getIt.registerLazySingleton<UserRepository>(
    () => UserRepository(getIt<ApiService>()),
  );
}
```

**Register at app start**

```dart
void main() {
  setupDependencies();
  runApp(const MyApp());
}
```

**Use anywhere**

```dart
class ProfileScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final repo = getIt<UserRepository>();

    return FutureBuilder(
      future: repo.getUser(),
      builder: (context, snapshot) => Text(snapshot.data ?? ''),
    );
  }
}
```

### Bonus: Using `injectable` with `get_it` (Recommended for larger apps)

For automatic registration using code generation:

```yaml
dependencies:
  get_it: ^7.6.3
  injectable: ^

### Question
What is GetIt in Flutter?

### Answer
GetIt is a **service locator** (dependency injection) library for Dart/Flutter. It allows you to register and access dependencies (services, repositories, BLoCs, etc.) globally without passing them through constructors or widget trees.

Unlike Provider or Riverpod (which combine state management with DI), GetIt is purely a service locator — it doesn't manage state or notify widgets of changes.

## Core Concepts

```dart
import 'package:get_it/get_it.dart';

final getIt = GetIt.instance;

void setup() {
  // Singleton: one instance for the entire app
  getIt.registerSingleton<ApiService>(ApiService());
  
  // Lazy Singleton: created only when first accessed
  getIt.registerLazySingleton<Database>(() => Database());
  
  // Factory: new instance every time it's requested
  getIt.registerFactory<CounterBloc>(() => CounterBloc());
}
```

## Typical Setup

```dart
// main.dart
void main() {
  setupDependencies();
  runApp(MyApp());
}

void setupDependencies() {
  getIt.registerSingleton<AuthRepository>(AuthRepositoryImpl());
  getIt.registerSingleton<UserService>(
    UserService(getIt<AuthRepository>())  // resolve dependency
  );
}
```

## Usage in Widgets

```dart
class ProfilePage extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final userService = getIt<UserService>();
    
    return FutureBuilder(
      future: userService.getCurrentUser(),
      builder: (context, snapshot) => ...,
    );
  }
}
```

## With Async Initialization

```dart
getIt.registerSingletonAsync<Database>(
  () async {
    final db = Database();
    await db.init();
    return db;
  },
);

// Later, ensure it's ready before using
await getIt.isReady<Database>();
```

## When to Use GetIt

- You want **decoupled** classes without widget tree dependencies
- You're using BLoC/Cubit or other patterns and need a way to access repositories/services
- You prefer manual wiring over code generation (though it pairs well with `injectable`)
- You need different lifecycles (singleton vs factory) for different services

## Note

GetIt does **not** rebuild widgets when dependencies change. If you need reactive state, combine it with `Provider`, `Riverpod`, or `ValueNotifier`/`ChangeNotifier`.

### Question
How do I use injectable in Flutter?

### Answer
**Using `injectable` in Flutter** (with `get_it`)

`injectable` is a compile-time dependency injection generator for `get_it`. It removes manual registration boilerplate.

### 1. Add Dependencies

```yaml
# pubspec.yaml
dependencies:
  get_it: ^7.6.0
  injectable: ^2.3.0

dev_dependencies:
  build_runner: ^2.4.6
  injectable_generator: ^2.4.0
```

### 2. Project Structure (Recommended)

```
lib/
├── di/
│   ├── injection.dart          # GetIt instance + init function
│   └── injection.config.dart   # Generated (do not edit)
├── services/
│   ├── api_service.dart
│   └── database_service.dart
└── main.dart
```

### 3. Create the DI Setup

**`lib/di/injection.dart`**

```dart
import 'package:get_it/get_it.dart';
import 'package:injectable/injectable.dart';
import 'injection.config.dart';

final getIt = GetIt.instance;

@InjectableInit(
  initializerName: 'init', // default
  preferRelativeImports: true,
  asExtension: false,
)
void configureDependencies() => init(getIt);
```

### 4. Annotate Your Classes

```dart
// Singleton
@singleton
class ApiService {
  ApiService();
}

// Lazy Singleton (created only when first requested)
@lazySingleton
class DatabaseService {
  DatabaseService();
}

// Factory (new instance every time)
@injectable
class UserRepository {
  final ApiService api;
  final DatabaseService db;

  UserRepository(this.api, this.db);
}

// Named injection
@injectable
class AuthService {
  final ApiService api;

  @Named('baseUrl')
  final String baseUrl;

  AuthService(this.api, @Named('baseUrl') this.baseUrl);
}
```

### 5. Register External Dependencies (Modules)

Create a module for things you can't annotate (third-party libs, primitives, etc.):

```dart
@module
abstract class RegisterModule {
  @singleton
  Dio get dio => Dio();

  @Named('baseUrl')
  String get baseUrl => 'https://api.example.com';
}
```

### 6. Generate the Code

Run this command:

```bash
dart run build_runner build --delete-conflicting-outputs
```

Or watch mode during development:

```bash
dart run build_runner watch --delete-conflicting-outputs
```

This generates `injection.config.dart`.

### 7. Initialize in `main()`

```dart
void main() {
  configureDependencies();
  runApp(MyApp());
}
```

### 8. Using Injected Dependencies

```dart
class MyScreen extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final userRepo = getIt<UserRepository>();
    final api = getIt<Api

### Question
How do I write unit tests in Flutter?

### Answer
**Unit tests** in Flutter test pure Dart logic (no widgets). They live in the `test/` folder and use the `test` package (included via `flutter_test`).

### 1. Project Setup

Add the following if not already present in `pubspec.yaml`:

```yaml
dev_dependencies:
  flutter_test:
    sdk: flutter
  # Optional but recommended for mocking
  mocktail: ^1.0.0
```

### 2. Basic Unit Test Example

**File to test** (`lib/calculator.dart`):

```dart
class Calculator {
  int add(int a, int b) => a + b;
  int subtract(int a, int b) => a - b;
  double divide(int a, int b) {
    if (b == 0) throw ArgumentError('Cannot divide by zero');
    return a / b;
  }
}
```

**Test file** (`test/calculator_test.dart`):

```dart
import 'package:flutter_test/flutter_test.dart';
import 'package:my_app/calculator.dart';

void main() {
  late Calculator calculator;

  setUp(() {
    calculator = Calculator();
  });

  group('Calculator', () {
    test('adds two numbers correctly', () {
      expect(calculator.add(2, 3), 5);
      expect(calculator.add(-1, 1), 0);
    });

    test('subtracts two numbers correctly', () {
      expect(calculator.subtract(10, 4), 6);
    });

    test('throws error when dividing by zero', () {
      expect(() => calculator.divide(10, 0), throwsArgumentError);
    });

    test('divides numbers correctly', () {
      expect(calculator.divide(10, 2), 5.0);
    });
  });
}
```

### 3. Running Tests

```bash
# Run all tests
flutter test

# Run specific test file
flutter test test/calculator_test.dart

# Run with coverage
flutter test --coverage
```

### 4. Useful Patterns

**Async testing**:

```dart
test('fetches data asynchronously', () async {
  final result = await fetchUser();
  expect(result.name, 'John');
});
```

**Using `group` + `setUp`/`tearDown`**:

```dart
group('UserService', () {
  late UserService service;

  setUp(() => service = UserService());
  tearDown(() => service.dispose());

  test('...', () {});
});
```

Use **mocktail** (simpler than Mockito):

void main() {
