```dart
ElevatedButton(
  onPressed: () {
    NotificationService.showNotification(
     

### Question
How do I open a URL in Flutter?

### Answer
**To open a URL in Flutter, use the `url_launcher` package** (the official/recommended solution).

### 1. Add the dependency

```yaml
dependencies:
  url_launcher: ^6.3.0
```

Then run:

```bash
flutter pub get
```

### 2. Basic usage (Recommended)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:url_launcher/url_launcher.dart';

class OpenUrlExample extends StatelessWidget {
  const OpenUrlExample({super.key});

  Future<void> _launchUrl(String urlString) async {
    final Uri url = Uri.parse(urlString);

    if (!await launchUrl(url, mode: LaunchMode.externalApplication)) {
      throw Exception('Could not launch $url');
    }
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Open URL')),
      body: Center(
        child: ElevatedButton(
          onPressed: () => _launchUrl('https://flutter.dev'),
          child: const Text('Open Flutter Website'),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 3. Different Launch Modes

| Mode                              | Behavior                                      | Use Case                     |
|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|------------------------------|
| `LaunchMode.externalApplication`  | Opens in external browser/app                 | Most common                  |
| `LaunchMode.inAppWebView`         | Opens inside the app (WebView)                | When you want to stay in-app |
| `LaunchMode.platformDefault`      | Lets the platform decide                      | Default behavior             |

**Example with in-app WebView:**

```dart
await launchUrl(
  Uri.parse('https://flutter.dev'),
  mode: LaunchMode.inAppWebView,
  webViewConfiguration: const WebViewConfiguration(
    enableJavaScript: true,
    enableDomStorage: true,
  ),
);
```

### 4. Platform Configuration (Important)

**Android** (`android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml`):
Usually works out of the box for `https`.

**iOS** (`ios/Runner/Info.plist`):
Add this if you're launching external apps (optional for `https`):

```xml
<key>LSApplicationQueriesSchemes</key>
<array>
    <string>https</string>
    <string>http</string>
</array>
```

### Bonus: Safe helper function

```dart
Future<void> launchURLSafe(String urlString) async {
  final Uri url = Uri.parse(urlString);
  
  if (await canLaunchUrl(url)) {
    await launchUrl(url, mode: LaunchMode.externalApplication);
  } else {
    debugPrint('Cannot launch $url');
  }
}
```

**Recommendation**: Use `launchUrl()` with `LaunchMode.externalApplication` for most cases. Use `inAppWebView` only when you specifically need the user to stay inside your app.

### Question
How do I share content in Flutter?

### Answer
To share content in Flutter, use the **`share_plus`** package (the actively maintained replacement for the deprecated `share` package).

## Installation

```bash
flutter pub add share_plus
```

## Basic Usage

### Sharing Text

```dart
import 'package:share_plus/share_plus.dart';

// Simple text sharing
await Share.share('Check out this Flutter app!');

// With subject (useful for email clients)
await Share.share(
  'https://flutter.dev',
  subject: 'Check out Flutter',
);
```

### Sharing Files/Images

```dart
import 'package:share_plus/share_plus.dart';

// Share a single file
final result = await Share.shareXFiles([
  XFile('/path/to/image.png'),
], text: 'Check out this image!');

// Share multiple files
await Share.shareXFiles([
  XFile('/path/to/file1.pdf'),
  XFile('/path/to/file2.pdf'),
]);
```

### Handling Share Results

```dart
final result = await Share.share('Hello World');

if (result.status == ShareResultStatus.success) {
  print('Thank you for sharing!');
}
```

## Important: iPad Positioning

On iPad, you **must** specify the share sheet origin to avoid crashes:

```dart
final box = context.findRenderObject() as RenderBox?;

await Share.share(
  'Hello',
  sharePositionOrigin: box!.localToGlobal(Offset.zero) & box.size,
);
```

## Platform Notes

- **Android**: Works out of the box. For Android 11+, add `queries` to `AndroidManifest.xml` if targeting specific apps.
- **iOS**: Add `NSPhotoLibraryUsageDescription` to `Info.plist` if sharing photos.
- **Web**: Limited to the Web Share API (text + URLs only); file sharing is not supported.

## Complete Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:share_plus/share_plus.dart';

class ShareButton extends StatelessWidget {
  const ShareButton({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return ElevatedButton.icon(
      icon: const Icon(Icons.share),
      label: const Text('Share'),
      onPressed: () async {
        final box = context.findRenderObject() as RenderBox?;
        
        await Share.share(
          'Check out my app: https://example.com',
          subject: 'My App',
          sharePositionOrigin: box!.localToGlobal(Offset.zero) & box.size,
        );
      },
    );
  }
}
```

### Question
What is Flutter flavors?

### Answer
Flutter **flavors** (also called build variants) allow you to create different versions of your app from the same codebase—typically for development, staging, and production environments. Each flavor can have its own:

- App name and icon
- Bundle ID / Application ID
- API endpoints and configuration
- Firebase projects
- Signing certificates

## Implementation

### 1. Android Setup (`android/app/build.gradle`)

```gradle
android {
    flavorDimensions "environment"
    
    productFlavors {
        development {
            dimension "environment"
            applicationIdSuffix ".dev"
            versionNameSuffix "-dev"
            resValue "string", "app_name", "MyApp Dev"
        }
        staging {
            dimension "environment"
            applicationIdSuffix ".staging"
            versionNameSuffix "-staging"
            resValue "string", "app_name", "MyApp Staging"
        }
        production {
            dimension "environment"
            resValue "string", "app_name", "MyApp"
        }
    }
}
```

### 2. iOS Setup (Xcode)

Create schemes in Xcode:
1. **Product → Scheme → New Scheme**
2. Duplicate your target for each environment
3. Configure **Bundle Identifier**, **Display Name**, and **Info.plist** values per scheme

Alternatively, use **Xcode Configuration Files** (`.xcconfig`) for cleaner management.

### 3. Dart Configuration

Use `--dart-define` alongside flavors to pass environment variables to Dart code:

```dart
// lib/config/app_config.dart
class AppConfig {
  static const String environment = String.fromEnvironment(
    'ENVIRONMENT',
    defaultValue: 'development',
  );
  
  static const String apiBaseUrl = String.fromEnvironment(
    'API_URL',
    defaultValue: 'https://api.dev.example.com',
  );
  
  static bool get isProduction => environment == 'production';
}
```

### 4. Running with Flavors

```bash
# Android
flutter run --flavor development --dart-define=ENVIRONMENT=development --dart-define=API_URL=https://dev.api.com

# iOS
flutter run --flavor development --dart-define=ENVIRONMENT=development
```

For production builds:
```bash
flutter build apk --flavor production --dart-define=ENVIRONMENT=production --release
```

## Alternative: Pure Dart Approach

If you don't need native differences (different icons/bundle IDs), you can skip native flavors and use only `--dart-define`:

```bash
flutter run --dart-define=FLAVOR=prod --dart-define=API_KEY=xyz123
```

Then access it via `String.fromEnvironment('FLAVOR')`.

**Recommendation:** Use native flavors when you need different app icons, names, or signing configs; use `--dart-define` for runtime configuration toggles.

### Question
How do I build a release APK in Flutter?

### Answer
**To build a release APK in Flutter**, use the following command:

```bash
flutter build apk --release
```

This generates a **signed release APK** (if signing is configured) at:

```
build/app/outputs/flutter-apk/app-release.apk
```

---

### 1. Basic Release Build (Unsigned)

If you just want a release APK quickly:

```bash
flutter clean
flutter build apk --release
```

> **Note**: This produces a single `app-release.apk` that works on all architectures but is larger.

---

### 2. Recommended: Build a Signed Release APK

For production (especially Google Play), you should sign the APK.

#### Step A: Generate a Keystore

```bash
keytool -genkey -v -keystore ~/upload-keystore.jks -keyalg RSA -keysize 2048 -validity 10000 -alias upload
```

#### Step B: Configure Signing in `android/app/build.gradle`

Add the following at the top of the file (outside the `android` block):

```gradle
def keystoreProperties = new Properties()
def keystorePropertiesFile = rootProject.file('key.properties')
if (keystorePropertiesFile.exists()) {
    keystoreProperties.load(new FileInputStream(keystorePropertiesFile))
}
```

Then inside the `android` block, configure the `signingConfigs`:

```gradle
android {
    ...
    signingConfigs {
        release {
            keyAlias keystoreProperties['keyAlias']
            keyPassword keystoreProperties['keyPassword']
            storeFile keystoreProperties['storeFile'] ? file(keystoreProperties['storeFile']) : null
            storePassword keystoreProperties['storePassword']
        }
    }

    buildTypes {
        release {
            signingConfig signingConfigs.release
            minifyEnabled true
            shrinkResources true
            proguardFiles getDefaultProguardFile('proguard-android-optimize.txt'), 'proguard-rules.pro'
        }
    }
}
```

#### Step C: Create `android/key.properties`

Create a file at `android/key.properties`:

```properties
storePassword=your_store_password
keyPassword=your_key_password
keyAlias=upload
storeFile=/Users/yourname/upload-keystore.jks
```

> **Important**: Add `key.properties` and `*.jks` to `.gitignore`.

---

### 3. Build the Signed Release APK

```bash
flutter clean
flutter build apk --release
```

You can also build a split APK per architecture (smaller size):

```bash
flutter build apk --split-per-abi --release
```

This creates multiple APKs:
- `app-arm64-v8a-release.apk`
- `app-armeabi-v7a-release.apk`
- `app-x86_64-release.apk`

---

### Quick Commands Summary

| Command | Description |
|-------|-------------|
| `flutter build apk --release` | Standard release APK |
| `flutter build apk

### Question
How do I sign an Android app in Flutter?

### Answer
To sign an Android app in Flutter, you need to create a keystore, configure Gradle signing, and build a signed release artifact (preferably an **App Bundle** for the Play Store).

### 1. Generate a Keystore

Run this command to create a keystore file:

```bash
keytool -genkey -v -keystore ~/upload-keystore.jks -keyalg RSA \
  -keysize 2048 -validity 10000 -alias upload
```

Store the keystore in a secure location (e.g., `android/app/upload-keystore.jks` or outside the project).

### 2. Create `key.properties`

Create the file `android/key.properties`:

```properties
storePassword=your_store_password
keyPassword=your_key_password
keyAlias=upload
storeFile=../upload-keystore.jks   # or absolute path
```

> **Important**: Add `key.properties` and the `.jks` file to `.gitignore`.

### 3. Configure `build.gradle`

Update `android/app/build.gradle`:

```groovy
def keystoreProperties = new Properties()
def keystorePropertiesFile = rootProject.file('key.properties')
if (keystorePropertiesFile.exists()) {
    keystoreProperties.load(new FileInputStream(keystorePropertiesFile))
}

android {
    // ... other config

    signingConfigs {
        release {
            keyAlias keystoreProperties['keyAlias']
            keyPassword keystoreProperties['keyPassword']
            storeFile keystoreProperties['storeFile'] ? file(keystoreProperties['storeFile']) : null
            storePassword keystoreProperties['storePassword']
        }
    }

    buildTypes {
        release {
            signingConfig signingConfigs.release
            minifyEnabled true
            shrinkResources true
            proguardFiles getDefaultProguardFile('proguard-android.txt'), 'proguard-rules.pro'
        }
    }
}
```

### 4. Build the Signed App

**App Bundle (recommended):**
```bash
flutter build appbundle --release
```
Output: `build/app/outputs/bundle/release/app-release.aab`

**APK:**
```bash
flutter build apk --release
```
Output: `build/app/outputs/flutter-apk/app-release.apk`

### Google Play App Signing (Recommended)

When uploading to the Play Store for the first time, enroll in **Google Play App Signing**. You upload the keystore you just created as your **upload key**. Google then manages the actual signing key.

### Summary

| Step | Command / File | Purpose |
|------|----------------|---------|
| 1 | `keytool` | Generate keystore |
| 2 | `android/key.properties` | Store credentials |
| 3 | `android/app/build.gradle` | Wire up signing config |
| 4 | `flutter build appbundle` | Produce signed release |

This setup ensures your app is properly signed for distribution.

### Question
How do I configure app permissions in Flutter?

### Answer
To configure app permissions in Flutter, you need to handle **platform-specific declarations** (AndroidManifest.xml / Info.plist) and use a package like `permission_handler` to request permissions at runtime.

### 1. Add the Package

```yaml
dependencies:
  permission_handler: ^11.3.1
```

Then run:

```bash
flutter pub get
```

### 2. Platform Configuration

#### Android (`android/app/src/main/AndroidManifest.xml`)

Add the permissions you need **outside** the `<application>` tag:

```xml
<manifest xmlns:android="http://schemas.android.com/apk/res/android">
    <!-- Example permissions -->
    <uses-permission android:name="android.permission.CAMERA" />
    <uses-permission android:name="android.permission.ACCESS_FINE_LOCATION" />
    <uses-permission android:name="android.permission.READ_EXTERNAL_STORAGE" />
    <uses-permission android:name="android.permission.WRITE_EXTERNAL_STORAGE" />

    <application ...>
        ...
    </application>
</manifest>
```

> **Note**: On Android 13+, use `READ_MEDIA_IMAGES`, `READ_MEDIA_VIDEO`, etc. instead of `READ_EXTERNAL_STORAGE`.

#### iOS (`ios/Runner/Info.plist`)

Add usage descriptions:

```xml
<key>NSCameraUsageDescription</key>
<string>This app needs camera access to take photos</string>
<key>NSLocationWhenInUseUsageDescription</key>
<string>This app needs location access to show nearby places</string>
<key>NSPhotoLibraryUsageDescription</key>
<string>This app needs photo library access to select images</string>
```

### 3. Request Permissions in Code

```dart
import 'package:permission_handler/permission_handler.dart';

Future<void> requestPermissions() async {
  // Request multiple permissions at once
  Map<Permission, PermissionStatus> statuses = await [
    Permission.camera,
    Permission.location,
    Permission.storage, // or Permission.photos on iOS
  ].request();

  // Check individual results
  if (statuses[Permission.camera]!.isGranted) {
    print('Camera permission granted');
  } else if (statuses[Permission.camera]!.isDenied) {
    print('Camera permission denied');
  } else if (statuses[Permission.camera]!.isPermanentlyDenied) {
    // User selected "Don't ask again"
    await openAppSettings();
  }
}
```

### 4. Best Practice: Check Before Requesting

```dart
Future<bool> ensurePermission(Permission permission) async {
  final status = await permission.status;
  
  if (status.isGranted) return true;
  if (status.isPermanentlyDenied) {
    await openAppSettings();
    return false;
  }
  
  final result = await permission.request();
  return result.isGranted;
}

// Usage
final hasCamera = await ensurePermission(Permission.camera);
```

### Common Permission Types

| Permission              | Android                  | iOS                          |

### Question
How do I use environment variables in Flutter?

### Answer
Flutter doesn't have built-in environment variables like backend frameworks, but you can achieve this using `--dart-define` (recommended) or the `flutter_dotenv` package.

## Method 1: `--dart-define` (Recommended)

Use this for API keys and secrets. Variables are compiled into the app at build time.

**Define variables at build/run time:**
```bash
flutter run --dart-define=API_URL=https://api.example.com --dart-define=API_KEY=abc123
```

**Access them in Dart:**
```dart
const String apiUrl = String.fromEnvironment('API_URL', defaultValue: 'http://localhost:3000');
const String apiKey = String.fromEnvironment('API_KEY');

void main() {
  print('API URL: $apiUrl');
  runApp(MyApp());
}
```

**For production builds:**
```bash
flutter build apk --dart-define=API_URL=https://prod.example.com --dart-define=API_KEY=$API_KEY
```

## Method 2: Using `flutter_dotenv`

Better for local development with many variables.

**Install:**
```yaml
dependencies:
  flutter_dotenv: ^5.1.0
```

**Create `.env` file:**
```
API_URL=https://api.example.com
API_KEY=your-secret-key
```

**Load in `main.dart`:**
```dart
import 'package:flutter_dotenv/flutter_dotenv.dart';

Future<void> main() async {
  await dotenv.load(fileName: ".env");
  runApp(MyApp());
}

// Access variables
String apiUrl = dotenv.env['API_URL'] ?? 'http://localhost';
```

**Important:** Add `.env` to `.gitignore` to avoid committing secrets.

## Best Practice: Combine Both

```dart
import 'package:flutter_dotenv/flutter_dotenv.dart';

const String apiUrl = String.fromEnvironment('API_URL');

String getApiUrl() {
  // dart-define takes precedence, falls back to .env
  if (apiUrl.isNotEmpty) return apiUrl;
  return dotenv.env['API_URL'] ?? 'http://localhost:3000';
}
```

**Recommendation:** Use `--dart-define` for CI/CD and production. Use `flutter_dotenv` only for local development convenience.

### Question
What is the difference between debug and release mode in Flutter?

### Answer
**Debug vs Release Mode in Flutter**

Flutter has three main build modes: **Debug**, **Profile**, and **Release**. Here's a clear comparison between **Debug** and **Release**:

### Key Differences

| Aspect                    | Debug Mode                                      | Release Mode                                      |
|--------------------------|--------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| **Compilation**          | JIT (Just-In-Time)                              | AOT (Ahead-Of-Time)                               |
| **Performance**          | Slower                                          | Much faster (optimized)                           |
| **Hot Reload**           | Supported                                       | Not supported                                     |
| **Assertions**           | Enabled (`assert()` runs)                       | Disabled (`assert()` is ignored)                  |
| **Debug Flags**          | `kDebugMode = true`                             | `kReleaseMode = true`                             |
| **Logging**              | `print()`, `debugPrint()` work                  | Stripped out (unless using logging packages)      |
| **App Size**             | Larger                                          | Much smaller (tree shaking + minification)        |
| **Error Messages**       | Detailed                                        | Minimal                                           |
| **DevTools**             | Full support                                    | Limited                                           |
| **Obfuscation**          | Not available                                   | Available (`--obfuscate`)                         |
| **Use Case**             | Development & debugging                         | Production / App Store                            |

### How to Run Each Mode

```bash
# Debug (default)
flutter run

# Release
flutter run --release

# Profile (for performance testing)
flutter run --profile
```

### Building for Production

```bash
# Android
flutter build apk --release
flutter build appbundle --release

# iOS
flutter build ios --release

# Web
flutter build web --release
```

### Useful Code Examples

#### 1. Conditional Logic Based on Build Mode

```dart
import 'package:flutter/foundation.dart';

if (kDebugMode) {
  print('Only runs in debug mode');
}

if (kReleaseMode) {
  // Production-only logic
  analytics.setAnalyticsCollectionEnabled(true);
}
```

#### 2. Using Assertions (Only in Debug)

```dart
void calculateTotal(List<int> items) {
  assert(items.isNotEmpty, 'Items cannot be empty'); // Removed in release
  // ...
}
```

#### 3. Removing Debug Code in Release

```dart
void logEvent(String message) {
  if (kDebugMode) {
    print('[LOG] $message');
  }
}
```

### Summary

- Use **Debug** mode during development (fast iteration with hot reload).
- Use **Release** mode for production builds (optimized performance and smaller size).
- Always test critical flows in **Profile** or **Release** mode, as some bugs (especially performance-related) only appear outside debug mode.

Would you like to see how to detect the current build mode in a more advanced way (e.g.,

### Question
What is hot reload in Flutter?

### Answer
Hot Reload in Flutter is a development feature that allows you to **inject updated code into a running Dart VM without losing the current application state**. This enables near-instantaneous UI updates while preserving variables, navigation stacks, and widget states.

## How It Works

When you trigger a hot reload:
1. Flutter performs an **incremental compilation** of only the changed Dart files
2. The updated code is sent to the running Dart VM
3. The widget tree is rebuilt with the new code, but **existing state objects are preserved**
4. The UI reflects changes without restarting the app

## Triggering Hot Reload

- **Terminal**: Press `r` in the terminal running `flutter run`
- **VS Code**: Press `Ctrl+Shift+F5` (Windows/Linux) or `⇧⌘F5` (Mac), or use the "Hot Reload" button
- **Android Studio/IntelliJ**: Click the lightning bolt icon or press `Ctrl+\` (Windows/Linux) or `⌃⇧⌘F5` (Mac)

## What Works

```dart
// Modifying build() methods, adding widgets, changing colors
class MyWidget extends StatelessWidget {
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Container(
      color: Colors.blue, // Change this → hot reload updates instantly
      child: Text('Hello'),
    );
  }
}

// Adding new StatefulWidget logic (state values persist)
class Counter extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  _CounterState createState() => _CounterState();
}

class _CounterState extends State<Counter> {
  int count = 0; // This value survives hot reload
  
  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return ElevatedButton(
      onPressed: () => setState(() => count++),
      child: Text('$count'),
    );
  }
}
```

## Limitations

Hot reload **cannot** handle:

| Change Type | Reason |
|-------------|--------|
| **Static variables** | Re-initialized on reload |
| **`main()` function** | Requires full app restart |
| **`initState()` / `didUpdateWidget()`** | Lifecycle methods aren't re-run |
| **Enum definitions** | Type system changes |
| **Generic type signatures** | Compilation boundary changes |
| **Changes to `const` constructors** | Constant pool invalidation |

## Hot Reload vs Hot Restart

| Feature | Hot Reload (`r`) | Hot Restart (`R`) |
|---------|------------------|-------------------|
| **Speed** | ~1 second | ~2-3 seconds |
| **State preserved** | Yes | No |
| **Widget tree rebuilt** | Yes | Yes |
| **Changes to `main()`** | No | Yes |
| **Use case** | UI tweaks, logic fixes | Resetting app state, testing initialization |

**Rule of thumb**: Use hot reload for UI/logic changes; use hot restart when you

### Question
What is the difference between hot reload and hot restart?

### Answer
**Hot Reload** and **Hot Restart** are both fast ways to update your Flutter app during development, but they differ in how they handle **state** and **speed**.

### Quick Comparison

| Feature              | Hot Reload                          | Hot Restart                          |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| **Preserves State**  | Yes                                 | No                                   |
| **Speed**            | Faster                              | Slightly slower                      |
| **Use Case**         | UI/logic changes                    | When state needs to reset            |
| **Command**          | `r`                                 | `R`                                  |
| **Loses State?**     | No                                  | Yes                                  |

### Hot Reload

Hot reload **injects updated code** into the running Dart VM **without restarting** the app. The current state of your widgets (variables, scroll positions, form inputs, etc.) is preserved.

**When to use:**
- Changing widget layouts, colors, text
- Modifying business logic that doesn't affect initialization
- Iterating on UI quickly

**Limitations:**
- Won't work if you add/remove variables, change `const` values, or modify app initialization logic
- Some structural changes require a restart

### Hot Restart

Hot restart **destroys the current widget tree** and rebuilds it from scratch, but it **doesn't recompile** the entire app (unlike a full restart). This resets all state to initial values.

**When to use:**
- When hot reload doesn't reflect your changes
- Testing initial app state or navigation flows
- After adding new dependencies or changing `main()`

### Example Scenario

```dart
class CounterPage extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  _CounterPageState createState() => _CounterPageState();
}

class _CounterPageState extends State<CounterPage> {
  int count = 0; // Initial state

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: Center(child: Text('$count')),
      floatingActionButton: FloatingActionButton(
        onPressed: () => setState(() => count++),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

- **Hot Reload**: Change `Text('$count')` to `Text('Count: $count')` → The counter keeps its current value (e.g., 5 stays 5).
- **Hot Restart**: The counter resets back to `0`.

### Pro Tip

Use **Hot Reload** (`r`) for most development. Switch to **Hot Restart** (`R`) when changes don't appear or when you need a clean state. For a full rebuild (including native code changes), use a **Full Restart** (`Ctrl + C` then `flutter run`).

### Question
How do I implement dark mode in Flutter?

### Answer
**Implementing Dark Mode in Flutter** (Best Practice 2024)

Flutter has excellent built-in support for dark mode. Here's the cleanest and most maintainable way to implement it.

### 1. Basic Setup (Recommended)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      title: 'Dark Mode Demo',
      themeMode: ThemeMode.system, // Follows device setting
      theme: _lightTheme,
      darkTheme: _darkTheme,
      home: const HomePage(),
    );
  }
}

// Light Theme
final ThemeData _lightTheme = ThemeData(
  useMaterial3: true,
  colorScheme: ColorScheme.fromSeed(
    seedColor: Colors.blue,
    brightness: Brightness.light,
  ),
);

// Dark Theme
final ThemeData _darkTheme = ThemeData(
  useMaterial3: true,
  colorScheme: ColorScheme.fromSeed(
    seedColor: Colors.blue,
    brightness: Brightness.dark,
  ),
);
```

### 2. Dynamic Theme Switching (with State Management)

For a toggle button, use a simple `ValueNotifier` or Riverpod/Bloc. Here's a clean example using `ValueNotifier`:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

final ValueNotifier<ThemeMode> themeNotifier = ValueNotifier(ThemeMode.system);

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return ValueListenableBuilder<ThemeMode>(
      valueListenable: themeNotifier,
      builder: (_, ThemeMode currentMode, __) {
        return MaterialApp(
          themeMode: currentMode,
          theme: _lightTheme,
          darkTheme: _darkTheme,
          home: const HomePage(),
        );
      },
    );
  }
}
```

### 3. Toggle Button Example

```dart
class HomePage extends StatelessWidget {
  const HomePage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Dark Mode Demo')),
      body: Center(
        child: Column(
          mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
          children: [
            const Text('Current Theme'),
            const SizedBox(height: 20),
            ElevatedButton(
              onPressed: () {
                // Toggle between light, dark, and system
                if (themeNotifier.value == ThemeMode.light) {
                  themeNotifier.value = ThemeMode.dark;
                } else if (themeNotifier.value == ThemeMode.dark) {
                  themeNotifier.value = ThemeMode.system;
                } else {
                  themeNotifier.value = ThemeMode.light;
                }
              },
              child: const Text('Toggle Theme'),
            ),
          ],
       

### Question
How do I make a Flutter app responsive?

### Answer
To make a Flutter app responsive, you should combine **MediaQuery**, **LayoutBuilder**, and flexible layout widgets. Avoid hardcoding sizes—use relative sizing instead.

Here’s a practical approach with working examples:

### 1. Basic Responsive Layout (Recommended Pattern)

Use `LayoutBuilder` + breakpoints to adapt UI based on screen width:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class ResponsiveHome extends StatelessWidget {
  const ResponsiveHome({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: LayoutBuilder(
        builder: (context, constraints) {
          if (constraints.maxWidth < 600) {
            return _MobileLayout();      // Phone
          } else if (constraints.maxWidth < 1200) {
            return _TabletLayout();      // Tablet
          } else {
            return _DesktopLayout();     // Desktop/Web
          }
        },
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Responsive Grid / Columns

Use `GridView` with `crossAxisCount` that changes based on width:

```dart
Widget _buildResponsiveGrid(BuildContext context) {
  final width = MediaQuery.of(context).size.width;

  int columns = 2;
  if (width > 600) columns = 3;
  if (width > 900) columns = 4;
  if (width > 1200) columns = 6;

  return GridView.builder(
    gridDelegate: SliverGridDelegateWithFixedCrossAxisCount(
      crossAxisCount: columns,
      crossAxisSpacing: 12,
      mainAxisSpacing: 12,
      childAspectRatio: 1.2,
    ),
    itemCount: 20,
    itemBuilder: (context, index) => Card(
      child: Center(child: Text('Item $index')),
    ),
  );
}
```

### 3. Flexible vs Expanded

Use `Expanded` and `Flexible` to make widgets grow/shrink properly:

```dart
Row(
  children: [
    Expanded(flex: 2, child: Container(color: Colors.blue)), // 2/3 width
    Expanded(flex: 1, child: Container(color: Colors.red)),  // 1/3 width
  ],
)
```

### 4. Responsive Text & Spacing

Avoid fixed `fontSize`. Use `MediaQuery` or packages like `auto_size_text`:

```dart
// Manual scaling
Text(
  'Hello',
  style: TextStyle(
    fontSize: MediaQuery.of(context).size.width * 0.05, // 5% of screen width
  ),
)
```

Or use the `auto_size_text` package for automatic fitting.

### 5. Full Responsive Widget Example

```dart
class ResponsiveCard extends StatelessWidget {
  const ResponsiveCard({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final

### Question
How do I use MediaQuery for responsive design?

### Answer
**MediaQuery** is the foundation of responsive design in Flutter. It gives you real-time information about the device's screen size, orientation, padding (notch, status bar), and more.

### 1. Basic Usage

```dart
double screenWidth = MediaQuery.of(context).size.width;
double screenHeight = MediaQuery.of(context).size.height;
double devicePixelRatio = MediaQuery.of(context).devicePixelRatio;
Orientation orientation = MediaQuery.of(context).orientation;
```

### 2. Common Responsive Patterns

#### Responsive Font Size

```dart
double responsiveFont(double size) {
  return size * MediaQuery.of(context).size.width / 375; // 375 = base width (iPhone)
}
```

#### Responsive Padding/Margin

```dart
EdgeInsets responsivePadding(BuildContext context) {
  final width = MediaQuery.of(context).size.width;
  final horizontal = width * 0.05; // 5% of screen width
  return EdgeInsets.symmetric(horizontal: horizontal, vertical: 16);
}
```

### 3. Full Responsive Layout Example

Here's a practical example that changes layout based on screen width:

```dart
class ResponsivePage extends StatelessWidget {
  const ResponsivePage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final mediaQuery = MediaQuery.of(context);
    final width = mediaQuery.size.width;
    final isTablet = width > 600;
    final isDesktop = width > 900;

    return Scaffold(
      body: SafeArea(
        child: Padding(
          padding: EdgeInsets.all(width * 0.04),
          child: Column(
            children: [
              // Responsive Text
              Text(
                'Responsive Design',
                style: TextStyle(
                  fontSize: isDesktop ? 32 : isTablet ? 28 : 22,
                  fontWeight: FontWeight.bold,
                ),
              ),
              const SizedBox(height: 20),

              // Different layouts based on screen size
              if (isDesktop)
                _buildDesktopLayout()
              else if (isTablet)
                _buildTabletLayout()
              else
                _buildMobileLayout(),
            ],
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }

  Widget _buildDesktopLayout() {
    return Row(
      children: [
        Expanded(child: _buildCard()),
        const SizedBox(width: 20),
        Expanded(child: _buildCard()),
        const SizedBox(width: 20),
        Expanded(child: _buildCard()),
      ],
    );
  }

  Widget _buildTabletLayout() {
    return Row(
      children: [
        Expanded(child: _buildCard()),
        const SizedBox(width: 16),
        Expanded(child: _buildCard()),
      ],
    );
  }

  Widget _buildMobileLayout() {
    return Column(
      children: [
        _buildCard(),
        const SizedBox(height: 16),
        _buildCard(),
     

### Question
How do I create a Flutter package?

### Answer
To create a Flutter package, use the `--template=package` flag with the `flutter create` command:

```bash
flutter create --template=package my_package
```

This creates a **pure Dart package** (no platform-specific code). Use `--template=plugin` if you need native platform code (iOS/Android).

## Project Structure

```
my_package/
├── lib/
│   ├── my_package.dart      # Main export file
│   └── src/
│       └── my_package_base.dart
├── test/
├── pubspec.yaml
└── README.md
```

## Implementation Example

**`pubspec.yaml`**
```yaml
name: my_package
description: A simple utility package
version: 1.0.0
homepage: https://github.com/yourusername/my_package

environment:
  sdk: '>=3.0.0 <4.0.0'
  flutter: ">=3.10.0"

dependencies:
  flutter:
    sdk: flutter

dev_dependencies:
  flutter_test:
    sdk: flutter
  flutter_lints: ^3.0.0
```

**`lib/my_package.dart`** (main export file)
```dart
library my_package;

export 'src/calculator.dart';
export 'src/models/user.dart';
```

**`lib/src/calculator.dart`**
```dart
class Calculator {
  /// Returns [value] + 1.
  int addOne(int value) => value + 1;

  /// Returns the sum of [a] and [b].
  int add(int a, int b) => a + b;
}
```

## Using Your Package Locally

Add to your app's `pubspec.yaml`:

```yaml
dependencies:
  my_package:
    path: ../my_package
```

Then import and use:

```dart
import 'package:my_package/my_package.dart';

void main() {
  final calc = Calculator();
  print(calc.addOne(5)); // 6
}
```

## Publishing to pub.dev

```bash
# Check for issues
flutter pub publish --dry-run

# Publish
flutter pub publish
```

**Tip:** Keep your public API surface small by only exporting what's necessary from `lib/my_package.dart`, and keep implementation details in `lib/src/`.

### Question
How do I use pubspec.yaml in Flutter?

### Answer
**`pubspec.yaml`** is the central configuration file for every Flutter (and Dart) project. It defines metadata, dependencies, assets, fonts, and build settings.

### 1. Basic Structure

```yaml
name: my_app
description: A new Flutter project.
publish_to: 'none'

version: 1.0.0+1

environment:
  sdk: '>=3.0.0 <4.0.0'

dependencies:
  flutter:
    sdk: flutter
  cupertino_icons: ^1.0.6
  http: ^1.2.0
  provider: ^6.1.1

dev_dependencies:
  flutter_test:
    sdk: flutter
  flutter_lints: ^3.0.0

flutter:
  uses-material-design: true

  assets:
    - assets/images/
    - assets/icons/

  fonts:
    - family: Roboto
      fonts:
        - asset: assets/fonts/Roboto-Regular.ttf
        - asset: assets/fonts/Roboto-Bold.ttf
          weight: 700
```

### 2. Common Ways to Use It

#### A. Adding Dependencies (Recommended Way)

Instead of editing manually, use the CLI:

```bash
# Add a package
flutter pub add http

# Add a dev dependency
flutter pub add --dev build_runner

# Add a specific version
flutter pub add provider:6.1.1

# Remove a package
flutter pub remove http
```

After running these commands, `pubspec.yaml` and `pubspec.lock` are automatically updated.

#### B. Adding Assets

```yaml
flutter:
  assets:
    - assets/images/           # All files in the folder
    - assets/images/logo.png   # Specific file
    - assets/data/config.json
```

Then use them in code:

```dart
Image.asset('assets/images/logo.png')
```

#### C. Adding Custom Fonts

```yaml
flutter:
  fonts:
    - family: Inter
      fonts:
        - asset: assets/fonts/Inter-Regular.ttf
        - asset: assets/fonts/Inter-Bold.ttf
          weight: 700
        - asset: assets/fonts/Inter-Italic.ttf
          style: italic
```

Usage:

```dart
Text(
  'Hello',
  style: TextStyle(fontFamily: 'Inter', fontWeight: FontWeight.bold),
)
```

### 3. After Editing `pubspec.yaml`

Always run:

```bash
flutter pub get
```

Or just click **"Pub Get"** in VS Code when the file is open.

### 4. Useful Advanced Sections

```yaml
flutter:
  generate: true                    # Enables code generation (e.g., l10n)

  assets:
    - path: assets/images/
      exclude:
        - "**/*.tmp"

  shaders:
    - shaders/my_shader.frag        # Custom shaders (Flutter 3.10+)


### Question
What is the difference between runApp and main in Flutter?

### Answer
In Flutter, **`main()`** and **`runApp()`** serve different purposes: `main()` is the Dart entry point, while `runApp()` is the Flutter framework function that starts your app.

## The Difference

| Aspect | `main()` | `runApp()` |
|--------|----------|------------|
| **Origin** | Dart language | Flutter framework |
| **Purpose** | Application entry point | Inflates and attaches the widget tree to the screen |
| **Required** | Yes (every Dart app) | Yes (every Flutter app) |
| **Signature** | `void main() {}` | `void runApp(Widget app)` |

## How They Work Together

```dart
void main() {
  // Optional: Initialize bindings or plugins before runApp
  WidgetsFlutterBinding.ensureInitialized();
  
  // Start the Flutter app
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(body: Center(child: Text('Hello'))),
    );
  }
}
```

## Key Points

1. **`main()`** is where Dart begins execution. It can contain setup logic (async initialization, dependency injection, configuring error handlers) before calling `runApp()`.

2. **`runApp()`** calls `attachRootWidget()` internally, which:
   - Creates an `RenderView` (the root of the render tree)
   - Schedules the first frame
   - Binds your widget tree to the screen

3. **Without `runApp()`**, your app won't render anything—even if `main()` executes successfully.

4. **Common pattern**: Use `main()` for async setup that must complete before the app starts:

```dart
void main() async {
  WidgetsFlutterBinding.ensureInitialized();
  await Firebase.initializeApp();
  await SharedPreferences.getInstance();
  runApp(const MyApp());
}
```

### Question
How do I use ThemeData in Flutter?

### Answer
**ThemeData** is Flutter’s way of defining your app’s visual style (colors, typography, shapes, etc.). It’s typically passed to `MaterialApp` and accessed anywhere via `Theme.of(context)`.

### 1. Basic Usage (Recommended Modern Approach)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      title: 'ThemeData Example',
      theme: _buildTheme(),           // Light theme
      darkTheme: _buildDarkTheme(),   // Dark theme
      themeMode: ThemeMode.system,    // Follows system setting
      home: const HomePage(),
    );
  }

  ThemeData _buildTheme() {
    return ThemeData(
      colorScheme: ColorScheme.fromSeed(
        seedColor: Colors.deepPurple,
        brightness: Brightness.light,
      ),
      useMaterial3: true,
      appBarTheme: const AppBarTheme(
        centerTitle: true,
        elevation: 0,
      ),
      elevatedButtonTheme: ElevatedButtonThemeData(
        style: ElevatedButton.styleFrom(
          padding: const EdgeInsets.symmetric(horizontal: 24, vertical: 12),
          shape: RoundedRectangleBorder(
            borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(12),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }

  ThemeData _buildDarkTheme() {
    return ThemeData(
      colorScheme: ColorScheme.fromSeed(
        seedColor: Colors.deepPurple,
        brightness: Brightness.dark,
      ),
      useMaterial3: true,
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Accessing ThemeData in Widgets

```dart
class HomePage extends StatelessWidget {
  const HomePage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final theme = Theme.of(context);
    final colorScheme = theme.colorScheme;

    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(
        title: const Text('Theme Example'),
        backgroundColor: colorScheme.primaryContainer,
      ),
      body: Center(
        child: Column(
          mainAxisAlignment: MainAxisAlignment.center,
          children: [
            Text(
              'Hello Theme',
              style: theme.textTheme.headlineMedium?.copyWith(
                color: colorScheme.primary,
              ),
            ),
            const SizedBox(height: 20),
            ElevatedButton(
              onPressed: () {},
              child: const Text('Themed Button'),
            ),
          ],
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 3. Common Useful Properties

| Property                  | Purpose                              | Example |
|--------------------------|--------------------------------------|--------|
| `colorScheme`            | Modern color system (recommended)    | `ColorScheme.fromSeed(...)` |
| `textTheme`              | Typography                           | `TextTheme(...)

### Question
How do I customize fonts in Flutter?

### Answer
To customize fonts in Flutter, you have two main approaches:

1. **Add custom font files** (best for branded/offline fonts)
2. **Use the `google_fonts` package** (easiest for Google Fonts)

---

### 1. Using Custom Font Files

#### Step 1: Add font files to your project

Create a folder (e.g., `assets/fonts/`) and add your `.ttf` or `.otf` files:

```
assets/
  fonts/
    Roboto-Regular.ttf
    Roboto-Bold.ttf
    Roboto-Italic.ttf
```

#### Step 2: Declare fonts in `pubspec.yaml`

```yaml
flutter:
  fonts:
    - family: Roboto
      fonts:
        - asset: assets/fonts/Roboto-Regular.ttf
        - asset: assets/fonts/Roboto-Bold.ttf
          weight: 700
        - asset: assets/fonts/Roboto-Italic.ttf
          style: italic
```

> **Note**: Run `flutter pub get` after editing `pubspec.yaml`.

#### Step 3: Use the font

```dart
Text(
  'Custom Font Example',
  style: const TextStyle(
    fontFamily: 'Roboto',
    fontSize: 24,
    fontWeight: FontWeight.bold,
  ),
)
```

#### Set as default font in your app

```dart
MaterialApp(
  theme: ThemeData(
    fontFamily: 'Roboto',
  ),
  home: HomeScreen(),
)
```

---

### 2. Using Google Fonts (Recommended)

Add the package:

```yaml
dependencies:
  google_fonts: ^6.2.1
```

#### Usage

```dart
import 'package:google_fonts/google_fonts.dart';

Text(
  'Hello from Google Fonts',
  style: GoogleFonts.poppins(
    fontSize: 28,
    fontWeight: FontWeight.w600,
    color: Colors.blue,
  ),
)
```

#### Set as default theme font

```dart
MaterialApp(
  theme: ThemeData(
    textTheme: GoogleFonts.poppinsTextTheme(),
  ),
  home: HomeScreen(),
)
```

---

### Bonus: Font Weights & Styles

| Weight       | Value | Usage                     |
|--------------|-------|---------------------------|
| `w100`       | 100   | Thin                      |
| `w400`       | 400   | Regular (default)         |
| `w500`       | 500   | Medium                    |
| `w700`       | 700   | Bold                      |
| `w900`       | 900   | Black                     |

You can also use `fontStyle: FontStyle.italic`.

---

### Summary

| Method           | Best For                    | Requires Download | Offline Support |
|------------------|-----------------------------|-------------------|-----------------|
| Custom files     | Branding, control           | Yes               | Yes             |
| `google_fonts`   | Quick prototyping          

### Question
How do I add assets to Flutter app?

### Answer
To add assets (images, fonts, JSON files, etc.) to a Flutter app, follow these steps:

## 1. Create an assets folder

Create a folder in your project root (commonly named `assets`):

```
my_app/
├── assets/
│   ├── images/
│   │   └── logo.png
│   └── data/
│       └── config.json
├── lib/
└── pubspec.yaml
```

## 2. Declare assets in `pubspec.yaml`

```yaml
flutter:
  assets:
    - assets/images/           # Include all files in this directory
    - assets/data/config.json  # Include a specific file
```

**Important:** Use 2 spaces for indentation (not tabs), and ensure the `assets:` section is under `flutter:`.

## 3. Access assets in code

### Images

```dart
Image.asset('assets/images/logo.png')

// Or with AssetImage
Container(
  decoration: BoxDecoration(
    image: DecorationImage(
      image: AssetImage('assets/images/logo.png'),
    ),
  ),
)
```

### Other files (JSON, text, etc.)

```dart
import 'package:flutter/services.dart' show rootBundle;

Future<String> loadAsset() async {
  return await rootBundle.loadString('assets/data/config.json');
}
```

## 4. Add custom fonts

Place font files in `assets/fonts/`, then declare them:

```yaml
flutter:
  fonts:
    - family: RobotoMono
      fonts:
        - asset: assets/fonts/RobotoMono-Regular.ttf
        - asset: assets/fonts/RobotoMono-Bold.ttf
          weight: 700
```

Use in code:
```dart
Text(
  'Hello',
  style: TextStyle(fontFamily: 'RobotoMono'),
)
```

## Key Notes

- **Hot reload vs. restart:** Adding new assets requires a **full restart** (`flutter run` again), not just hot reload.
- **Path must match exactly:** Asset paths are case-sensitive and must match `pubspec.yaml` exactly.
- **Subdirectories:** If you list a directory (e.g., `assets/images/`), all files inside are included recursively.

### Question
How do I use localization in Flutter?

### Answer
**Flutter Localization (Official Way)**

Flutter has built-in support for localization using `flutter_localizations` + `.arb` files. Here's the modern, recommended setup:

### 1. Add Dependencies

Update your `pubspec.yaml`:

```yaml
dependencies:
  flutter:
    sdk: flutter
  flutter_localizations:
    sdk: flutter
  intl: ^0.18.1

flutter:
  generate: true   # Required for code generation
```

### 2. Create Localization Configuration

Create a file called `l10n.yaml` in the root of your project:

```yaml
arb-dir: lib/l10n
template-arb-file: app_en.arb
output-localization-file: app_localizations.dart
```

### 3. Create ARB Files

Create the folder `lib/l10n/` and add your translation files:

**`lib/l10n/app_en.arb`** (English - template)
```json
{
  "helloWorld": "Hello World!",
  "@helloWorld": {
    "description": "A simple greeting"
  },
  "welcome": "Welcome, {name}!",
  "@welcome": {
    "placeholders": {
      "name": {
        "type": "String"
      }
    }
  }
}
```

**`lib/l10n/app_es.arb`** (Spanish)
```json
{
  "helloWorld": "¡Hola Mundo!",
  "welcome": "¡Bienvenido, {name}!"
}
```

### 4. Configure `MaterialApp`

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';
import 'package:flutter_gen/gen_l10n/app_localizations.dart';

void main() {
  runApp(const MyApp());
}

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      title: 'Localization Demo',
      localizationsDelegates: AppLocalizations.localizationsDelegates,
      supportedLocales: AppLocalizations.supportedLocales,
      locale: const Locale('en'), // or remove to use device locale
      home: const HomePage(),
    );
  }
}
```

### 5. Use Localization in Widgets

```dart
class HomePage extends StatelessWidget {
  const HomePage({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    final l10n = AppLocalizations.of(context)!;

    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: Text(l10n.helloWorld)),
      body: Center(
        child: Text(l10n.welcome('John')),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 6. Generate Localization Files

Run this command after adding new strings:

```bash
flutter gen-l10n
```

Or just run `flutter run` — it will generate automatically.

### Bonus: Access Localization Outside Widgets

```dart
final l10n = AppLocalizations.of

### Question
What is the difference between Stateful and Stateless lifecycle?

### Answer
**StatelessWidget vs StatefulWidget Lifecycle in Flutter**

### 1. StatelessWidget Lifecycle

A `StatelessWidget` is **immutable**. Once built, it cannot change internally.

**Lifecycle (very simple):**

```
Constructor → build()
```

- Only two main points:
  - **Constructor**: Widget is created.
  - **`build()`**: UI is rendered.
- When the widget is removed from the tree, it is disposed (no explicit `dispose()` method).
- It has **no state management lifecycle methods**.

**Example:**

```dart
class MyStatelessWidget extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyStatelessWidget({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const Text('Hello');
  }
}
```

---

### 2. StatefulWidget Lifecycle

A `StatefulWidget` is also immutable, but it creates a **mutable `State` object** that can change over time.

**Full Lifecycle Order:**

| Stage | Method | Called When | Can Call `setState()`? |
|-------|--------|-------------|------------------------|
| 1 | `createState()` | Widget is inserted into the tree | No |
| 2 | `initState()` | State object is created | No |
| 3 | `didChangeDependencies()` | Dependencies (e.g., `InheritedWidget`) change | Yes |
| 4 | `build()` | Widget is rendered | Yes |
| 5 | `didUpdateWidget()` | Widget is updated with new configuration | Yes |
| 6 | `deactivate()` | Widget is removed from tree temporarily | No |
| 7 | `dispose()` | Widget is permanently removed | No |

### Complete StatefulWidget Example

```dart
class MyStatefulWidget extends StatefulWidget {
  const MyStatefulWidget({super.key});

  @override
  State<MyStatefulWidget> createState() => _MyStatefulWidgetState();
}

class _MyStatefulWidgetState extends State<MyStatefulWidget> {
  
  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    // Called once when state is created
    print('initState');
  }

  @override
  void didChangeDependencies() {
    super.didChangeDependencies();
    print('didChangeDependencies');
  }

  @override
  void didUpdateWidget(covariant MyStatefulWidget oldWidget) {
    super.didUpdateWidget(oldWidget);
    print('didUpdateWidget');
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    print('build');
    return const Text('Hello');
  }

  @override
  void deactivate() {
    print('deactivate');
    super.deactivate();
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    print('dispose');
    super.dispose();
  }
}
```

### Summary Table

| Aspect                        | StatelessWidget              | StatefulWidget                          |
|------------------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|


### Question
How do I use LayoutBuilder in Flutter?

### Answer
**LayoutBuilder** is a Flutter widget that lets you build a responsive UI based on the **available space** (constraints) provided by the parent widget.

### Basic Usage

```dart
LayoutBuilder(
  builder: (BuildContext context, BoxConstraints constraints) {
    // Use constraints here
    return Container();
  },
)
```

### Common Example: Responsive Layout

Here's a practical example that switches between a **Row** and **Column** based on available width:

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class ResponsiveWidget extends StatelessWidget {
  const ResponsiveWidget({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      body: LayoutBuilder(
        builder: (context, constraints) {
          if (constraints.maxWidth > 600) {
            // Wide layout (Tablet/Desktop)
            return _buildWideLayout();
          } else {
            // Narrow layout (Mobile)
            return _buildNarrowLayout();
          }
        },
      ),
    );
  }

  Widget _buildWideLayout() {
    return Row(
      children: [
        Expanded(child: Container(color: Colors.blue, child: const Center(child: Text('Left')))),
        Expanded(child: Container(color: Colors.green, child: const Center(child: Text('Right')))),
      ],
    );
  }

  Widget _buildNarrowLayout() {
    return Column(
      children: [
        Expanded(child: Container(color: Colors.blue, child: const Center(child: Text('Top')))),
        Expanded(child: Container(color: Colors.green, child: const Center(child: Text('Bottom')))),
      ],
    );
  }
}
```

### Understanding `BoxConstraints`

The `constraints` parameter gives you access to:

| Property         | Description                          |
|------------------|--------------------------------------|
| `maxWidth`       | Maximum width available              |
| `maxHeight`      | Maximum height available             |
| `minWidth`       | Minimum width (usually 0)            |
| `minHeight`      | Minimum height (usually 0)           |

### Useful Patterns

**1. Percentage-based sizing**
```dart
LayoutBuilder(
  builder: (context, constraints) {
    return SizedBox(
      width: constraints.maxWidth * 0.8, // 80% of available width
      height: constraints.maxHeight * 0.5,
      child: YourWidget(),
    );
  },
)
```

**2. Inside `ListView` or scrollable widgets**
```dart
ListView.builder(
  itemCount: 10,
  itemBuilder: (context, index) {
    return LayoutBuilder(
      builder: (context, constraints) {
        return Text('Width: ${constraints.maxWidth}');
      },
    );
  },
)
```

### Important Notes

- `LayoutBuilder` rebuilds when the **parent constraints** change.
- It does **not** give you the full screen size — only the

### Question
How do I use CustomPainter in Flutter?

### Answer
**CustomPainter** lets you draw custom graphics directly on a `Canvas` in Flutter. It's commonly used for charts, signatures, custom shapes, loading indicators, etc.

### Basic Usage

You need two things:

1. **`CustomPaint`** widget (places your drawing in the widget tree)
2. A class that extends **`CustomPainter`**

### Complete Example: Drawing Shapes

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return MaterialApp(
      home: Scaffold(
        appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('CustomPainter Example')),
        body: Center(
          child: CustomPaint(
            size: const Size(300, 300),
            painter: MyPainter(),
          ),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}

class MyPainter extends CustomPainter {
  @override
  void paint(Canvas canvas, Size size) {
    final paint = Paint()
      ..color = Colors.blue
      ..strokeWidth = 4
      ..style = PaintingStyle.stroke;

    // Draw a circle
    canvas.drawCircle(
      Offset(size.width / 2, size.height / 2),
      80,
      paint,
    );

    // Draw a rectangle
    final rectPaint = Paint()
      ..color = Colors.red
      ..style = PaintingStyle.fill;

    canvas.drawRect(
      Rect.fromCenter(
        center: Offset(size.width / 2, size.height / 2),
        width: 100,
        height: 60,
      ),
      rectPaint,
    );

    // Draw a line
    final linePaint = Paint()
      ..color = Colors.green
      ..strokeWidth = 6;

    canvas.drawLine(
      Offset(30, 30),
      Offset(size.width - 30, size.height - 30),
      linePaint,
    );
  }

  @override
  bool shouldRepaint(covariant CustomPainter oldDelegate) => false;
}
```

### Key Points

| Method | Purpose | Recommendation |
|-------|--------|----------------|
| `paint(Canvas, Size)` | Where you do the actual drawing | Required |
| `shouldRepaint()` | Tells Flutter whether to redraw | Return `false` if static, `true` if data changes |

### Passing Data to CustomPainter

If your painter needs external data (recommended approach):

```dart
class MyPainter extends CustomPainter {
  final Color color;
  final double progress;

  MyPainter({required this.color, required this.progress});

  @override
  void paint(Canvas canvas, Size size) {
    // use color and progress here
  }

  @override
  bool shouldRepaint(MyPainter oldDelegate) =>
      oldDelegate.color != color || oldDelegate.progress != progress;
}
```

Usage:

```dart


### Question
What is RenderObject in Flutter?

### Answer
`RenderObject` is the core class in Flutter's **rendering layer** that handles layout, painting, and hit testing. It forms the render tree that actually draws pixels on the screen.

## The Three Trees

Flutter maintains three parallel trees:

| Layer | Tree | Purpose | Mutability |
|-------|------|---------|------------|
| **Widget** | Configuration | Describes what you want | Immutable |
| **Element** | Instantiation | Manages lifecycle & relationships | Mutable |
| **RenderObject** | Rendering | Computes layout & paints | Mutable |

When you write `Container(color: Colors.blue)`, the `Container` widget creates a `RenderDecoratedBox` (a `RenderObject`) that actually draws the blue background.

## Key Responsibilities

- **Layout**: Computing size and position via `performLayout()`
- **Painting**: Drawing via `paint()`
- **Hit Testing**: Detecting pointer events
- **Compositing**: Managing layers for effects like opacity or transforms

## Common Subclasses

- **`RenderBox`**: 2D box layout model (most common)
- **`RenderSliver`**: Viewport-aware layout for scrollables
- **`RenderView`**: Root of the render tree

## Practical Example: Custom RenderObject

Here's a custom `RenderBox` that draws a diagonal line:

```dart
class DiagonalLine extends LeafRenderObjectWidget {
  const DiagonalLine({super.key, required this.color});

  final Color color;

  @override
  RenderObject createRenderObject(BuildContext context) {
    return RenderDiagonalLine(color: color);
  }

  @override
  void updateRenderObject(
    BuildContext context,
    RenderDiagonalLine renderObject,
  ) {
    renderObject.color = color;
  }
}

class RenderDiagonalLine extends RenderBox {
  RenderDiagonalLine({required Color color}) : _color = color;

  Color _color;
  Color get color => _color;
  set color(Color value) {
    if (_color == value) return;
    _color = value;
    markNeedsPaint();
  }

  @override
  void performLayout() {
    // Take whatever size we're given
    size = constraints.biggest;
  }

  @override
  void paint(PaintingContext context, Offset offset) {
    final paint = Paint()
      ..color = _color
      ..strokeWidth = 2.0;
    
    context.canvas.drawLine(
      offset,
      offset + Offset(size.width, size.height),
      paint,
    );
  }

  @override
  bool hitTestSelf(Offset position) => true;
}
```

**Usage:**
```dart
SizedBox(
  width: 100,
  height: 100,
  child: DiagonalLine(color: Colors.red),
)
```

## When to Use RenderObjects

You typically work directly with `RenderObject`s when:

1. Building custom widgets with complex layout/painting logic


### Question
How do I implement search functionality in Flutter?

### Answer
**Best way to implement search in Flutter** depends on your use case. Below are the two most common and recommended approaches with **working code**.

### 1. Simple In-Memory Search (Most Common)

Use a `TextField` + `ListView` with filtering. Great for local data.

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class SearchListScreen extends StatefulWidget {
  const SearchListScreen({super.key});

  @override
  State<SearchListScreen> createState() => _SearchListScreenState();
}

class _SearchListScreenState extends State<SearchListScreen> {
  final List<String> _allItems = [
    'Apple', 'Banana', 'Cherry', 'Date', 'Elderberry',
    'Fig', 'Grape', 'Honeydew', 'Kiwi', 'Lemon'
  ];

  List<String> _filteredItems = [];
  final TextEditingController _searchController = TextEditingController();

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _filteredItems = _allItems;
    _searchController.addListener(_filterItems);
  }

  void _filterItems() {
    final query = _searchController.text.toLowerCase();
    setState(() {
      _filteredItems = _allItems
          .where((item) => item.toLowerCase().contains(query))
          .toList();
    });
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _searchController.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Search Example')),
      body: Column(
        children: [
          Padding(
            padding: const EdgeInsets.all(12),
            child: TextField(
              controller: _searchController,
              decoration: InputDecoration(
                hintText: 'Search fruits...',
                prefixIcon: const Icon(Icons.search),
                suffixIcon: _searchController.text.isNotEmpty
                    ? IconButton(
                        icon: const Icon(Icons.clear),
                        onPressed: () {
                          _searchController.clear();
                          _filterItems();
                        },
                      )
                    : null,
                border: OutlineInputBorder(
                  borderRadius: BorderRadius.circular(12),
                ),
              ),
            ),
          ),
          Expanded(
            child: ListView.builder(
              itemCount: _filteredItems.length,
              itemBuilder: (context, index) {
                return ListTile(
                  title: Text(_filteredItems[index]),
                  onTap: () {
                    // Handle item tap
                  },
                );
              },
            ),
          ),
        ],
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

### 2. Full-Screen Search (Recommended - `SearchDelegate`)

Use Flutter’s built-in `SearchDelegate` for a more native experience (with suggestions and results).

```dart
// In your screen
IconButton(
  icon: const Icon(Icons.search),
 

### Question
How do I implement pagination in Flutter?

### Answer
**Implementing Pagination in Flutter**

The most common and lightweight way to implement pagination is using `ListView.builder` + `ScrollController`. Here's a clean, production-ready example:

### Complete Pagination Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class PaginatedListScreen extends StatefulWidget {
  const PaginatedListScreen({super.key});

  @override
  State<PaginatedListScreen> createState() => _PaginatedListScreenState();
}

class _PaginatedListScreenState extends State<PaginatedListScreen> {
  final List<String> _items = [];
  final ScrollController _scrollController = ScrollController();
  
  int _page = 1;
  final int _limit = 20;
  bool _isLoading = false;
  bool _hasMore = true;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _fetchData();
    _scrollController.addListener(_onScroll);
  }

  void _onScroll() {
    if (_scrollController.position.pixels >=
            _scrollController.position.maxScrollExtent - 200 &&
        !_isLoading &&
        _hasMore) {
      _fetchData();
    }
  }

  Future<void> _fetchData() async {
    if (_isLoading) return;

    setState(() => _isLoading = true);

    // Simulate API call
    await Future.delayed(const Duration(milliseconds: 800));

    // Replace this with your actual API call
    final newItems = List.generate(
      _limit,
      (index) => 'Item ${(_page - 1) * _limit + index + 1}',
    );

    setState(() {
      _items.addAll(newItems);
      _page++;
      _isLoading = false;
      
      // Stop pagination after 5 pages (example)
      if (_page > 5) _hasMore = false;
    });
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Pagination Example')),
      body: ListView.builder(
        controller: _scrollController,
        itemCount: _items.length + (_hasMore ? 1 : 0),
        itemBuilder: (context, index) {
          if (index == _items.length) {
            return const Padding(
              padding: EdgeInsets.all(16),
              child: Center(child: CircularProgressIndicator()),
            );
          }
          return ListTile(
            title: Text(_items[index]),
            subtitle: Text('Index: $index'),
          );
        },
      ),
    );
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _scrollController.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }
}
```

### Key Points

- **Scroll detection**: Triggers when user is 200px from the bottom.
- **Loading state**: Prevents multiple simultaneous requests.
- **hasMore flag**: Stops pagination when no more

### Question
How do I use RefreshIndicator in Flutter?

### Answer
`RefreshIndicator` provides the "pull-to-refresh" pattern in Flutter. It requires a scrollable descendant (like `ListView`, `GridView`, or `CustomScrollView`) and an `onRefresh` callback that returns a `Future<void>`.

## Basic Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

class RefreshExample extends StatefulWidget {
  @override
  State<RefreshExample> createState() => _RefreshExampleState();
}

class _RefreshExampleState extends State<RefreshExample> {
  List<String> items = List.generate(20, (i) => 'Item ${i + 1}');

  Future<void> _refreshData() async {
    // Simulate network delay
    await Future.delayed(const Duration(seconds: 2));
    
    setState(() {
      items = List.generate(20, (i) => 'Refreshed Item ${i + 1}');
    });
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Pull to Refresh')),
      body: RefreshIndicator(
        onRefresh: _refreshData,
        child: ListView.builder(
          physics: const AlwaysScrollableScrollPhysics(),
          itemCount: items.length,
          itemBuilder: (context, index) => ListTile(title: Text(items[index])),
        ),
      ),
    );
  }
}
```

## Key Points

1. **`onRefresh`** must return a `Future`. The indicator stays visible until the future completes.
2. **AlwaysScrollableScrollPhysics** ensures the list is scrollable even when content doesn't fill the viewport (required for pull-to-refresh to work when the list is short).
3. The scrollable widget must have enough room to scroll or use `AlwaysScrollableScrollPhysics`.

## With CustomScrollView

```dart
RefreshIndicator(
  onRefresh: _refreshData,
  child: CustomScrollView(
    physics: const AlwaysScrollableScrollPhysics(),
    slivers: [
      SliverAppBar(title: Text('Sliver Refresh')),
      SliverList(
        delegate: SliverChildBuilderDelegate(
          (context, index) => ListTile(title: Text(items[index])),
          childCount: items.length,
        ),
      ),
    ],
  ),
)
```

## Customization

```dart
RefreshIndicator(
  onRefresh: _refreshData,
  color: Colors.white,
  backgroundColor: Colors.blue,
  displacement: 40,           // Distance from top
  edgeOffset: 20,             // Offset from the start of the list
  strokeWidth: 3,
  child: ListView(...),
)
```

**Important**: The `onRefresh` callback should handle errors internally; if it throws, the indicator may get stuck. Wrap your refresh logic in try-catch if needed.

### Question
How do I implement infinite scroll in Flutter?

### Answer
**Infinite Scroll in Flutter (Manual Implementation)**

Here's the cleanest and most commonly used approach using `ScrollController` + `ListView.builder`.

### Complete Working Example

```dart
import 'package:flutter/material.dart';

void main() => runApp(const MyApp());

class MyApp extends StatelessWidget {
  const MyApp({super.key});

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return const MaterialApp(home: InfiniteScrollScreen());
  }
}

class InfiniteScrollScreen extends StatefulWidget {
  const InfiniteScrollScreen({super.key});

  @override
  State<InfiniteScrollScreen> createState() => _InfiniteScrollScreenState();
}

class _InfiniteScrollScreenState extends State<InfiniteScrollScreen> {
  final ScrollController _scrollController = ScrollController();
  final List<int> _items = [];
  bool _isLoading = false;
  bool _hasMore = true;
  int _page = 1;

  @override
  void initState() {
    super.initState();
    _loadMore(); // Load initial data

    _scrollController.addListener(() {
      if (_scrollController.position.pixels >=
              _scrollController.position.maxScrollExtent - 200 &&
          !_isLoading &&
          _hasMore) {
        _loadMore();
      }
    });
  }

  Future<void> _loadMore() async {
    if (_isLoading) return;

    setState(() => _isLoading = true);

    // Simulate network delay
    await Future.delayed(const Duration(milliseconds: 800));

    // Simulate end of data after 5 pages
    if (_page > 5) {
      setState(() {
        _hasMore = false;
        _isLoading = false;
      });
      return;
    }

    // Add new items
    final newItems = List.generate(20, (index) => (_page - 1) * 20 + index + 1);
    
    setState(() {
      _items.addAll(newItems);
      _page++;
      _isLoading = false;
    });
  }

  @override
  void dispose() {
    _scrollController.dispose();
    super.dispose();
  }

  @override
  Widget build(BuildContext context) {
    return Scaffold(
      appBar: AppBar(title: const Text('Infinite Scroll')),
      body: ListView.builder(
        controller: _scrollController,
        itemCount: _items.length + (_hasMore ? 1 : 0),
        itemBuilder: (context, index) {
          if (index == _items.length) {
            return _buildLoader();
          }
          return ListTile(
            title: Text('Item ${_items[index]}'),
            subtitle: Text('This is item number ${_items[index]}'),
          );
        },
      ),
    );
  }

  Widget _buildLoader() {
    return Padding(
      padding: const EdgeInsets.all(16.
